I hadn’t seen her approach. She looked me up and down, and I did the same to her. She was in a snazzier black suit than usual, a button-down white shirt, and her usual sunglasses tucked into her dark hair. Her badge was clipped visibly to her belt.

“Mrs. Henderson,” she said, touching two fingers to her forehead in a casual salute. She knew Mrs. Henderson as her office secretary, which is how I first met the goblin.

“You can see me?” Mrs. Henderson’s color faded all the way to an artificially lye-cured green olive. Yes, I’m from Northern California. Most people know the wineries, but the olive groves are also—extensive.

“Um.” Scharf looked between the two of us, adding up two and two and getting four, but swallowed whatever comment she had. “Shall we?”

Scharf gestured to the front entrance where “J. Edgar Hoover FBI Building” was stamped in gold above a rotating door. A scraggly line of a half dozen people in the DC grey business casual uniform were now waiting to go through the rotating glass doors. Mrs. Henderson sidled up to me. “The Princess of Beasts cannot join the FBI.”

“The museum theft was done by a supernatural,” I said miserably.

“I know.”

“You know? Why didn’t you say? Who was it?”

“I don’t know who it was,” Mrs. Henderson said. “I know that it was. It’s my job to know in case anything needs covering up.”

“Do you know of a short, human-looking creature that can go invisible like us?”

“Yeah, we’re having tea at three,” she said impatiently.

“What?”

“Well, it’s like asking if I know any bipedal, brown and beige primates who can talk.”

I took a deep breath. “The perpetrators are short, pale, humanoid, probably with ears something like ours, and nobody normal noticed them.”

“Glamour and invisibility are skills that almost all of us have, so no, you’re not going to solve this on the sidewalk by talking to your secretary.”

I tried really hard not to smile. “My secretary?”

“Scharf’s secretary!”

“I have to help.”

“When will you get it through your head? We are a minor power! The beasts of the magical world. We play around with the most basic elements. We are nothing. Your father was crazy as hell, but even he understood that.”

“I don’t know, we’ve been doing pretty good so far, don’t you think? Maybe it’s time to retire the hierarchy.”

“It’s not labeling. It’s reality. We are weak. And it’s one thing to be one agent’s little pet goblin, but NTEPUEL is no joke. Real powers work on this. And it isn’t because of their sterling hearts. Mostly it’s ‘cause they got caught. And you’re volunteering.” She looked me up and down and shook her head. She continued, “You break the taboos of centuries, Princess.”

“Stop using that word! And I got caught too. Scharf caught me. And here I am, bargaining for all of us.”

“This is not the way to do it.”

“I am the p-word. And you’re not.” I couldn’t help adding the last bit.

She took a breath to argue some more when Scharf backtracked. The line had disappeared in front of us. “You guys okay? We’re already late, and we still have security.”

And with my arrest record, even with the Presidential pardon, getting into certain buildings when I was not invisible took some time. Mrs. Henderson scratched at her floppy ear in an inhuman gesture. She was genuinely upset because most of the time she was better at passing than I was. It probably looked to Scharf like she was batting the air beside her head.

Scharf narrowed her eyes but nodded and retreated toward the spinning glass. I followed before Mrs. Henderson voiced any more of the objections I had in my head.

I pushed the cold glass around in a circle and was greeted by the smell of cleaner and printer ink. A long counter stretched before us, and a guard stood behind it next to a metal detector station. When I hit the counter, a man looked up and jumped. “Didn’t see you there.”

I forced a smile.

“Can I see some ID?”

My smile died. “I’m with her,” I said, pointing at Scharf, already beyond the metal detectors.

“I know, but I still need to see ID. It’s standard procedure.” He sat up a little straighter and took a better look at me. The opposite of what I wanted to happen.

I dug out my wallet and my California driver's license. I winced as I looked at the picture. This was from before I knew I was half-goblin and how to shift my appearance.

Mrs. Henderson rolled her eyes and said, “Just do it to your ID too. It’s made of earth.”

I blinked and stared at my ID. I let my magic smooth it out and held it up. He took it from me, and I winced because the glamour would fade the moment it left my fingers, but he didn’t really look at the picture. He scribbled down an illegible name and asked for a fingerprint. I mushed that up too and was soon through security and back with Scharf.

“When did you and Mrs. Henderson become such good friends?” she asked as she led me deeper into the building, which, once we got past security, seemed to be a pretty normal office building.

“You know,” I said as we reached a huge bank of elevators.

“I don’t, that’s why I asked.” Another few feet and she added, “So is she. . .”

“Ask her,” I said and couldn’t keep the edge out of my voice. “Except don’t.”

“You realize you just confirmed it,” she said as one elevator opened, and we stepped inside.

I gave her my most wide-eyed “I’m normal” smile. “Confirmed what?”

Scharf nodded at Mrs. Henderson, who offered another terrifying smile, which Scharf obviously couldn’t see clearly because she faced the doors without drawing a weapon or screaming. I shook my head at the other goblin, and she bared her teeth at me. This was going to be a long morning.

As we left the elevator on a high floor, I trailed behind Scharf so I could ask Mrs. Henderson, “Is there anything else I need to be doing?”

She turned back, eyes narrowed. “With your life?”

I fought off a blush. “No, with…you. With anybody.”

“Goblins?”

I nodded.

“Nuclear weapons in the hands of children,” she muttered, and I thought she wasn’t going to answer me when she spun to me just before the door. “I wouldn’t worry too much. We’ll find you if we need you.”

Then she cackled. Full on evil, fairytale cackling. The hairs all over my body rose as she slipped through a door.

We were swept into a surprisingly normal conference room of chairs around a wide wooden table. The room was empty. Apparently, Scharf’s definition of late was not an hour early. An old-school projector hung from the ceiling shining the FBI logo on the screen on one wall. I don’t know what I expected—high-tech crime-fighting equipment on every wall?

I stared at the logo. It was one of those moments where life just hits you a little hard. Normally, you don’t notice all the choices that have gone into who you are, but sometimes all the accumulated twists and turns are illuminated at once. Last March, I was in the middle of a semester failing math for the second time but enjoying my anthropology courses and writing a paper on Christian versus Celtic myths in Ireland. I had one half of a bedroom in an apartment in Berkeley, and my mother remembered my name and everything else. And though I hadn’t seen him in years, I’d had absolute faith my father had landed on his feet wherever he’d disappeared to.

And now I was a goblin. In a meeting. In the Hoover Building. Solving crimes. What happened to my life?

“Everything okay?” Scharf asked, and I blinked. She and Mrs. Henderson had seated themselves in two of the swivel chairs at the end of the table. She was silhouetted against a bank of shaded windows.

The windows looked bigger from the inside than from the outside, showing a nice view of an old, fancy building across the street. I cleared my throat and hurried to sit between them.

The door opened and Detective Lee stumbled in, escorted by someone who backed out and closed the door on us. He was wearing the same blazer as he had at the crime scene, but his pants were dark blue instead of khaki. He looked a little stunned himself. “Why am I here?”

Scharf just smiled. “You brought the footage?”

He nodded and hurried to sit down beside Scharf. He really must have been stunned if he viewed us as a safe harbor. He looked more than stunned; he looked pale.

“Have you eaten?” I asked him.

He frowned at me.

“Never mind,” I said and winced.

When I was invisible once, I’d seen him giving himself an insulin injection. He was hiding it from his boss. I kept trying to help him and just freaked him out instead since he’d told nobody about it.

The next person to enter made Lee look like a fashion model. The man wore a suit jacket, but it looked like he’d been wearing it for at least a decade. Beneath it, he had a t-shirt from a band I didn’t recognize and a pair of beat-up jeans. He clutched a mountain of folders at least three inches high and sat down quickly across from Scharf without making eye contact with anyone. He mumbled to himself, sniffed, and opened a folder. I glanced at Scharf. She shrugged.

“Ladies and Gentlemen, and Agents, thank you for coming.”

Giudice stood in the door. Today’s suit was gray. I could see the sweat on his scalp in the sparse, dark hair of his buzzcut.

He sat at the other end of the table. Another dour agent followed him in. This one looked like a Viking if Vikings wore buzzcuts. He’d matched his boss down to the gray suit and even mimicked Giudice’s body language as he folded his hands on the table in front of him. Everything he did screamed protege.

“Welcome to orientation for the newest members of the National Taskforce on the Efficacy of Partnering with the Unexplained for Enforcing the Law.”

I sat up. His eyes focused on me and then on Scharf, and I didn’t like the sudden avarice in his gaze. He looked like a kid on Christmas morning who just got exactly what he wanted. I glanced around the room. He’d said *newest* members. So this wasn’t the whole task force. I’d been feeling pretty good, magically speaking, about my chances with this crowd, but this was just the new recruits.

“We are here to increase the efficacy of today’s law enforcement efforts by preparing for and exploiting anomalies uncovered by forensic and investigative techniques.”

I leaned toward Scharf, “What did he just say?”

She shifted in her chair and settled without answering me.

“I’m Martin Giudice, Counterterrorism. Thank you for coming. This is my associate and tech support for the day Alexander Axel.” He gestured to Mrs. Henderson, “This is Gloria Henderson, who will be taking minutes.”

The lights in the room went out and I “eeped” again. The FBI logo behind him dissolved into a blue screen. I glanced around and saw the Viking manipulating a small tablet.

The fancy seal of the FBI popped back up on a white background with the task force name spread out below it.

“Since the Bureau’s inception,” Giudice continued, “we’ve kept records of open cases or evidence we could not explain. We are confident like the mysteries of the past, that science will catch up with criminals. But for now, we must work with what we know.”

I bounced in my seat. “Are we going to see the grassy knoll?”

With the lights low, Scharf didn’t bother with a poke. She elbowed me in the ribs.

He started flipping through what looked to be media coverage of various crimes. I drank it up, entranced with guys in zoot suits from the 1920s looking at glowing guns and what looked like a zombie. Many said solved.

“Here are the unsolved.”

The screen changed, and I stopped breathing as I saw the wreckage of Inauguration Day from the air. I’d been under the wooden platform, fighting for my life, and then I’d gotten arrested. I hadn’t seen the after-effects. It looked like a bomb had gone off—only without the bomb part. Destruction with no known cause.

Scharf whispered, “You aren’t subtle.”

I frowned at her.

“Effective!” she added.

I sat slouched back in the chair, heart pounding. “But not subtle.”

I focused on Giudice to find him staring straight at me. Whatever he’d said about the unexplained, he had an explanation for this one.

# 6

I glanced at Mrs. Henderson to see how she was taking this talk of my former destruction. Her hands were still on the table; she was staring at the screen without blinking. I nudged her, and she turned on me with a snarl. I raised an eyebrow, and she hunched over her notebook.

I turned back to Scharf. “At least he doesn’t have a picture of Union Station to add.” That one had been chalked up to structural instability. Which was technically true. I’d definitely destabilized that structure.

Lee raised his hand. “Sir, you should investigate the events at Union Station over Christmas.”

I collapsed back in my chair. *Never mind.*

“Thank you, Detective,” Axel said.

“Hot damn. This explains so much.” Lee sounded thrilled.

“Yes, uh, Detective, thank you,” Giudice added. “You can so clearly see that we have left the bounds of science. For now. I leave the implications of that to the talking heads in that building,” he said, pointing right toward the National Mall. I tried to remember how the mall was laid out. He was either pointing at the Capitol or the White House. “And the tests the military are running.”

Mrs. Henderson and I came to attention. I looked at Scharf, but she shrugged subtly.

“What I’m interested in is keeping our people safe. And they are not safe with people like that running around. You are here because you’ve demonstrated an expertise that I find useful.”

I didn’t know what was scarier, the heads apparently talking about us, the military testing us, or the guy right in front of me who found me *useful*.

“For the law enforcement officers, you’re on special assignment as of today. You will still be deployed in your positions but will be working on NTEPUEL investigations. For the civilians among us, you will be compensated for your time. I recognize that you may have other obligations and thank you for the assistance you offer. Needless to say, if any word of this gets out, you will be violating US Code 3341 governing national secrets and will be prosecuted.”

I sat back and crossed my arms. “Apparently, I am a national secret.”

“With us today we have Agent Laura Scharf of Violent Crimes out of the DC office, Detective Daniel Lee of Metro PD Homicide, Dr. Henry Shonkoff, Ufology.” The guy dressed worse than Lee with all of his folders straightened up.

“Is that… UFOs?” I whispered as Giudice finished, “And Orange Sinclair.”

I forgot about aliens.

“Folklore expert,” Scharf said into the silence of my lack of credentials.

Giudice looked between us. “Of course.”

Mrs. Henderson said, “And the other?”

She pointed to the screen where Madame Juliette was listed beneath my name.

“I’m here!” The door opened and a woman dressed in skinny black jeans and a black velvet shirt waltzed into the room. She had long fair hair and very light skin; the blue of her veins glowed in the projector’s light.

“And Madame Juliette,” Giudice added. Axel flipped the lights on, and I straightened in my chair, only now realizing I had been slowly trying to sink below the table. A few more orange lines popped up hopefully, showing me a way out. I ignored them.

“Apologies for the delay,” the woman said with a subtle, lilting accent I could not place and was probably fake. “I do not subscribe to earthly branding and so had some difficulty with your gatekeepers.”

No one had anything to say to that.

“They wanted a driver’s license,” she clarified with a dramatic shudder. “I also do not hold with hurling myself around the city in such inflexible conveyances.”

I narrowed my eyes at that one. I wanted to ask her what she meant by inflexible. Did she mean iron? Fairies didn’t like iron. She looked human as she wandered around the room, but she could be glamoured just like I was.

“This space is filled with such violence,” she said with another shudder as she stretched long thin fingers over the table.

“Madame is the most successful psychic in the country,” Giudice said slowly.

This time it was Scharf who muttered, “Oh, my god.”

I pinched her side.

The psychic’s eyes were closed, and she was swaying slightly. The professor ignored her and stared at the now blank screen with a rapt expression on his face like he’d had some kind of religious experience.

My legs twitched under the table and the tremors threatened to spread to the rest of me. An orange line was branching out toward every exit, including the windows. I’d jumped from such a height before, but only with a bargain in place. I *really* didn’t want to be here.

The psychic gathered herself and pointed to Mrs. Henderson, who said, “No.”

She moved on to Scharf and said, “You have a tragic history.”

Scharf glanced at me, and I shrugged and whispered, “Seems human? Maybe.”

The woman pivoted to me and said, “So do you.”

Lee muttered, “Imagine that.”

I smirked.

I’d always like the guy, his penchant for arresting me notwithstanding.

“Ma’am, please take a seat,” Axel said.

The woman walked in a circle and Scharf repeated with some heat behind the words, “Take a seat.”

The woman sat down. Giudice said, “Ms. Furst, thank you for gracing us with your presence.”

Scharf stood up. “Before we get to more foretelling, I wanted to bring a case currently with Art Crimes to the, uh, task force. Due to some unexplained elements, they won’t be in a position to, um, investigate.” She sounded like a teenage version of Scharf giving a school presentation she hadn’t prepared for. I stared at her open-mouthed, and she sat down and elbowed me.

“What is unexplained?” the professor, whose name I forgot, spoke up for the first time. I didn’t like the light in his eye.

“There were, um, no aliens involved,” Scharf said, but her hesitancy said: *there are aliens involved.* That’s certainly how the professor took it. He started scribbling in one of his folders.

Kidnapped child. I was here for him. And for the goblin sitting next to me, ungrateful though she was. That was what this was about, whatever the dude with the buzzcut was saying about all the threats. Also, they were compensating me. My consultant work got a stipend from Scharf’s office, but not enough to pay DC rent. It mostly covered the mug cake ingredients. With my mom’s bills piling up for her treatment, I wasn’t going to sniff at cash.

Another orange line showed up right through the back wall. I’d also walked through walls before when completely desperate. Apparently, I really, really did not want to be here.

Scharf straightened, “Sir, I’d like to revisit the museum heist if we could.” I could read her nerves in the stiffness of her shoulders.

Axel spoke up. “I evaluated that scene. Art crimes has it.”

Scharf took a deep breath and turned to Lee. “Did you bring the footage?”

Lee slid a flash drive to Axel.

Axel tossed it between his hands. “This secure?”

Lee nodded. “It’s been through the Metro labs. Scrubbed.”

Axel nodded, glanced at Giudice, and fed it into his little tablet. The TV screen lit up with the intact exhibit, and a little counter on the top right corner of the screen said 3:00 AM.

Scharf took a deep breath and said, “Play it.”

It rolled forward, nothing changing. When the first guy came into view, I sucked in a breath. I glanced a Scharf, and she nodded quickly. She saw them.

I let out all the air in a whoosh. I glanced around the room. Everyone was watching without exclamation.

We were coming up on the snatch. Scharf said, “Pause it there.”

She looked at me. I shook my head, wishing we had rehearsed this part. How do you make someone believe?

“What do you see?” she finally asked when I stayed silent.

“Scharf?” Giudice asked.

“Just an exhibit? Notice anything else?”

Everyone shook their heads.

She took a quiet breath and said, “Play it forward.”

On this bigger screen, I could see the men better. I noticed in this light that their coats seemed to be deep red not black, and they were tailored to match knee breeches from some bygone era.

A few had beards and looked a little older, so they weren’t copies of one person like the ghuls we’d faced before. Their hats were tricornered like a sea captain’s from long ago. I couldn’t pinpoint the exact age. After Shakespeare, before the steam engine.

They reached their appointed displays, and that hand came into view. I scanned the assembled group as he snapped, and they made the snatch. The professor jumped and tried to hide it.

Axel shrugged. “So someone spliced the cameras.”

Lee shook his head. “It wasn’t doctored. That’s one continuous stream, no cuts. According to our guys.”

Giudice said, “Axel, get this footage, we need our own guys looking at this.”

“Sir, if I could,” Scharf said. “Could you rewind?”

I shook my head, and whispered, “Ask to drive.”

“What?”

I took a breath. “If I could get that doohicky, Mr. Agent, Sir?”

Axel double-blinked. “No?”

“We have *another* clip to play,” I said, looking at Scharf. Mrs. Henderson pinched me. Hard. I ignored her. “A different camera.”

Scharf nodded, finally understanding, and got up. Without asking, she took the tablet from Axel. “In this *different* clip, you will see the perpetrators. How they hid on the main security cameras is, well, unexplained, but you can see them clear as day. Short men with dark jackets who walk in with the dirt and walk out with the artifacts.”

Scharf looked calm, but I could see the artery in her neck pounding as she took her seat with the tablet. She hit a little button that said, “stop sharing screen” and hit rewind. In the sudden dark, with only the light of the tablet on Scharf’s face, she looked inhuman. When we got to the moment just before the men entered, she stopped, turned the projector back on, and hit play.

I held my breath, my feet vibrating under the table. Mrs. Henderson was completely frozen beside me, staring into the side of my neck as if she could slit it with her eyes.

The first little man walked on screen and there was a collective intake of breath.

“What the…” somebody said and was shushed.

“You see them?” Scharf said confidently, but the stress was clear in her voice.

“Where did you get a second tape?” Lee demanded.

“Just watch,” Scharf said.

I watched the shadows, trying to see the finger snapper enter. He was good. Or he could genuinely hide from video cameras, not just unnoticed but actually invisible. His hand appeared. The snap, transfer, and exit happened, and Scharf hit pause. With a deep breath, she set the tablet carefully on the table.

“Where did you get that?” Lee said, his affable detective facade gone.

“Why didn’t Art Crimes get it immediately?” Giudice asked, and I rolled my eyes. Apparently the unexplained paled in comparison to breaching regulations.

She glanced around the room. “It’s the same footage.”

“What are you talking about?” Axel asked and snatched back the tablet. He closed the video and spun through a few apps. The screen was still shared, so we could see everything he did. We came to a view of a normal file folder with one video in it. “There’s one file!”

Giudice’s gaze bounced between all of us. “What is this?”

“What is going on?” the professor asked as if he’d suddenly woken up from a nap.

My shoulders climbed to my ears as they all converged on Scharf.

Giudice stood up. He wasn’t tall but didn’t need height to dominate the room. “Explain.”

Scharf had gone pale, but she didn’t shy away. “Sir, when Lee called me to the crime scene, I knew there were potential creatures,” I jerked and she corrected, “People who can go unnoticed on security video. This seemed like a possibility, and I called you. Immediately. First thing, I called you. And we reviewed the footage and. . .” She reached for her hair but put her hand back by her side. “And it took a moment to confirm, but now it’s with this task force.”

Giudice steepled his fingers, very calm. “Thank you for bringing this to our attention. Ms. Sinclair, I assume it’s your—expertise that informed Agent Scharf of this possibility.”

I sat up very straight and funneled as much pompous bureaucrat into my voice as I could. “I’ve consulted for the DC field office on a multitude of cases, often contributing expertise on evidentiary anomalies like this. In this specific case, we’re lucky enough to have footage of the crime, and it was a simple factor to check for the, um, unnoticed.”

Axel coughed. “It wasn’t a killer’s reflection in a toaster. It was twenty men traipsing across thirty feet of open ground.”

“Thank you, Agent Axel,” Giudice said*.* “Does anyone else have a surprise for us this morning? Ms. Furst?”

I looked at the psychic and met her eyes. She’d been staring at *me.* She stirred and broke the impromptu staring contest to look at Giudice. “I can’t explain that,” she said calmly, all trace of the psychic performance gone. For some reason, her normal voice made me think she was even more magical.

Giudice raised an eyebrow. “Yes, well, that’s rather the point.”

I smiled. Giudice had a latent sense of humor in there somewhere.

“Well, since this does seem to fall to the auspices of NTEPUEL,” Giudice said ponderously. “Where is Metro with the case?”

“The case?” Lee said, sitting up straighter and glancing around.

“Tell him about O’Connor,” Scharf said quietly.

“Of course. We interviewed the security guard that had called in sick last evening. His child went missing thirty minutes before he was set to leave for his shift and was returned around midnight. The parents aren’t sure of the time. At his house, there was a Celtic symbol on the glass of the back door, very similar to the designs at the crime scene. Which led us to conclude, well, hypothesize. That is to say, we think the child was taken to force that man to call out, to allow another worker to work that shift.”

Giudice stood up. “And where is that worker?”

“Still missing, Sir.”

“Along with a motherload of our national heritage?” Giudice added mildly.

“Yes, Sir,” Lee confirmed.

“Any leads on the, um, national heritage? Black market? Dark web? ” Scharf asked.

Lee grimaced. “We’ve been looking into tracing what has gone missing. All are American-made, of course, so the provenance is considerably shorter. In fact, one is a local artist. He has a shop of some sort near Dupont Circle. And some of the other pieces came from an Appalachian museum in town. Others were from artists further afield.”

“It wouldn’t hurt to work in parallel to Art Crimes.” Scharf put the slightest emphasis on “parallel.”

“We’ll take Dupont Circle!” I said, holding up Scharf’s arm.

Giudice nodded. “Lee, you take the museum. We’ll officially add the heist to the list of NTEPEUL’s active cases.” He glanced at Axel. “Please inform the rest of the team. Have one of them get on the Art Crimes force to babysit them. And we need somebody on that footage.”

Axel nodded. “I’ll get Ko—”

“Yes, that’s perfect,” Giudice said quickly. “I should mention ladies and gentlemen when you go about your investigation, no one but us will know about this task force. Please refer to your original credentials but keep in touch with Axel. Welcome to the team.”

We all nodded as we walked out of the room.

As we all trooped toward the elevators, Lee mumbled to himself, “I knew it. And I can’t say a damn word. I just knew it.”

When he got in the elevator, I held Scharf back. Mrs. Henderson didn’t leave my side.

Once the doors shut on the psychic, the professor, and Detective Lee, I blinked twice and looked to Mrs. Henderson. We said to each other, “You were right.”

“I shouldn’t have come,” I added.

She shook her head. “They already knew. I didn’t, not about you. I’m sorry, Sinclair.”

“You would have warned me?”

Scharf cut in. “Everything okay?”

“Just how long do *consultants* stay on this task force?” I asked.

“Well…” Scharf looked distinctly uncomfortable, which given her usual poker face, was really saying something.

“Scharf, what if I go back to California?”

“The FBI has offices all over the country.”

“Scharf! Just don’t joke. Ever.”

The elevator dinged and we trooped on, but I could hear her say, “I wasn’t.”

I leaned against the elevator panel. I was helping Scharf because I had a bargain to fulfill, balancing the scales in exchange for her helping keep me out of jail after my powers came in and I destabilized Union Station.

And yes, okay, I enjoyed the power she had and how we made things come out right for people. But did I want to do this for the rest of my life? A life that would stretch considerably longer than the average human?

Scharf, in a very un-Scharf gesture, stuffed her hands in her pocket. “I don’t know Orange. I didn’t know about this. I didn’t know they had your name already. They seem pretty clear on what you’ve done. But I do know you’re on our side, and I’m glad about that. And I know there are a few people out there who wouldn’t hesitate to kidnap a child. So let’s make ourselves useful.” That was one of the longer speeches I’d ever heard her give.

I glanced at Mrs. Henderson who said, “We want useful. We want to be in bed with the one who finds us useful, not just interesting.”

Scharf said, “Us?”

“Why?” I asked, my teeth grinding together.

“The ones who are just interesting end up in labs.”

# 7

Scharf drove along K Street at about half her usual speed. She hadn’t recovered from that bizarre meeting either.

“I just live my life and help you consult, and they leave me alone,” I said again, probably for the third time.

“That’s what they said.” We pulled to a stoplight, and she took the opportunity to yank on her hair.

“Just promise me this one thing,” I said. “If I get stuck in a lab, come get me.”

She sighed. “Orange, that’s not a thing.”

“And Area 61 is just a stretch of desert in New Mexico.”

“Tell me there are no aliens.”

I flung my hands up. “I have no idea anymore. Maybe Area 61 has *us* in it!” Impulsively I grabbed her hand. “What can I promise you? What do you need? What’s a fair deal to get you to rescue me if I disappear?”

“You can’t offer me anything.”

I sagged back in the seat.

“I’ll do it for free.”

Something deep in my guts unclenched. But the bargaining part of me could not rest. “Don’t offer that.”

“Okay, I’ll do it because I’m your friend. And because we don’t leave men behind.” Sometimes her army days showed a little hard. “I’m serious Orange. You don’t even need to ask.”

“But it will be a government lab, and you’re a government agent.”

“If there is a lab, it won’t be any kind of legal to keep you without you doing anything wrong and without your consent. So it would be very much in my oath to come get you. And everybody else there too.”

I sighed. Of course, this would be about her duty.

She glanced at me. “But even if it wasn’t legal, I would still come get you.”

I couldn’t help my smile. That was a huge concession from Scharf. Unless it was a speed limit, she did not break rules.

“Thanks,” I said and the chime in my head hurt. “Ow.”

“What?” Scharf slowed down again to enter one of DC’s endless traffic circles. This one was massive with a fountain in the middle.

“I kind of have to be your friend now.”

She raised an eyebrow. “Weren’t you already?”

I thought about that. “I guess so.”

She slammed on her brakes without warning.

“What the—”

“I have to get over!” Scharf said. With jolting starts and stops, we muscled our way through dozens of on and off-ramps. For once, I was grateful for her aggressive driving as she finally swooped off an exit on the other side of the circle.

“What was that?”

“The roundabout from hell. Dupont Circle.”

“I know they’re supposed to be so efficient, but that thing was put in when we were riding around on *horses*!”

She just grinned.

“What’s funny?”

“You know most of our streets and laws came from when we were riding around on horses?”

I sat back. “Oh yeah.”

We ended up on a side street of brownstones in one of the more upscale parts of town. There were cultivated gardens and expensive lawn furniture on the little square of cement in front of each house. The cars parked bumper to bumper in front looked expensive.

Scharf parked in a bus zone at the end of the street and got out. I scrambled after her. We walked back toward the roundabout until we got to a collection of white tents. “The artist with crafts in the Smithsonian works at a farmer’s market?”

“What? No. He owns the store behind it.”

This early in the year, the little market stretched the length of one short block and seemed to be perilously short on farmers. A stand with a bunch of honey in different shades sat next to a stall full of screen-printed t-shirts with the monuments on them. One guy at the end of the row sat behind anemic mounds of brussel sprouts, cabbage, parsnips, and potatoes. I slowed down when I saw a pile of baked goods and a woman in yoga gear, complete with mat, chatting enthusiastically with the baker.

“Oh yeah, they’re gluten-free, vegan, soy-free. These over here are paleo!” the baker said, pointing to some flat-looking treats on the end.

I froze.

I did an about-face and went to the stall at the end with t-shirts from the farmers market and a cash machine propped up under the tent. “How do I get a stall here?”

The girl popped a bubble and handed me a stack of forms. I took one look at the top one and dug out my cell phone. “Astrid?”

“You’re calling to cancel.”

*Cancel what?* “Definitely not! We’re selling in the wrong place. We shouldn’t be on campus with broke college students. We should be at the Farmer’s Market for the people with house plants outside!”

“Who?”

“I have forms!” I said, waving them in front of me.

“You? You have forms,” Astrid said flatly.

I stiffened. “I can contribute more than recipes to this. They’re vegan!”

“The forms are vegan?” I could hear incredulity.

“No, my mug cakes. Cake in a bag? I forgot what we’re calling them. They’re *vegan.*”

“Why didn’t you tell me?” This time, excitement colored her voice.

“I’m not going to leave milk and eggs to sit out in the sun! Besides, oil has a better mouth feel. Anyway! We need to be on this side of town with the delusional yuppies. Trust me; I’m from California. You stick vegan on the label, and we can double the price.”

“Let me do some research.”

“You always say that.”

I flipped through the packet. Where was the price? We were going to have to front the price of a stall and then sell enough to not go into debt from it. See? I knew the extreme bare minimum of business: sell more than you spend to make them.

“There you are!” Scharf said. I spun to see her tapping her foot. “What are you doing?”

“Astrid? I have to go.”

“But we have to talk this out. We have to look at fees. I bet there’s a license. This would be the biggest outlay of cash we’ve ever done.”

“We’ll figure it out,” I said and covered my phone to say, “Two seconds.”

I put my phone against my ear long enough to hear, “*You* always say that.”

I remembered the appointment she was talking about. “I’ll see you this afternoon, right? This is a good idea.”

“If you do say so yourself,” Astrid said, but she still sounded happy.

“You do the driver’s license part.”

“Orange, it’s not a license for driving. It’s a license for food service. Never mind.”

She hung up before I could say anything else. “I told you two seconds!”

“That was more than... Never mind. Come on.” She led me back through the market. “Oh, and I got a call from our dirt expert at the FBI lab.”

“Yeah?” I was just happy we wouldn’t have to take a drive into Virginia where the giant FBI labs were.

“It’s from around here. It’s just DC dirt.”

I sighed; we’d had a major break in a case when she’d been able to isolate sand to a region in the Middle East.

Scharf shrugged. “She’s going to run more tests, though. See if she can narrow it down more than that.”

We ducked between two white tents, and I asked, “Where is this shop?”

She pointed. I saw a four-leaf clover on the sign with the words, “Celtic Central.”

The door hit a windchime as Scharf opened it. The shop was the first floor of a ubiquitous DC brownstone. The storefront was narrow but deep, stretching the whole length of the ground floor.

It was also very green inside. Near the door, I could see a collection of woolen hats, sweaters, and scarves. The walls were tagged with famous Irish sayings in whirly green font and more four-leaf clovers. Silver jewelry was locked in a cabinet in front of me and behind that was a wall of books.

I picked up a key chain that said, “Kiss me, I’m Irish.”

“Gee golly, we’d better call out art crimes before they steal the lot.”

Scharf looked at the keychain. “You’re not helping.”

I put it down.

“You aren’t getting any, um, feelings?”

I looked at her. For all we’d worked together and fought together, she sometimes wanted me to have more power than I did. “I don’t get hunches, Scharf. I make bargains. And disappear. And move dirt around.”

Scharf shook her head and continued to the next display.

“And change how I look. And can do a little bit with water. And also hob.”

“That one!” She said, pointing to me. “That’s not hunches? Feelings? Foretelling?”

I’d once pulled a man’s toupee off his head in exchange for an answer to my question. Hobgoblins could pull their power out of the humans around them, not just the elements, and I wasn’t all that clear myself on what that meant. I knew it came with a normal bargain, but the end result was usually not what I expected. Thus, not very useful. Scharf paused and looked at me. “That’s it? That’s the current list?”

I frowned. “And I know how to escape any situation.” Even as I said it, an orange line glowed back out the front door. I shook my head to disperse it. “And I can jump out of buildings.”

“Yeah, I know about that one. And probably so does Giudice.”

I winced and ducked deeper into the shop. Beyond the books, I could see a few more serious pieces, crystal vases and pottery sets that were much higher quality than the stuff you find at, well, farmer’s markets.

“That’s it?” Scharf pressed.

I shrugged. “For now, I think, yes?”

“Don’t tell that to Giudice.”

“Wow, thanks Scharf for that brilliant insight. Because I was going to tell him everything, but now that you’ve said that, I’m keeping my mouth shut, boy.”

“Is Mrs. Henderson a goblin?”

I blinked twice. “Just over here, keeping my mouth shut.”

“That means yes.”

“It doesn’t!”

“You didn’t say no.”

“But that’s not a yes!” I protested.

“Is Giudice a goblin?”

“No!”

She raised an eyebrow and I sagged. “That’s not the same thing.”

“Got him!”

We pivoted, Scharf with her hand on her weapon, to see a girl with a mop of honey-blond hair perched behind an old-fashioned cash register. Her eyes were glued to the screen of her phone, and she was waving it in front of her face.

“Um, hello?” Scharf said.

She jumped and put down the phone in a hurry. “Can I help you?”

I had to get through a display of wispy scarves and a row of tartans to get closer to her. She blinked red-rimmed eyes. “I’m sorry, but I think we’re closed.”

I half expected a lilting accent, but she sounded pure American.

Scharf looked back at the door. “It says…”

“I know, my mom wanted to open to honor him, but she’s gone back upstairs.”

Scharf palmed her badge. “I’m from the FBI.”

She looked up with sudden hope. “Do you have news?”

Scharf didn’t answer that, only asked, “Can you tell me who you are?”

She hopped off the stool. She was taller than me by a few inches, so very short for a human. She wore a t-shirt that was stamped with Celtic Central and a name tag that said Kristy. “I’m Kristina Gallagher.” She looked at us hopefully.

Scharf took a breath. “Your Brian Gallagher’s. . . “

“Granddaughter. He’s been gone five years already. I thought we were fine, but then to hear his art was taken. It was like mom lost him all over again.”

I glanced at Scharf, but she had her poker face on. I stepped up. “We’ve done everything we can to find his art and recover it for now. In exchange,” I added carefully, “Can you tell me the real story as you know it?”

“I. . . “

“About the theft?” I added quickly.

“Of course, anything I can do!” she said, bobbing her head and sniffling. Her phone beeped, and she quickly turned it off. “It’s just a stupid game.”

I glanced at the black screen. “No worries. The art?”

“I don’t know who would do this thing! I don’t know at all. It was the honor of a lifetime to be included in the exhibit. I just wish Grandda was alive to see it.” She bounced around the corner and as the chime sounded, she said, “I’m sorry, we’re closed due to family tragedy.”

She ran to the door and shut it on the young woman and her daughter who were trying to come in.

She spun back around and disappeared behind the books and tartans to emerge by low, glass display cases along the far wall I hadn’t noticed. We joined her at the cases and looked down at a collection of swords, daggers, and spears.

“Dear god,” I said.

She mistook my shock for awe. “Aren’t they beautiful? They’re dirks and long daggers made in the traditional way.” She pointed to one wicked blade with bronze etching and a long wooden, perfectly round handle. “See the handle? He used traditional methods. He made the fake stuff too for Renaissance Fairs, but this was his true passion.”

“They’re beautiful,” I said.

She gulped. “And now they’re the only ones left. The bulk of his collection went to the exhibit. We thought it such an honor.”

“We?” Scharf asked.

“My parents own this shop. Well, grandda did and then they did, and then me someday. They’re the ones who donated them.”

I took a breath. “And there was nothing odd around the transfer of the items? No men you’ve seen around. Short? With hats? Other things disappearing?”

“You have a suspect?”

“We can’t discuss an ongoing investigation,” Scharf answered before I could say anything.

“Is there anything else you know about the crime?” I asked when I didn’t get a chime signaling I got the information I needed.

She shook her head before leaking tears all over again. “They didn’t even tell us! We heard it on the news! My parents have been calling the museum every day. We don’t know what to do!”

Scharf fished a card from the wallet her badge was attached to and handed it over. “You can reach me at any time.”

I winced at that broad promise and added, “Nine to five is probably best.”

“Orange,” Scharf said quietly.

“Oh, were you looking to buy?” Kristy asked.

Scharf echoed, “Buy?”

“You said orange?” Kristy led us to a display of plaids. “We carry all the big names,” she said, fishing a neon orange cloth cut through with greens. “The Camerons are probably the most famous. But we have them all.”

I backed away from the eye-searing color that was definitely not traditional.

“No, thank you,” Scharf said.

She started to turn for the door, but I shook my head. No chime had sounded, so our bargain was not done. “Tell us about your grandda. About his art.”

“He started when he was a little boy. He learned from his grandda. Straight from Ireland. It was an art to him, which is what the museum definitely saw. Like I said, he’d do all the fairs and sell here and on Etsy.”

Scharf cleared her throat. “You can buy weapons on Etsy? The craft site?”

She nodded enthusiastically. “But in the end, a lot of them were just direct sales. Collectors all over the world wanted a Gallagher blade. But that’s all done.”

Scharf looked at me, and I shook my head.

She asked, “Have you had any problems with theft before?”

Kristy shrugged. “I mean, there are always shoplifters. Mostly they go for the jewelry.”

She pointed to the case with the cash register on it. There were some beautiful pieces. Necklaces with polished stones and bracelets of intricate knots of silver, but even to my untrained eye, I could see there was something special about those knives. Of all the pieces here, they really did belong in a museum.

I frowned. “What about clients? Not theft, but someone who wanted to buy, but couldn’t?”

She sat up straighter. “There was a guy asking. He was Irish, which we don’t get that many of, ironically. He wanted to take a bunch of blades back home, he said. But then the museum called.” She retreated to the counter for another tissue. “My parents had a big fight about it. We aren’t exactly rolling in it. And the rent here is just crazy. It was fine in the seventies but now it’s almost all of our profit. Dad wanted to sell. It would have been a big sale. But Mom was adamant that the museum was a better legacy. Since grandda was her dad, she won.”

The chime echoed in my head. “Do you have info on that buyer? Any other potential buyers that lost out with the donation?”

She nodded. “Um, probably we have the emails. I’ll look?”

“That’s very helpful, thank you.” Scharf smiled, but it was her hunter’s smile and just made her look scary.

As Kristy picked up her phone and silenced the wailing Celtic pipe music that came up with whatever game she was playing, I looked at the blades. They looked like they’d come from another era. “We should probably find that Irishman.”

# 8

“So the feds already knew about you?” Jack, my boyfriend, asked. He was ignoring the dinner he cooked, which meant something was seriously wrong. We technically hadn’t had the boyfriend conversation yet, but what else do you call the man you crash with two to three nights a week and who foists his weird cooking experiments on you? He was either my significant other or an overly officious Airbnb host.

“Maybe. I don’t know,” I replied, spearing an exotic dark green vegetable from a square white plate. I was seated at Jack’s white glass table where I fled to after my meeting with Astrid.

Astrid took my farmer’s market idea and ran with it, booking a stall for the very next weekend. I had days of panicked mixing ahead of me but wasn’t thinking about that yet.

I abandoned Scharf after we left Celtic Central. One meeting with the FBI per day was my limit. She had taken all the paperwork for the people who had tried and failed to buy a knife from Celtic Central before they gave it to the museum. She was going to hand it to the cyber people to try and track down.

“And who were these big wigs?” Jack still hadn’t picked up his fork.

I put my fork down and squinted at him. “You’re unusually interested in this meeting. You know I work for the FBI.”

“You worked for Scharf. One person.”

“And now I work with more than one. Are you going to eat the fancy meal you spent all evening cooking or just stare at it?”

Finally, he sliced off a piece of meat that cost as much as my weekly grocery bill. Something in me unclenched. Jack looked like a Scottish laird from long ago—burly with red curly hair and freckles. His hands were speckled with burns from work. He was a chef at a fusion restaurant only a block from this apartment.

Combining world cuisines was DC’s version of fine dining, and some combos were more successful than others. The Thai Southern thing he had going weirded me out. But when he was home and could cook for himself, he was very, very good.

He was also a demon, which we did not discuss. I kept telling myself the theology seemed greatly overblown. In the schema of magical creatures, he was a spirit like I was a beast. But since I was a hobgoblin, we actually shared a power: the ability to use human emotion to “power” our magic. Which was not to say we punched in the same weight class at all. I could pull down buildings. He could pull down cities.

But he was mostly content cooking for humanity, not smiting them, which was one of the reasons we were together. And I just liked the guy. I’d had a boyfriend in my last year of high school and the first year or two of college. (The end date of our relationship depends on which one of us you talk to.) He was the brilliant but responsible type, and our relationship seemed to consist of him tut-tutting my poor life choices. His words. Jack seemed unfazed by any of my choices, poor or not. And in fact seemed delighted by any chaos I caused, which was another reason we were together.

Except for this choice.

“Jack, what are you so afraid of?”

He stiffened and color flooded his face. “I’m not afraid of anything!”

I grinned. “So what? I investigate crimes, and now I do it as a committee. It will make my job easier.” Was I trying to convince him or myself?

He put his fork down again with his meal half-eaten. His appetite correlated directly to his level of calm, which at the moment was not very. “Look, if you want to run tame with the humans, fine. They hear goblin and they think, how cute. They hear demon and start summoning priests.”

I bit my lip. “And if they summoned a priest, would that work?”

He went completely still, and his face drained of all color. I froze too and could not stop myself from reaching for my magic. But after five tense seconds, he relaxed back in his chair with his signature grin. “It hasn’t worked yet.”

Meaning somebody sometime had tried it.

“Don’t bring me up,” he said, carefully casually.

I held up my hands. “I have a dozen people’s secrets in my head. I’m trying to help, not recruit us all.”

My phone rang, and we both jumped. He said, “Put it on airplane mode.”

“It’s Scharf.” I answered the call. “What?”

“We have another meeting tomorrow.”

I sagged. “I can’t do that again.”

Jack pointed at me in triumph, and I swatted at his hand.

“I mean, just so soon. I can definitely do it again.”

“Not with the brass. Hernandez found something in the dirt. She knows where it’s from.”

He grabbed my hand and drew one finger into his mouth. I pulled free and scooped a little potato off his plate and shoved it in his mouth and leaned out of reach. “I thought you said it’s just regular DC dirt.”

“Not quite. Seven AM back at the Hoover Building.”

“Why do you people start work that early?”

Jack snapped and suddenly my phone was in his hand, not mine. “Hey!”

He said extremely politely, “Do you have what you need, Agent Scharf?”

“Yes, if Orange—”

He hit the phone and cut her off.

“You hung up on Scharf!”

He looked at me. “What I would not do for you!”

I held out my hand and when he didn’t give it back, I *snatched* it with magic. I tucked it away. “Isn’t it a massive red flag when your boyfriend magically confiscates your phone?”

He smiled and this time it looked a little more natural. “Is that what I am?”

“Hunh?”

“Your boyfriend?”

“I didn’t say that.”

“You just said that.”

I rewound the conversation—something about red flags and boyfriends. *Oops*. “I’ll make a deal with you,” I said and started collecting plates. “We go labeless a little bit longer, and I won’t rat you out to the FBI.”

He crossed his arms, finally relaxed in his chair, finally giving me the amused grin that captivated me the first time I’d met him. “That’s a terrible bargain. Unbalanced. A threat of future behavior instead of a promise. And you already agreed not to tell, so it won’t even work. I already got what I want. And you can do the dishes.”

I huffed out a laughed and used the power I’d gathered to sweep up the cutlery and glasses and let them trail after me to the kitchen and into the sink. “Insert good bargain here. No labels, please?”

“We’re already arguing about the dishes.” He stood up and loomed over me, which gave me a pleasant shiver down my spine. “Let’s call a spade a spade, shall we?”

“Who says that? Seriously. A spade?” The sudsy white bubbles filled his white porcelain sink beneath an industrial pipe holding a towel. His whole apartment was white and aggressively hipster. Appliances, dishes, couch.

He put his arms around me, stilling my hands under the hot water. “You’re vibrating over here, and not in a good way.”

I turned off the sink and spun, though leaving a dirty dish in the sink physically hurt. “Okay, fine. Today scared me. I barely know what I’m doing with this goblin thing on a normal day. And have even less of a clue about the rest of my life. And this feels really—”

“Dangerous.”

“Not dangerous.” I twisted away from him. “Did you burn something?”

“I don’t burn food!”

“It just smells like smoke or something.”

He sniffed and said, “I don’t smell it.”

He went to the couch that snaked in front of the kitchen and the essential oil diffuser he had on the end table. He hit the button, and I smelled something piney. Unless he was cooking, he always had a diffuser going. He said it helped him with focus. Maybe another reason I liked him. He was surprisingly California for an East Coast chef. I flopped onto the couch, and he sat down next to me. “This feels really what?” he asked.

I lifted one shoulder to my ear in a half-shrug. “Permanent. This job feels really permanent.” The word hung in the air so I rushed in, “And I just can’t have the relationship permanence conversation on the same day. I just can’t.”

He laughed then, loud and rich. Weirdly, he sounded human when he laughed. I cackled like a, well, goblin. Juya sounded the most like a goddess when she laughed, her voice deepening past any human register. But he sounded more human, not less. “Fair enough. No more permanence.” He stroked a hand down my forearm, raising goosebumps. I let him. When he reached my hand, he clasped my fingers. “But you agreed to do the job anyway?”

“I don’t think I have a choice.”

He gave me a flat look. “You can change your appearance, disappear, and escape every situation.”

I snickered as an orange line led to his front door as if it heard itself talked about. Yes, those were a goblin’s key skills. “I don’t want to change my name. And if I try to get another degree, I’m going to be middle-aged before I finish it. Astrid keeps having ideas. We’re getting a farmer’s market stall for the cakes. If the Dupont crowd will eat them.” I swayed closer to him, feeling my heart speed up as I said, “You’re here. Who else would put up with me?”

He used our joined hands to pull me closer as our lips met, warm and familiar. He deepened it, but I pulled away.

“One more thing.”

He sat back with a familiar amused exasperation.

“The case we’re working on now? They stole a kid. I’m not Scharf who has known since she was in single digits that she was going to right wrongs for a living. But I hated seeing that.”

“I got it,” he said simply. “I admire it.”

“But you won’t be joining NTEPUEL.”

“Remember the priests?”

“I’m kidding.”

He pulled me close again. “How about we forget about the FBI and do something very, very temporary.”

My lips curved against his.

As he pulled me into his lap, somebody pounded on the door, and I found myself on the other side of the couch from one second to the next.

“Damn it!” Jack said from the other side of the couch where he’d leaped.

“Literally?” I asked as I made for the door.

“I knew you should have turned off your phone!”

“I did! I doubt the FBI would knock like that.”

With a crack, the bottom half of the door burst toward us, and the little purple goblin I’d dismissed at the Hoover building in the morning came bursting in. “Princess!”

“My door!”

“You followed me? This whole day!”

“Maybe.” He turtled into his neck, making him even shorter, about three feet. He wore a modern jumpsuit the same color as his skin. I looked down at my orange leather and thought about Mrs. Henderson’s green clothes. Did we all end up in clothes the same color as our skin? Was this some kind of weird self-expression in a world where we had to hide? *Focus*.

“Why did you wait until *now* to bust the door in?” I asked him with no little pique.

He shrugged. “Never ask on an empty stomach.”

Jack’s mouth dropped open, and he was turning almost as many colors as the goblin with his fair skin. “This little sucker has been waiting outside the door until—”

“Jack, please.” I got between him and the goblin. “What do you want? I know how this works. You want something. You’re going to try to trick me into thinking my father would give it to you. I’m going to surprise you that I already have access to my magic because I’m half-human, and we’re going to make some kind of bargain I will think is fair but will end up regretting later.”

The goblin perked right up at that. “Perfect!”

Jack gaped at me. “Just how many goblins have been following you around begging for help?” He crossed his arms. “Did they bust down Scharf’s door?”

I started to deny it but hadn’t been there in a day. “I’m not sure. I’m going to get rid of him.”

The goblin gasped and clung to my leg. “Please Princess, do me no harm!”

“I am not going to harm you!” I tried to peel him off. “A little help here?”

“I’m not the Princess.” Jack sat on the couch and lay one foot across his knee, clearly deciding to be entertained.

“Thanks!”

Jack sobered for a minute. “You don’t want me helping. Not with a fellow with a much, much weaker magic.”

I glanced at him and could see the beginnings of a flame in his eyes. I swallowed and said, “Good point.”

I finally detached the goblin and set him down. “Stay right there. Who are you? Where did you come from, and what do you want? And make it quick.”

“I am Green.”

“Your name is Green and you’re purple?”

“Um. Yes?” He sounded American, and I didn’t remember any historical goblins who were purple, so probably just a European transplant from the formerly Celtic countries like the rest of us. Being so close to him though, he had a patchouli, earthy smell and a glazed look in his eyes I didn’t like. “Are you high?”

He cocked his head. “No? I am very low.” He put his hand up at his head and then reached up for mine.

I crouched down so we were eye to eye. “This had better be good.”

“She doesn’t love me!” The last word ended with a wail. “You must fix it, Princess.”

Jack guffawed.

“You have got to be kidding me. And don’t call me that!”

“I am not Kidding Me! I am Green.”

I ground my teeth. “You can’t make someone love you.”

“But you can!” he said, taking my philosophical advice literally. “You hob!”

“I won’t!” When he tried to glom on again, I kept him away from me with magic. His little grubby hands kept reaching for me. I wiped off my arm, though he hadn’t left a residue.

“She has to love me.”

“Why? Why do men think that women have to love them?”

“Because I love her!”

I pointed at Jack. “Don’t say a word.”

“And I have to have her!”

Jack mimed zipping his lips.

I thought about throwing the little goblin out a window but didn’t know if he had access to his magic to land safely. I’d have to bargain with him and make sure he would live, and then throw him out the window. I sat down on the floor. “Where do you come from?”

He pointed left. I wondered if he was seeing a purple line in his head back to his home. I had to think about Jack’s apartment and what direction it was. North.

“I live at the newest, tallest tower!” He was extremely proud of that.

“What tower?”

“On the island city. The biggest city in the tallest building!”

I blinked. “You live in the new World Trade Center?”

He cocked his head. “What’s that?”

“You live in New York. Where’s the girl?”

“She lives in only the fourth tallest. But she’s beautiful.” I wasn’t sure which was the fourth tallest, the Chrysler Building or the Empire State Building, or another nameless skyscraper. It didn’t matter.

Goblins claimed homes. I guessed in New York, the height of that home mattered. I was glad in DC, nothing could be taller than the Washington Monument. I hid a smile at what he would think of Scharf’s two-story house out in the suburbs.

“And you heard there was a new *Peallaidh,* and you just had to come. Wasn’t there a hobgoblin closer?”

He scoffed. “There’s one on the seventeenth floor but he wouldn’t help me!”

I crossed my arms.

“You won’t help me either? But I’ll die if she doesn’t love me!”

Jack crossed his arms and said in a falsely sweet voice, “He just can’t die. You have to do something.”

I glanced at him. “I considered throwing him out a window.”

Green turned a different shade of purple and started running around the room.

“But I didn’t!” I said to his whizzing form. “Don’t move. Let me see if I can try to help.” I rubbed my temples trying to think of a bargain that would make him think he was getting what he wanted and prevent him from ever going near her again. Nothing presented itself.

“Why would you want someone who doesn’t want you back?” I asked, helpless to stop myself.

He cocked his head. “She’s beautiful. She’s powerful. She’s smart. She lives on a higher floor. Her ears almost reach her shoulders!”

“Quite the catch,” Jack said, still looking highly entertained.

I resisted the urge to tug on my ears, relieved mine only extended an extra couple inches, though apparently longer was more attractive? I realized I hadn’t the slightest idea what goblin courtship and marriage customs were. Maybe women did just get carried off by obsessed dudes and lived happily ever after. I shuddered; not while I was the *Peallaidh.*

“Did you ask her?”

He nodded spastically. I sighed in relief. “And she said no.”

He nodded again sadly.

“I’m sorry dude. There is no coming back from that!”

Jack stretched, his shoulders bunching, “I don’t know. I’ve been slowly wooing you for months.”

Green looked at him, ears flopping. “How?”

Jack cleared his throat and said, “I won her by word and deed, beast.”

I crossed my arms and muttered, “Stop calling us that.”

“This evening, I won the word *boyfriend* by cooking for my fair maiden, offering her shelter, listening to her tale of woe, and was rewarded with her—trust.”

I rolled my eyes, but Green was lapping it up. *Hmm.* “That’s your task, Green. You do whatever she asks.”

“Good, good, good,” he said, nodding spastically.

“By listening to her very, very carefully and going away the very second she asks you to.”

“But how will I win the fair maiden if I have to keep going away from her?”

“That is the trick. You might have to stay away your entire life. Just to show your love.”

He stuck a finger in his ear, plucking at the hair there as he thought. He nodded and said, “I accept your quest.”

“No, no—”

A chime sounded in my head, and I began to groan. I’d bargained despite myself. But then a *second* chime sounded, a tone lower. I froze. *What was that?*

I shook my head, the dual notes still ringing. The high note I’d heard a dozen times before, the chime sealing the start or end of a bargain. The lower one I’d never experienced.

Jack leaned forward. “You okay?”

I thought back over his words. “Green, what’s a quest?”

HE put one hand to his heart. “I complete your tasks and win the fair maiden.”

“Yeah, okay, sure, good, fine. Just in general, what’s a quest?”

He tugged on one ear, eyes unfocused. He’d perked up talking about his girl but now looked listless. “A quest is a quest?”

“Right. So I helped enough?” Enough for him to leave us alone?

“I have a quest!” he said, pounding fist to heart again.

“You sure do.”

He marched out the hole he’d made in the door. Jack closed his eyes, and the wood knit back together. I gaped. “How’d you do that?”

I could move earth around, but I was pretty sure I couldn’t repair it back to its original state.

He opened his eyes and cracked his neck with a quick twist of his head and turned the diffuser to a higher setting. “I put it back together? I don’t know.”

“I could never do that.”

“It’s not an earth power. I can’t move earth.”

“Just reality,” I said as I sat next to him on the couch.

He grinned. “Exactly!”

“I was joking!”

“You could probably hob.”

I shuddered. “If I try emotion to fix a door, no doubt a construction crew would burst in your ceiling and totally fix the door while wrecking everything else.”

He didn’t laugh. “What happened?”

I squirmed. “What do you mean?”

“When he said quest, you went as orange as I’ve ever seen you.”

I winced. I forgot he didn’t see my blond, pale glamour, but my orange skin and frizzy hair.

“Orange, you still don’t trust me?”

Did I trust him? Why were we having the boyfriend conversation if I could not answer that question?

“I don’t know what happened.” Unable to bear his gaze, I got up and went to the window to stare at the brownstone across the street. “Something new.”

I jumped when his hands landed on my shoulders. “Did it hurt? Do you think it was dangerous?”

My head was shaking before I thought through his question. “It felt like a bargain. Only…” *Not.*

“Only?”

I spun and looked up at him. At five feet flat, I was used to looking up at everyone and took comfort in his smile. “I didn’t hold a side of it? That doesn’t make any sense.”

I tugged on his hair to get him to bend and kiss me, distracting both of us, but still, that note echoed in my head.

# 9

The next morning, bright and earlier, I found myself scooting my way around the big table in a familiar conference room at FBI headquarters. The lights were already dim, and the FBI logo glowed from the projector. *Two meetings in two weeks.* Meetings weren’t at the top of the list for why I didn’t want to be trapped in this gig long-term, but they were on the list.

I passed Axel and Lee to get to a chair next to Scharf close to the screen. There didn’t seem to be anyone leading this thing. Scharf bobbed her head at me as she scrolled through her phone.

“Hi! I’m Maria,” a chipper voice said from behind me. I peered into the dim and recognized Maria Hernandez, a tech from the big labs in Virginia. She’d helped us with a sand problem on a previous case.

I twisted around. “Um, hi. Nice to see you again.”

“Again?”

I winced. “Nice to meet you.” I forgot that I’d been invisible in her office while Scharf worked with her.

Hernandez had a few inches on me, which is to say, she was very short, and was dressed in green corduroy pants and a button-down shirt of a slightly different green that clashed. Her long hair was in a ponytail and trailed almost to her hips.

“Ms. Hernandez,” Axel said, “If you would proceed.”

She glanced around at the group and said, “I don’t know why you all had me come here. I could have—”

“Procedure,” Axel interrupted. “Go ahead.”

“Sorry, I’m late!”

We turned as Agent Carlisle swept through the door, and I suppressed a groan. Carlisle was a junior agent fresh out of the academy a year ago whom I privately and once accidentally publicly called the underwear model. 

For the first time in our unfortunate acquaintance, he got outgunned in the looks department by the dude who followed him into the room. 

Scharf scanned both with a professional eye and said, “Carlisle?”

Carlisle was a blond superhero type with a personality disorder. Whatever disorder made one throw everyone else under the bus for the smallest advantage. The man who followed him was less classically handsome. He was shorter than Carlisle. His skin was translucent which made his thick black hair look blue and his bottle-green eyes glow. His only imperfection was an impressive cowlick that made his hair look like he’d just stepped out of a wind tunnel.

Carlisle stood with feet planted, surveying the scene like he was in charge while his companion lounged at his elbow, seeming to cling to the shadows like me.

“This is Cool,” Carlisle said, sweeping his hand back to point to his companion.

Scharf narrowed her eyes. “Your name is Cool?”

I snorted and whispered, “You run around with a girl named Orange.”

She cleared her throat. “I’m Scharf.”

“Ma’am. I’m happy to be of assistance in any way that I may.” Cool’s voice was smooth and deep with a definite lilt, probably Irish, which explained why Carlisle had dug him up. I had to give a small number of props to Carlisle for bringing a useful expert, though it worried me how quickly our little task force had spread through the FBI grapevine.

Carlisle put his fists on his hips and said, “You didn’t think your little task force would go unnoticed? They’ve tapped the best of the best.” He thumbed his breast. “Yours truly.”

And that was the end of the props.

Scharf went still; when her poker face spread to her entire body, I knew to shut up and back away slowly. Carlisle hadn’t caught on. 

Lee, catching the mood in the room, said, “Uh, Cool, what is your expertise?”

“Irish arts and crafts,” Cool said without missing a beat. He glided to the chair next to the professor type who still hadn’t looked up from his folders. 

“I am so sorry to be late” Carlisle added. “Giudice called me into his office a moment ago to go over protocol for the meeting.” He took the seat at the head of the table and said with a wave of his hand, “Please proceed.”

Maria squinted into the gloom for a beat before Axel said, “By all means.”

“Can you pull up the file I sent?” she asked.

Axel tapped on his little screen, and the TV changed to a graph full of spikey lines. The y-axis had a series of minerals listed. The x-axis was a bunch of tiny numbers, but I wasn’t really clear on the unit of measure. I guessed I was looking at the composition of magical dirt.

Maria took a breath. “There are six main soil types in the DMV.”

I glanced a Scharf. “What does the DMV have to do with it?”

“DC, Maryland, Virginia,” she murmured. “DMV.”

I sat back. Of course, DC would make an acronym for the entire region.

“I won’t get into all that for the agent types,” Maria continued, “But what we’re looking for is mineral composition, organic matter, and living matter.”

Scharf glanced at me. “Got that?”

I crossed my arms, “Rocks, dead bugs, and live bugs. Got it.”

“Mostly dead plants!” Maria said brightly.

I cranked on a smile, chagrined she’d heard me. “Thanks.”

“We have the most information about this area because we assist in a lot of local cases, so you’re lucky!” She glanced around at the stone-faced law enforcement officers in the room and swallowed. I gave her a thumbs up and she continued, “So you’re looking at a sample of Pamunkey soil. That’s the official state soil of Virginia, famous for its high clay content and fluvial sediments.”

The whole room tensed when she mentioned Virginia, like hound dogs sensing their quarry. Lee asked, “Where in Virginia exactly?”

Maria swallowed and seemed to remember her audience. “Well, the problem is that it’s all over Virginia. That’s why it’s the state soil.”

Lee groaned.

“But these three elements here narrow it down considerably!” She pointed to a few squiggles indistinguishable to my eyes from the rest. Again she looked hopefully at the room who hadn’t reacted. “Um, I don’t normally give presentations.”

I poked Scharf and whispered, “Smile!”

Scharf smiled. It was her hunting-bad-guys smile.

I poked her again and said, “Smile for real.”

She unbent in her chair and smiled. “That’s okay. Just give us the facts.”

Hernandez nodded and yanked on the end of her ponytail. “Okay. It’s gunpowder.”

Axel jumped to his feet. “What?”

Lee shook his head. “We swept for explosives. The dogs didn’t even blink!”

Hernandez shook her head. “No, no, no. Trace amounts. Really very minuscule amounts. This gunpowder was manufactured in the 1870s.”

“What?” Axel repeated.

Carlisle sat back.

“The Civil War,” Lee said into the silence.

Scharf ran a hand through her hair, knocked off the sunglasses, and dove for them as she said, “You’re saying the dirt came from a century ago?”

Maria blinked. “Well, all dirt comes from centuries ago?” She paled as no one agreed. “This dirt came from a Civil War-era fort or battlefield in DC, Maryland, or Virginia. Within twenty miles of the Potomac. Uh, River. Potomac River.”

Carlisle frowned. “You said it was Virginia soil!”

“No, I didn’t.” She cringed. “I mean, no sir, I’m sorry for any confusion I may have caused. It’s the state soil of Virginia, but it’s not like it stopped at the border. It’s most likely Virginia, but I don’t want to exclude a potential site. This whole area is known as the Piedmont Plateau, which creates this kind of soil.”

“But that’s great,” I said. “How many of these sites can there be? We’ll find it today!”

Scharf shook her head and Lee straight up laughed. “The whole point of the Civil War was to take Washington, DC. Do you know how many forts and battles and campaigns were fought around here?”

“No.” I hunched in my chair, mirroring Hernandez in my discomfort.

Carlisle scrolled through his phone and said, “Two, four, six, ten. . .”

“At least fifty,” Lee said.

Carlisle nodded, scrolling down his screen. “He’s right.”

“It’s um, a Union Fort if that helps?” Hernandez said.

Lee put his head in his hands. “DC was the capital of the Union. They’re all Union Forts.”

Carlisle looked up from his internet search and said, “The list is still at forty-plus. So Detective Lee is unfortunately correct.”

Lee glanced at him. “Thank you.”

“How on earth do you know that?” I asked him.

I meant Lee, but Carlisle said, “Wikipedia?” He turned his phone around to show a list of links. “Civil War sites around DC. It’s got its own page.”

“How did you know it’s from the Union army?” Scharf asked Hernandez slowly.

“There was one main manufacturer of gunpowder for the Union. Du Pont.”

“Like Dupont Circle?” Scharf asked.

“We named a roundabout after the dude making explosives?” I muttered.

Hernandez’s face fell. “I’m not really sure? I’d have to check on that—”

Scharf waved that away. “She’s sorry for interrupting, please explain.”

“Particle size. 15mm with rounded edges is a classic Du Pont milling technique. Plus sulfur from Italy, the Sicily region to be exact. Carbon from charcoal, also imported, which means it was later in the war because they started with willows from their own property and then had to buy charcoal. Plus microscopic oak. They put the gunpowder in old whiskey barrels.”

I smirked. “So it could have that aged oaky flavor?”

“Orange,” Scharf murmured.

“Classic Du Pont,” Hernandez said, “late Civil War Era gunpowder. It’s laced into loam and clay soil from the Piedmont Plateau.”

Scharf rubbed a hand over her face and looked at Axel then around the room. Carlisle and Lee were engaged in an intense whispered discussion. Lee had his arms crossed and his whole body pointed toward the door. Axel peered at the graph on his little tablet, ignoring us all. He hadn’t looked at me once.

Scharf sighed when nobody said anything. “Thank you, Hernandez, for your excellent work. Lee, does Metro PD have an update on the kidnapping?”

Lee slicked a hand over his already slick black hair. “We’re trying to image match the symbol found on the glass. We have found several possibilities, but it seems to be a pretty common Celtic… thing. Forensics have not returned any fingerprints or trace, which indicates the kidnapping was premeditated as they wore gloves. The kid’s unharmed. Pediatrician confirmed that.”

“So nothing,” I said. Very quietly.

Scharf didn’t move her head as she glanced at me. The glance meant shut up. “So no suspects.”

Lee shook his head. “There are security cameras at the metro stop and at a corner store near his house. We’re looking at those.”

The room fell silent as Scharf looked at Axel again. When he didn’t say anything, she straightened and said, “What about the museum?”

“The guard that took his place,” Lee said, “Harold Peterson, is still in the wind. He hasn’t returned to his residence, but Metro is staking it out.”

“Has the art turned up anywhere?” Scharf asked.

“No movement on the actual pieces,” Axel said. “Domestically or internationally that we have found.”

“What about the names from the Celtic shop?” Scharf asked. “The ones I turned in, people who wanted to buy those knives?”

Axel sniffed and said, “Nothing yet. There are a few fences in Ireland that Interpol is keeping an eye on, but so far, they haven’t uncovered any connection between them and DC or other black markets. They’re legitimate collectors. There isn’t a huge trade in—crafts.”

“I would agree,” Cool said in his Irish lilt. “If you’re talking authentic bronze age weaponry or some of the new arts from some of the well-known crafters, that’s a different story. But American-made Irish goods? They’d be good for your Medieval Fairs.”

“Renaissance,” I whispered.

“Pardon?” Carlisle asked.

Scharf poked me as I said, “Renaissance Fairs. They’re from the Renaissance, not the Medieval… Never mind.”

Scharf glanced around the room and said, “Okay then. So let’s see where this dirt leads us. Carlisle, can you put up the list of Union Civil War sites so we can all see what we’re working with? Axel, can we get some agents to support us checking these out? We can divide them up.”

People slowly straightened and focused on her. Carlisle said, “Uh, sure, Axel, I’ll send you a link?”

“Great,” Scharf answered for him. “Lee, how much dirt ended up in the exhibit?”

“Ooh!” Maria raised her hand. “I know that one.”

Lee grinned. “Do share.”

“All told, it is estimated to be over fifty pounds.”

Scharf nodded. “So that would leave a hole. We check these out and circle back here tomorrow?”

Hernandez raised her hand again.

“You don’t have to raise your hand...” Scharf said. “Never mind, what?”

“I could come. Some techs could come and start analysis onsite.”

“Absolutely set that up!” Scharf said.

I poked Scharf and whispered, “Thanks.”

“For what?”

“Thank her!”

“Thank you. That would be very helpful. Anybody have questions, comments, objections?”

Hernandez raised her hand.

“You don’t… Yes?”

“Focus on officially designated historical sites. That the gunpowder survived at all means this ground has been undisturbed for a long time.”

“That still doesn’t narrow it down,” Lee said. “This city has turned everything into a monument.”

“Including one to Civil War battlefield nuns, for God’s sake, “ Carlisle added, staring at his phone.

“It probably was for God’s sake,” I muttered, and Scharf snort-coughed.

“Cool and I will start in Virginia,” Carlisle said. “Scharf, if you could take the north, all the Maryland ones. Detective, since your jurisdiction does not exceed the city, I’ve left you the sites in DC proper. Anything north of the rivers.”

Lee sighed. “Fort Totten, Fort Stevens, and a bunker in Rock Creek Park. I’ll dawdle as hard as I can.”

“That’s it?” I asked.

He scratched his nose as he said, “Well, if the Confederates reached the city, that would have been too late for forts.”

Nobody seemed to know what to say next when Scharf stood up. “See you in two days.”

The door opened again, and Madame Juliette swept into the room, this time wearing jeans and green velvet. “I’m here.”

“We’re good!” Scharf almost shouted.

“Oh, there is always something—” She froze as she caught sight of Cool and all expression left her face for a second before she swept forward. “Mr. Cool. It’s a *pleasure* to see you again. Who knew we’d be in the same room again.”

“You know her?” Carlisle asked.

He nodded. “My father was very fond of… Psychics, is it? We met as she, um, assisted him.”

“Wasn’t I correct? He lived a long life.”

The Irishman burst out laughing. “Oh, aye, a long, long life.”

“Thanks anyway!” Scharf said as she circled the table and made for the door.

“I’m too late?” the psychic asked.

“Darn,” Scharf said.

“We’re searching for forts!” Maria told her.

The psychic spun in a circle, her head bobbing from side to side before settling her gaze on Cool. “I shall accompany you,” she said, her finger pointing to Cool.

“Great idea!” Lee said, patting her forearm before jogging out the door. We followed him out.

As we waited for the elevator, I glanced at him. “How do you know so much about the Civil War?”

“Well, with my last name, people kept asking me if I was related to General Lee.”

“But you’re… “ I trailed off and shut my mouth.

“Clearly not related to a Confederate General?”

“I was not going to say that!” I said, feeling color flood my face and grateful for the glamour that disguised me.

He laughed. “That I’m Korean?”

“Well, yeah.”

“When you’re the only Asian kid in your class growing up in the South, you want any connection to them you can find. They had no idea how the hell genetics worked.” The elevator dinged and we all trooped on. “That’s what started it, but it’s a fascinating piece of history. Especially since most of my family was screwed by our Civil War.”

“Yours?”
