I stiffened my spine. “I am confiscating this. For the interrogation.”

He sputtered but I glanced at Scharf and said, “Bargain.”

“We definitely need that,” she said.

A few walked away muttering as Scharf held open the door for me. I looked around my first official FBI interrogation room. Despite all my brushes with law enforcement, I’d never been in one before. That had more to do with the fact that the field offices Scharf was so proud of weren't set up to detain prisoners. When I’d been arrested, even though I had federal charges against me, I was held in a Metro police jail.

I was a little dismayed to find that it had been more intimidating than this one. This was just a plain office with gray walls and a desk and a couple of chairs. I could see where they retrofitted the room with cameras and recording equipment, but it was nowhere near the setup I’d been subjected to by the local police.

Harry sat with his hands crossed over his body, hunched, and staring at the floor. He never even glanced our way.

The agent across from him said, “What the hell?”

“Above our pay grade. Take a break,” the older dude said.

The man turned a few colors and looked ready to explode, but one glance at Harry shut him up. He hustled out of the room. The agent left, looking more like a teenage girl getting rejected than a law enforcement officer as he shuffled out of the room. Scharf shut the door in his sputtering face. We could vaguely hear shouting outside, but there was clearly soundproofing. The kid finally looked up.

Scharf took his seat, and I took the chair next to hers wedged against the wall in the corner. Axel didn’t move from his position by the door. The kid followed these maneuverings with a lost look on his face.

“Hi, Harry Petersen?” Scharf said.

He straightened a little and said with a sneer, “So the other guys think I'm such a wimp they have to bring the girls to question me?”

My sympathy died really fast.

“Yep,” Scharf said cheerfully. “That's exactly why I'm here. I'm Laura.”

I frowned. She never used her first name. I never used her first name.

“And this is. . . “ She trailed off and glanced at me. “My colleague.”

“And who’s the chaperone?” Harry asked with a glance and away at Axel looking menacing near the door.

“I’m Axel,” he said with false sweetness, “Since we’re all such good friends.”

Scharf glanced at me, and my mind went blank for a second before I remembered the soda in my hand. “You want?”

He looked at the root beer and said, “Yeah.”

I sighed in relief. To make a bargain, you needed to have something they wanted as much as you needed something from them. I held it just out of reach and said, “I’ll make you a deal. You tell me the truth about this whole mess, and you get to enjoy the soda.”

He shrugged.

“Deal?”

“Sure. Because I didn’t do anything!”

I handed him the soda, waiting for the chime, and he said, “It’s warm.”

I winced. I’d said he’d enjoy the soda. “Okay, new deal,” I said quickly. “You tell me the truth, and you get to drink the soda whether you enjoy it or not. Deal?”

“What are you guys doing?” Axel asked and then stiffened when Scharf gave him the Scharf middle finger by staring at him without reacting in the slightest.

“Yes? You didn’t have anything to do with this. Am I right?” I asked; he needed to say yes or deal or nod or somehow agree.

“Yes!”

I nodded at Scharf, fighting a grin.

Scharf straightened in her chair. She was really good at this, and it was fun to watch her work. I was fairly certain this kid was just a kid and not some kind of master criminal out of folklore. But they’d caught him with some of the art, so he was close to whoever was behind this and that one was really starting to piss me off.

“Why don’t you cover your movements on the night of the robbery?” Scharf asked.

“Cover them?”

“Tell us where you were when.”

“I got called in. To the Museum?”

“When and by whom?” Scharf asked.

“By my supervisor. I don't have a regular gig, but there's never not a callout in the museums, especially nights, which pays an extra buck an hour. So I do okay.” He held up a hand. “And before you ask, there wasn't nothing special about it. It was my regular sup on the phone. He called at the usual time. He told me the shift I had to cover, and I went.”

“And you worked a completely normal shift,” Scharf said slowly.

“Yes!”

“Even though there is no record of you past 1 AM, and you were supposed to clock out at 5 AM?”

He rubbed the back of his neck. “That’s not illegal.”

“What isn’t?” I asked.

“I may have slunk off early, but that’s not illegal! Okay, okay. Tell the museum! Fine.”

Scharf said, “The next morning, no one could find you. Where did you go?”

“I got one of those $17 buses to New York.”

“You didn’t answer your phone.”

He wiped his nose. “Well… So… Um… There’s this game I play?”

I hid my smile. “Fae kingdom?”

“How did you know?”

Scharf put a fingertip on my forearm. “We’ll get to that in a sec. What does that have to do with not answering your phone?”

“I told this to the guys already.”

“Quick summary,” Scharf said, unruffled.

He gulped at the root beer. “Speed matters. I don't got the most recent phone. But if I delete all of the other apps, I can keep up with some of the newer tech.”

Scharf didn’t show any reaction. Axel hung his head for a moment. “You deleted your ability to call someone to play elves in New York.”

He straightened. “Fae. You know the fae are actually much more fascinating characters than elves. Elves have one culture and one set of powers, but the fae can be anything they want.”

“He’s not wrong,” I said.

The fingertip on my arm became a vice grip, and I shut up.

“There’s nothing going on in the middle of the night,” he said. “They don’t need us!”

“All evidence to the contrary,” Scharf said, throwing a hand around the room. “Mr. Peterson. Harry. You’re right, I don’t care about when you started and ended your shift. That’s between you and the museum.”

He straightened up, looking hopeful.

“But we can’t get around the fact that there is a million dollars worth of art in your garden shed!”

“It wasn’t me! It was planted! I didn’t do it!”

“I know, Harry. I know,” Scharf said quietly.

“You do?” Harry and Axel said in unison.

“They set you up, these guys. To take the fall for them. And now you’re covering for them, and you shouldn’t. Because they’ve betrayed you as thoroughly as one human being can betray another.”

“Well, they weren’t exactly *human*,” I said before I could stop my mouth.

Everyone looked at me.

“Never mind.”

Harry scrubbed both hands over his face.

“What happened before you left the museum?” Scharf asked.

“Nothing!”

“You’ve done it before, right? Let someone in. But it was harmless. You always stayed with them. No one would ever know.”

He hunched further in his chair.

“You didn’t mean to…” I added. He didn’t mean to let in a gang of art thieves so he could level up a game on his phone. “You didn’t mean to put anything at risk?”

He shook his head.

“Stop protecting the people who put you here!” Scharf said in an abrupt shift in tone. “Start trusting the people who can get you out of here!”

“Who?” he asked, suddenly hopeful.

“Us! We can help you. But we need the truth, Harry.”

I winced. The person who could help him the most was a lawyer. There was no guarantee things would end well if he told us the truth. As a champion bargain-maker, I was achingly aware of that. But I wasn’t here to help him see that; reason number 331 I was really not happy to be stuck on this task force.

“Who did you let into the museum?” Scharf asked.

“How did you know about that? I mean, I did not!”

I sighed. Master criminal, this kid was not.

“All those points,” Scharf continued. “All those quests and all those jousts.”

“Duels,” I corrected.

Everyone looked at me.

“Fairies don’t joust,” I said with a shrug.

“Actually, there is a jousting option,” Harry said. “But only in the summer. They partner with the Ren Faire. Um… Never mind.”

“Who did you let in, Mr. Petersen?” Scharf asked. “Help us get them.”

“Just for a second, and he was in my sight the whole time.”

“Who?” Axel practically shouted.

“And where?” I added.

“I don’t know who he was. Just a competitor. It’s insane points, checking in from a location nobody can get to! Except me.”

“Why isn’t this on the security cameras?” I asked. I knew the perpetrator could cover his tracks, but there was no record of Harry even approaching a door.

He shrugged. “See, the guy who runs the cams plays the same game, so we just cut him in on it, and he switched the paths. We were in a blind spot. But we checked in and got out. I escorted him right to the door.”

And became the perfect fall guy. I bet the psychic went invisible and let in her army. I froze. Harry had said “him,” not “her.”

The head art crimes honcho burst into the room. “That’s it. I need my guys in here. I need you—” He pointed at the kid, “To tell me who the hell he was and what you did with the rest of the artifacts and I need you—” He pointed at me. “To get out.”

“No,” Scharf said. “Did you plan the robbery?”

“No!”

“Did you know the person you ‘fought’ with?”

“I told you, no!”

“But *he* was a boy?” I asked. “Describe him.”

“I don’t know! Average?”

“Age?” Scharf asked. “Weight, height, race, hair, eyes?”

“Um, a white guy. Shorter than me, older than me, more built than me. Um… Black hair!”

I looked Harry up and down and tried to imagine someone shorter, fitter, and older than him. It left us with most of the white, black-haired guys in the city.

Scharf glanced at me, and I shook my head. He hadn’t told us “all he knew” yet. Giudice pulled the art crimes guy out of the room without a word.

“I reported the art myself!” he said, his voice getting higher and higher. “Why would I do that if I stole it? And if I did, where is the money?”

Scharf sighed and said, “How did you—”

I sat up and put a hand on her forearm. “How did you know the art was there? Most people don’t go out to a garden shed in March. What made you go out there?”

“My dogs were going crazy, barking.”

I frowned.

“I have two Greyhounds who are normally as sweet and lazy as any dog I’ve had, but they tore through the back screen and almost got the back door open trying to get out there. Woke my Mom who’s afraid of them under normal times, so I let them out and they almost ate through the shed door. I opened the door, and there was the art. I mean, I didn’t know it was art. I mean, when I saw the shield, I just thought it was another friend from the game leaving me a gift! But when I saw the rest, I called the cops! I thought they were pranking me! My friends, not the cops.”

“You didn’t see anyone in your backyard?” Scharf asked.

He shook his head. “I didn’t. But I thought I wasn’t alone, you know. Just that creepy feeling and the dogs were just crazy, but then they quieted down. And my mom made me call the cops. And then—”

“Stop talking,” I said. Dogs barking. Who was it? I knew who it was. It was right at the edge of my brain.

“You’re damn right, stop talking,” the agent said, bursting into the room again. He looked smug and opened the door. “You’re done.”

Giudice shadowed him, looming above him in the doorway, and nodded.

I looked at the kid. It still wasn’t everything. “Why did you think a shield was a gift?”

“Lugh’s shield. It’s my shield in the game. It looked just like it!”

“Do you have a picture?”

He shrugged “On my phone. Which you people took!”

I handed him mine. “Pull it up!”

The agent tried to take it away, saying, “You can’t give a suspect a cell phone! What if he sets off a bomb?”

“With *my* phone?” I stopped him by grabbing his bicep and threw the full weight of my magic into keeping him back and keeping the other agents from coming through the door.

The kid spun the phone around and there was the symbol I’d been searching for, the one I’d last seen in the heat of my breath on a cold window. The chime in my head deafened me. “Thank you very much!”

I released the kid and danced out of the way of the toppling agents.

“We’re not done!” Scharf said.

“You’re damn straight we’re not done!” Axel echoed.

“We are!” I said and pulled on her to get her out of there too.

# 15

“He’s a mindless dupe,” I said to Scharf as I led her away from the interrogation room. Giudice and Axel stayed to placate the art crimes guys and try to get us back in on the interrogation, but we didn’t need any more. “The perpetrator figures out he plays that game and lets people in where they shouldn’t be. Then he just waits for the right night to challenge, gets in, and then plants a few pieces in Harry’s shed for him to take the fall.”

“I agree,” Scharf said. “And it would have worked if you hadn’t *noticed* the thieves on the security cameras.”

I rubbed the back of my neck. They would have sent that poor kid to prison without a second thought. “I’m beginning to hate these guys.”

“But *who* are they?” Scharf said.

I pulled up the list of vanquished foes again. “Somebody on this list.”

“Somebody who isn’t the psychic,” Scharf said.

“Okay fine, I was wrong about the psychic.” I considered. “Unless the psychic is good at drag? Is it still called drag if a girl is dressing up as a guy?”

“Yes,” she said simply. “Let me get cyber crimes on this list. Though how we’re going to get a warrant is beyond me.”

I shrugged. “Or we could just fight them all.”

“What?”

“I could just try to duel them all. Do like the kid and challenge them to a duel at a specific place.”

She stopped moving for a full three seconds. “This app keeps constant track of where you are? In real life?”

“You can turn that part off. But these guys wouldn’t, not to be at the top of the board.”

“Where’s the winner right now?”

I clicked on the top guy, “Beowulf,” and tried to request a duel. “Um, I’m not powerful enough to know that.”

“I would think a goblin outranks a gamer in the power department.”

I laughed. “If my power translated to a video game, absolutely, but I’m not talking in the real world.”

“I’m going to talk to cyber crimes,” she said flatly.

“Okay, but can I not do that?”

“You just want to get out of the Hoover Building.”

An orange line helpfully flew out to our right showing me the way to the closest exit. “Yes. But I can also get started questing and dueling and jousting and whatever else to try and get the power to talk to this dude.”

She patted my shoulder. “Sure, you try that. I’ll meet you out front.”

I followed my orange line out the door, telling the security guards not to notice me to avoid any more conversations with strange FBI agents, and parked myself on one of the huge cement planters protecting the front of the building. There was only a little lip of cement and then bare dirt flower beds, so I used magic to hold up the earth in my body as a fake seat.

I toggled to the menu. The side menu listed a series of quests that you could play anywhere and those you had to be somewhere specific to accomplish. There were guilds you could join to team up with others and quest cooperatively. At the bottom was a message board. Something on the menu waved, and I dropped my phone.

I stared at my facedown phone in the dirt of the planter. “Cute.”

Gingerly, I picked up the phone, telling myself it was just lines of code. I chose the first duel available, playing an NPG character in the game rather than going visible and seeing if anybody around me was available.

The view changed to a fairy glen, and a tiny creature stood up. He was a pale man with wings, a stereotypical representation of a pixie if I had to guess.

He made a move on the screen, and I took a half-point of damage according to the menu at the top. I cringed as I hit the controls to fight. I chose to attack, and my goblin avatar bopped the pixie on the head.

He toppled over, and my life and power meters increased.

I chose another duel and fought another pixie and got a little bit more power.

“Orange.”

I looked up to see Scharf frozen in the doorway.

“I have four more eternal life points.”

“Please tell me you’re invisible!”

“Um, yeah.”

Her mouth opened and closed a few more times.

“What?”

She pointed, and I looked down. I was hovering four feet above the planter. As soon as I noticed, I crashed down. “Ow!”

“What was that about?”

“I have no idea!” I’d never magically floated before. My magic had failed me often enough that it was embarrassing, but it never did *more* than I asked.

“What were you doing?”

“I was sitting here! And playing the game. And…” I stared at the phone in my hand. I had just been joking about fake life points, but what if I was wrong? What if those life points constituted *real* magic? Which meant Fae Kingdom was *real.*

“Oh, I hate these guys!” I shook my phone at Scharf. “It’s right there in the name!”

“I don’t follow,” Scharf said quietly and walked closer.

“Fae kingdom. The literal fae kingdom. I bet my father’s box, the fairies did it. They recreated *Tír na nÓg*. It’s a magical place not in the real world where you can be anything and get anything you want! Does that sound like the internet to you?”

“You’re telling me the magical fairy game is run by actual magical fairies?”

“Yeah! Who are getting real power out of their users. It’s brilliant!”

Which meant…

“Oh no!” I pulled up the app. My last duel was still on the screen with the unmoving pixie in a heap. I poked at him. “Please get up.”

He disintegrated, and the game suggested a quest for weapons to duel the next “rank” of creature.

Eyes wide with panic, I went back to the menu and hit the same challenge button. A pixie showed up, but his hair was longer and his skin was blue. He did some fancy move and did a tiny bit of damage to me before I closed the app and threw my phone into the planter.

“I didn’t.” I backed up, heart pounding. I was pretty sure I’d just killed an innocent little pixie who had done nothing to me.

Scharf’s right hand was on her weapon as she dove for the planter. “What is it?”

I sighed. “Nothing *that’s* going to help with. In the game! I killed it.”

She looked around the planter, searching for the dead body.

“If *Tír na nÓg* is back, there are real fairies living in there!”

She sighed. “In the game. You killed a—pixie?”

“I think so. You don’t understand!”

“I really don’t.”

“The perpetrator isn’t just on the list of vanquished foes. He’s not just playing the game. It’s *his* game.”

She frowned. “How did you get from killing a pixie to the mastermind behind a museum heist?”

“You’re right. It’s a leap, but listen. I got power in the game and power in real life. It’s called Fae Kingdom. I think they mean that literally. I think they found a way to upload *Tír na nÓg* onto the internet. It was always a magical realm for fairies to live.”

“And why is it the thief’s game?”

“Because there is power in art too. Human striving. It’s really addictive. It’s what all of us are craving, Juya, Jack, me. Human endeavor can fund endless power for us.”

“Juya? The baker?”

“Forget I said that! And don’t get off topic. Yes, it’s a stretch. But every time I turn around, somebody is playing Fae Kingdom. And it’s weakening them, and the power is going somewhere. And they’ve managed to put doors to this thing in every major monument in the capital city of the country. Those kinds of symbols matter to magic.”

“We’re investigating a symbol?”

“We’re investigating a Celtic god who just got his home back.”

She blanched. “What?”

I shook my head and sat back on the planter. “It would have to be! Who else could create a kingdom, but a king?”

“You said god, not king!”

“What we now call fairies used to be the religion of Ireland. They were literally the gods of the Celts. It’s right there on the menu. You can play as Brigid or Lugh or whoever!”

“Whoever?” She took a deep breath. “How many gods are we talking about? Because we barely survived just one.”

Our last case involved a forgotten god. “It was a pantheistic religion. Dozens. Plus the heroes. I don’t know where they’ve been hanging out all this time.”

“Presumably Ireland?”

“What happened to them?”

“As Christianity took over, they faded from gods into myths into fairies. They became the creatures who stole milk and made trouble. They’ve been trying to get back into power and trying to save *Tír na nÓg* ever since. I think they’ve found a way. The internet is a magical realm. I think they’ve taken that literally. They’ve hooked up their app to the real world and are siphoning power from anyone who plays it.”

“Is there a fort?” Scharf asked, suddenly scarily calm.

“What?”

“You said it’s hooked to real places around the city. Is one of them a Civil War fort?”

“You mean close to the Potomac with some very special dirt in it?” I asked, suddenly excited. “Let’s find out.”

# 16

As Scharf drove south to check out more Civil War forts in Virginia, I trolled through maps in *Fae Kingdom* trying to narrow them down. There were a lot of Virginia Civil War forts, and a lot of them were connected to the game. It seemed to privilege public historical monuments that people could get into for free.

Scharf blasted around a truck with West Virginia plates going forty. Even I couldn’t blame her for passing that one.

I mapped the next fort on my list and froze. “Scharf.”

“We could bring in Carlisle. He was here before. He did all these forts.”

I paused and pivoted. “Don’t call Carlisle.”

“Why not?”

“Because he did all these forts before and didn’t find it. Why is that?”

“You’re saying *Carlisle* is a fairy?”

I laughed. “No.” I thought about it for a second. He did have beautiful hair and a terrible attitude. “I don’t know, maybe? Harry said he’d let in a ‘him’.”

“Carlisle is not a fairy.”

“Okay, but let’s not put anybody else in danger until we know what we’re looking at.”

We continued south on the beltway, Scharf weaving in and out of cars like we were in some kind of race.

“Scharf, seriously, look!”

I tugged at her shoulder.

“Hey, you’re the one insisting on no distracted driving.”

“Fine, just exit here.”

“Why?”

“The closest fort is coming up on our left. Fort…” I zoomed in with two fingers. “Fort Ward.”

It wasn’t just the closest fort. It was the fort.

I knew I was right. The fort was shaped like a five-pointed star. If I was trying to steal artifacts, I would want to hide them in a symbol of protection. And then I’d put a clue right in the front of the exhibit, just to thumb my nose at the world. Apparently, fairies and goblins weren’t so different.

I clicked into it in the game, assuming it’d be an armory since it was a fort.

Instead, I found a market full of arts and crafts. I goggled at all the listings you could trade your eternal life for. I tried to remember what had been in the exhibit. Did it match? Some of the glasses looked familiar, and I recognized a ceramic plate but couldn’t be sure.

I directed her through neighborhoods of small houses and apartment buildings. We were actually getting close to Scharf’s house in Arlington, one town over.

“I had no idea this was here,” she said as we circled another apartment building and a series of typical tiny Virginia houses. It looked like any other suburb with no indication we were blocks away from a huge fort.

“Don’t look at me. I’ve lived here for two seconds. I don’t think I even knew the goal of the Civil War was about taking DC.”

She offered me a sideways glance. “Or about *defending* DC?”

“Right.”

A grassy lawn came into view. It looked like a park in the heart of a neighborhood, except it had a visitor’s center and a bright white gate that said “Fort Ward.”

“I don’t think that was there in the 1800s,” Scharf said slowly.

I consulted the National Park website. “They rebuilt the entire thing. None of this is original. Well, except for the dirt. Hopefully.”

“Okay, then.” She had shifted to game-time Scharf, eyes scanning.

We got out of the car and hiked through the gate. Two women with baby strollers perched on expensive-looking small bicycle wheels were jogging and chatting around a paved path. Up a verdant lawn, I could see one slanted earthen wall topped by white wood.

A man with the leathered skin of a serious runner in blue spandex lapped the baby carriages on the paved path that wound through the green around the fort. I scanned the peaceful trees and sloping lawns. “Undisturbed for a long time. Trace amounts of fertilizer.”

Scharf nodded and cut across the lawn toward the fort.

As we walked up to the earthen walls, I showed her the app. “Look what you can get at the star fort.”

She scrolled through the listings, getting more and more excited. “I think you’re right about this place.”

“Yeah. What are they doing uploading their crimes?” I froze. “What if this was the entire point?”

“What do you mean?”

“If this is *Tír na nÓg*, what if they stole all this art to upload it? Somehow magically it’s all in an alternate realm now!”

“I know people are working on uploading real objects, but I don’t think anyone actually succeeded.”

“We’re talking magic, not physics.”

“Why would anyone want to?”

“I told you; human creations have power. Fairies are so power-hungry it hurts. Especially the *Tuatha de Denann*, the Irish. It’s been centuries of power leaking out of the island, first to the Christians and then to migration. If you were staging a comeback and saw a big exhibit full of art made by people who should be home and worshipping you, but instead are across an ocean worshipping somebody else, wouldn’t you find a way to get it back? Not just to Ireland, but into *Tír na nÓg*?”

“Then why put it on sale for people in the game to own?”

“It’s genius, then you get people to pay double for it! It doesn’t matter who has it in the game. The whole game belongs to them. The avatars of people who buy them belong to them. The art belongs to them. My god, it’s one big power suck. It’s brilliant! You’ve seen me trying to get more power out of bargains, right? And the difference when I make a really good one? Magic isn’t something we just turn on and then it’s there forever. Physics still applies. Well, magical physics.”

“In which you can turn real items into online items.”

“In theory. I mean, I don’t know how we prove any of that.”

“A judge would definitely look askance at our evidence.”

Finally, a break in the wall led into the interior of the star. The interior walls were all painted a blinding white. I felt nothing but a peaceful city park, not that I had any special *feeling space* powers. I looked around the white paint on this ancient fort, fighting a vague disappointment that the bad guy didn’t pop out and say, “Here is the loot. I turn myself in. You are brilliant.”

From the inside, I could see the misshapen suggestion of a star. Black lacquered canons sat at regular intervals, and white double doors interrupted the earth walls every few feet. We wandered around, and I saw a little sign. “Scharf!”

She pivoted from digging in the dirt with the tip of her boot.

“Look: ‘The armory’.”

She jogged over, scanning the area as she went. We were alone in the interior. People seemed content to run in endless circles on the paved path around the fort. “Can you get it open?”

I used magic to wrench the doors. They swung open, and I ducked inside. Scharf joined me at the door, one hand on her weapon. I smirked. “Thank you for rushing to my defense, but I think I got it.”

She blushed. “Habit.”

She dug out her phone and turned on the flashlight. A hole about four feet deep and five feet wide greeted us just inside the door.

I shoved my hands in my pocket. “Does that look like where someone could get fifty pounds of dirt to sprinkle all over a museum?”

Scharf turned off the flashlight and put the phone to her head. “Axel? Hi. How fast can you get a team to Fort Ward in Alexandria? Well, I just found a pretty big hole in the ground.”

The room built into the side of the fort was surprisingly large. From the outside, the sloping dirt walls didn’t look this deep. I hopped out the other side of the hole. A movement in the shadows startled me enough to have me jumping over the hole and out the door. Scharf had just turned on the flashlight to go back in, and I snatched her phone from her hand.

“Hey!”

I shined it into the gloom beyond the hole, and the lump of rags moved again.

“Oh god.”

I jumped over the hole with Scharf’s phone as she scrambled behind me.

“Don’t touch it!” she said. “It could be a wild animal.”

I spared her a fulminating glance and flipped over the bundle with magic.

“Dun!”

The little brownie was more gray than brown. He wore torn clothes, the thing he loathed, and was skinnier than ever. He’d first come to me in Juya’s courtyard, trying to seek my help to get rid of an unwanted guest. I guess the guest had tried to get rid of him instead.

I dropped the magic and grabbed him with my hands. “Dun, what happened?”

He mumbled something.

“Go get some water! And the bar thing in your glove compartment!” I shouted at Scharf.

I considered just grabbing it myself out of her car and pulling it to me, but I could only make things invisible by touching them, so far as I knew. I didn’t have time for hysteria over floating water bottles.

Scharf nodded and jogged away. I got him upright. His eyes fluttered, and he mumbled something unintelligible.

“Dun, can you hear me?”

He opened his eyes; his pupils were tiny, and his eyes were bloodshot. Scharf was back in less than two minutes, and for once I was grateful for the treadmill in her living room where most people put chairs. I grabbed the water bottle and a half-eaten energy bar out of her hands and flew them toward me now that she was out of sight of the path.

I popped the cap and let a little water dribble into his mouth. Once he got a taste, he drained the bottle and swallowed the bar in one great gulp like he hadn’t had sustenance in days.

He looked at me with gooey eyes and said, “My princess has saved me! And you are the guest! I should be feeding you.”

I slumped. “Please don’t say that.”

He coughed and finished off the water. “I forgot! *Peallaidh*.”

“Dun, what happened?”

“I did as you said.” He swiped his hand in front of him as if to say, finished. “I treated him like no guest.” He plucked miserably at his clothes. “And he treated me as no host! He gave me these!”

I touched a drying cut on his head. “And worse, I think.”

“He is too strong and too tricky.”

I shivered. “Him who?”

“He didn’t give me his name. Names are power. I don’t know.”

“Orange, look.” Scharf gingerly held up a sliver of bronze. It was the curved start of some sort of hairpiece.

“Dun, did they keep art here?”

“The treasure.” He touched his busted lip. “They hit me with the treasure.”

“Where is it?”

“When they locked me here, they moved it to the other side.”

“The other side of what?” *The city? The veil?*

“Don’t move.” Scharf followed her own advice, frozen in the hole. “We have to get forensics in here.”

“Dun, did you hear what they were going to do with it?”

“Win the game.”

“Game? The fae game?”

“Orange, can you…” She gestured wildly. “Get us out of here.”

I looked around and said, “Well, okay.”

I closed my eyes and lifted us all off the ground and out.

Dun goggled. “You can do magic now? No bargain?”

I shrugged. “I’m half-human. I bargain with myself and do magic whenever I want.”

“I want! Make me human too!”

“It really doesn’t work like that.”

I glanced at Scharf; she’d landed gracefully and was typing frantically on her phone. “We don’t have time to teach him about the birds and the bees. Forensics will be here in fifteen minutes. We’re not that far from the lab. It’ll take a bit more for a team out of DC to get down here.”

I looked around. Scharf turned in a full circle and pointed to the double doors opposite the armory. “Open those.”

There were doors set around the entire fort. This really had been some kind of glorified storage depot. I focused on them with magic and then froze when the sprinklers kicked on across the grass. A gravel path lined the fort walls and canons, but the empty expanse in the middle of the fort was green.

“Oh god, it will destroy the evidence! Stop them!”

I had a little bit of water power, but it wasn’t enough to deal with spouting water everywhere.

“They’ve probably gone off every day for weeks,” I said. She glared at me. “But let me try!”

The sprinkler heads were made of earth. I focused on those and bent the water toward the middle of the grassy lawn and away from the walls of the fort. I put up a hand before she could say anything else. “That’s as good as you’re going to get.”

With a gesture, I opened every door in the fort. I headed across the gravel path to the first one.

“Don’t touch anything.”

I narrowed my eyes, fighting annoyance. I knew about crime scenes. “Haven’t we got beyond, ‘Can’t touch this’?”

“We’ll never be beyond bad eighties pop,” Scharf said.

“Was that a joke? We are not beyond you never, ever joking.”

“Ice ice babe.”

“That isn’t even the line!” I said.

I turned to the door she’d pointed to and caught a flash of something. I peeked my head around the door, mindful of defenses, and then couldn’t stop my cackle. Scharf pivoted. “What the hell is funny?”

“The other side. He meant the other side of the fort.”

Dun popped his head out of his armory. “The human knows?”

“The human is a special human.” I thought about the task force and their partnership with my kind. “Though not that special, it turns out.”

We both froze as the jogger passed the entrance, oblivious to the millions in arts and crafts a few feet from him. Once he disappeared, I turned back to the loot. I saw the table and chair set with its pentagram pattern that caused such panic at the crime scene. They were half covered with bolts of cloth. There was other furniture, but most of it was smaller pieces all heaped together.

“Yoohoo!”

Scharf dove for the door before we saw Maria waving wildly from the gate in a clean suit zipped to her neck, though her head was free and her heavy braid was wrapped around her neck. A few more people followed her, all with their hoods up.

“Hernandez, you didn’t have to come yourself,” Scharf said.

“This is sort of my case!” she said with a huge grin. “I mean we don’t have cases. Or they’re all our cases. But since I was in the field before for this case…”

Scharf quelled a smile. “Good to see you.”

Maria bobbed her head cheerfully and then froze. “Whoa. We have to get you booties. We should quarter the grounds. We need to get a bigger truck. Don’t move.”

She bolted from the fort.

Scharf took careful steps toward me. “Should we get him to a hospital?”

I shook my head. “No. Really no. I’m not giving anyone a chance to examine any of us any closer. I’ll put him in the car.”

Her eyes widened but she put her poker face back quickly. “And then what?”

“Don’t worry. You’re not getting another roommate. This is home. Apparently, we have a thing for Civil War Monuments.”

“Like Whatever at Antietam? Just Civil War?”

I giggled and bit my lip. “We’ll see. If someone gets slaughtered at a Revolutionary War monument and there’s another sprite there, I’ll give you an answer.”

“DC didn’t exist during the Revolutionary War. We created the city with the constitution.”

“Oh, yeah.”

“Hello?” We pivoted to see the psychic waving her hand wildly. Scharf unconsciously spread her feet and crossed her arms as if she could block the view of the eight-foot-wide armory with her frame.

“How did she hear about this?” I asked.

“Axel probably told her? I told him to roll out the team. I didn’t mean the *whole* team.”

Dun said, “Eep!”

He scrambled over the fort wall and away in seconds. I went cold. I wanted to ask him whether she was the one who had hurt him. But Harry had said “him.” She was probably not our thief. Unless they were working together? I gathered as much magic as I could.

“Try for a poker face,” Scharf said quietly.

*Focus, Orange.*

“If she communes with the space, so help me god,” Scharf muttered.

“I shall not commune with the space,” the psychic protested. “I shall not open myself to the essence of such a terrible conflict as the war and the death and destruction it cost.”

Scharf snorted. “They never got this close to DC. This fort never saw action.”

I raised an eyebrow. “Look at you with the facts.”

“Lee wouldn’t shut up about it.”

“Instead I will read the flows. It is less personal and less accurate, but the only thing I’m going to do.”

“That’s very important,” Scharf said, her voice as dry as a California forest. “Maybe try first from outside the fort. I’d love your opinion of the overall, um, flows.”

She obediently drifted back out toward the circling path with her hands waving. I did a circle of the fort looking for Dun, but he’d disappeared. Why?

When I got back, more vehicles were arriving, and I went back inside the grounds to keep an eye on the treasure.

“I knew we didn’t need art crimes!” Carlisle said from the fort entrance. I rolled my eyes as we all pivoted to look at him. “For starters, nobody move. We can’t remove the treasure.”

“Why?” Maria asked. I guessed it was Maria, though she’d put up the hood of her clean suit. She was a head shorter than any of them, and there was a disturbing bulge around her neck from her braid.

“Carlisle, you are definitely not in charge of this investigation,” Scharf said.

Carlisle nodded enthusiastically. “It’s a sting operation. We will wait for the villains to come back.”

“The villains?” I asked. He was talking with a false jocularity I’d never heard in his voice before.

Not taking his own advice, Carlisle strode closer. “Ms. Sinclair. I know I saw your birth certificate during your previous arrest, but for the life of me, I can’t remember your real first name.”

*My real name?*

“It can’t be Orange,” he said, slapping me on the back.

“Don’t touch me.”

Up close, I could see the beginnings of crow’s feet around his eyes, the only sign of age in his otherwise perfect face. Did fairies age? In stories, they didn’t, but in reality? I no longer thought he was the slightest bit beautiful.

“Agent Scharf. Laura Scharf, correct?” Cool said in a lilting, oily voice seconds before he strode into the fort.

“Yes?”

“Excellent work.”

Cool was dressed in black, including black gloves, and his pale face had taken no sun in the weeks he’d been in DC tramping around battlefields.

Carlisle turned back to me. “I’m so sorry, what is your name again?”

“You’re not going to take Orange for an answer?” I asked, resisting the urge to use my hob powers to tap into my considerable annoyance to let loose the much more subtle and fun version of my magic. In the past, I’d pulled off a senator’s toupee, collapsed Scharf’s entire refrigerator, and other small bits of chaos. I glanced at the pile of priceless artifacts. Not the time.

“Come on,” Carlisle wheedled at me, “Here I am admitting I forgot your name. And I’m sorry about that and you’re holding out on me.”

I waited for Scharf to kick his ass, but she stood strangely still.

“If it isn’t Orange,” I said. “It’s, uh, Astrid.”

He slapped his thigh. “That’s it! Cool, it’s Astrid!”

Cool stepped up to me as if he’d been next to me this whole time, except I’d seen him twenty feet away a few seconds ago. My heart went from nervous to completely panicked in less than a second.

“Astrid Sinclair, it’s truly lovely to meet you.”

“Uh, sure thing.”

He rubbed his pale hands together, and I frowned. He said he was some kind of historian of Celtic art, but his fingernails were jagged, and his palms were roughened from serious manual labor of some kind.

“Everyone, if you would be so kind to step back from the, um, we’ll call this art for the sake of clarity. And close up those doors. How on earth did you get them open?”

Scharf said, “Orange used force.”

I gaped at her. Yes, we were on her vaunted task force together, but we’d had this discussion multiple times about not revealing what I could do unnecessarily.

He pivoted. “Astrid Sinclair, what manner of creature are you?”

“A strong one. I work out.”

His eyes flared with annoyance. “I did ask what manner, didn’t I?” He actually tsked. “What creature are you?”

I stepped up to him. “I am not a creature.”

“One of the tricksters then.” In the background, I saw the team struggle with the doors, heedless of crime scene protocol. One man stomped on a small ceramic tureen and broke it into a few pieces as he wrenched at the doors. I gasped and Cool simpered.

I went cold and then hot with the horrible suspicion we’d gotten the main suspect wrong. There was power in his voice like Juya sometimes could wield.

As they got all but one door shut, he tsked again and said, “And why don’ ye put all that nasty metal in there with the treasure? T’will be a grand offering.” His accent had gotten thicker. I was staring at a fairy who made no bones about kidnapping children and destroying artifacts.

Carlisle and Scharf cheerfully peeled their badges, handcuffs, and guns off their belts and dropped them inside the armory. Cool flicked his fingers and the door closed.

I resisted every urge I had to tackle him. He knew I was not human and didn’t seem very worried about that fact, which meant he was probably stronger than me. I didn’t want to start a fight I couldn’t win. He hadn’t hurt anyone yet.

“What other evidence have ye gathered?” he asked the techs, and they dumped everything on the ground. Why was I immune? Because of my goblin half? I didn’t think so; Cool seemed to think it was working on me.

*What is your name?*

Fairies were funny about names. As Dun said, they held power. And he had my name. I laughed silently. He just didn’t *believe* it could possibly be my name.

As the techs spread the dirt back into the ground from their test tubes, I stood frozen, heart pounding out of my chest with fear and fury. He was controlling everyone in this fort but me.

“And now go and fill the hole.”

The techs and the two agents convened on the armory. The problem was that the dirt to fill the hole was gone, now being picked over in the FBI lab miles from here, so they scrabbled at the hard ground with their fingernails. I glanced up and suddenly worried the psychic would come in, but the flows must have been interesting from the outside, thankfully. We didn’t need more drones in his thrall.

I’d always thought my name was a joke. My father—and mother—both said it was a joke. My mother, looped on painkillers after the C-section, took one look at the wrinkly half-goblin baby she’d birth and declared, “She’s orange!”

My father chose to take her literally and put that on the birth certificate. I wondered for the first time in my life whether that was the real story. Cool had Carlisle dig for my real name. The agent had probably been doing his bidding for *weeks*.

Apparently, fairies didn’t just need to know your true name, they also had to believe it was your true name. And no one would believe Orange was anything other than a stupid nickname. I was feeling more charitable toward my father than I had in years. Maybe he hadn’t been trying to get me bullied in every class I took. Maybe he was trying to protect me.

“Astrid,” Cool said, “please fill that hole with the people. How very careless of me to leave ye standing here.”

I took a breath and shuddered the way Scharf had and tried to match her stiff gate as I slotted in with the others. I tried to pause the sheer panic at seeing Scharf and a bunch of other highly skilled agents turn into puppets so I could dig through my brain for all I knew of the powers of fairies.

It wasn’t that much. I’d written a paper once called “The march of Christianity and the path from god to myth,” which got me an A, but it had been more philosophy than practicality. It covered how what we once worshiped became our children’s tales eventually. It was not a primer on the exact powers of those myths and how to defeat one if you happen to run into them at a Civil War fort where they turned your friends into marionettes.

There was no way to sneak out of this; I had to make him order me and the entire crew to go. I was terrified he would move or destroy everything in the meantime but keeping everybody safe had just become my first priority. Annoy the hell out of somebody? Manipulate everyone to get everything I want? Sounded like a job for a goblin.

# 17

Every instinct in my body was screaming at me to run like hell. Instead of an orange line showing me a single escape route, there was an orange blanket over the entire misshapen star fort up and over the wall. Usually, even outside, my magic picked the best escape route. But I guess the best route was one in any direction away from Cool. But not without Scharf and everybody else.

Okay, even the psychic.

And Carlisle.

As I scrabbled in the dirt with Carlisle, Scharf, and the techs, I frantically paged through all I knew of fairies. How do you break a fairy’s hold? Garlic? That was vampires. Iron? He’d locked it all up. He was controlling them with their names. Maybe two could play that game.

“Maria Hernandez,” I whispered.

She jerked but did not meet my eyes; hers stayed on her ravaged nails in the dirt.

“Leave this place.”

She ignored me. There was no power in my words. I ground my teeth, watching their fingers begin to bleed as they dug into the ground, creating a new hole even as they filled the other. What had he told me to do? Fill the hole with the people.

I cleared my throat to quell the completely inappropriate giggle clawing its way out. I used magic to pick up as many people as I could hold and lay them side by side in the hole. They didn’t notice, only kept digging as I layered more on top of them.

I glanced at Cool who was entranced by his phone.

“I have finished.”

He looked up to see the writhing pile of humanity.

“What did you do, ye daft beast?”

I coughed. “You said to fill the hole with the people.”

“Fill the hole with dirt!”

I blew out a breath, rolled the people as far away as I could, lifted a bunch of earth from right where he was standing, and dumped it in the hole. He was covered in flecks of sod and soil, and I’d used way too much magic.

“What are ye?” he asked, fury flattening his words.

“Cells. Atoms. Complex energy.”

“What creature are ye?” he screamed at the top of his lungs.

“Beast,” I said, and for the first time in my life, I owned the word. It was the truth without the information he needed; I’d heard many times that goblins are the beasts of the magical world. But there are a lot of beasts.

Cool started to pace and mutter to himself. “Can’t be a goblin or a brownie. No time for a bargain.” He pulled up his phone again. Was he googling fairytale creatures? But no, the lilting melody his phone gave out was familiar. Was he searching Fae Kingdom for beasts?

“Power of earth,” Cool muttered. “Not a fairy, not an ogre, troll, gnome.”

He paced away and back and said, enunciating every word, “Fill the hole in a way that is no longer noticeable with dirt from within the armory.”

I could do it in seconds with magic and those instructions were not open to any interpretation. But he didn’t say with magic. I dropped to my hands and knees and started raking at the dirt. He let out an inhuman growl and said, “Maria Hernandez.”

She got to her feet with none of her usual energy and stared vacantly into the middle distance.

“How did you know the treasure was here?” Cool asked.

Maria said in a monotone, “Scharf asked me here.”

He paced to Scharf beside me in the dirt. “Laura Scharf, how did you know it was here?”

“Orange told me.”

He spun back to me. “You again. What brought you here?”

My mind went horribly blank for a few seconds and then spooled back through what brought me to this fort at this particular time. “Maria showed us the dirt.”

He looked between the three of us with a groan and swung back to Scharf, “Who else knows?”

“Axel,” Scharf said in a monotone.

“Aha. I have that one. Alexander Axel. I need you to get him down here.”

As Scharf dug for her phone, I had an idea. I waited until Cool turned back to his phone and ripped hers out of her hand with magic. I flung it over the wall of the fort.

Scharf looked blankly at her empty hand and then marched out of the fort.

“Where are you going?” Cool asked when he looked up from his phone.

“To get Axel down here.”

“Okay good.”

When he went back to the app, I pulled on Scharf’s car keys, and they went flying from her pocket. I let them drop at the fort entrance so Cool wouldn’t see them. I didn’t know how far the fairy’s influence extended and frankly, it didn’t matter. She would either walk to the Hoover building or come to somewhere between here and the Jefferson Memorial.

Now for the rest. And for Cool to think it was his idea. Let him buckle this place up, tell us all to forget, and we’d be on our way.

“The hole is filled,” I told him and braced for round two.

He stepped back and stumbled on a tech. He kicked him, and I worked to keep my face in neutral as he turned. “You! Just leave!”

I growled; I couldn’t leave everyone here with him, but how could I wiggle out of that one? Wait, *leaves*.

I spared a glance at the trees all around the fort and pulled on as many leaves as I could find. I dashed them in his face. I’d once watched an enslaved djinni defy his master in just this way. Words had a lot of meanings.

“I didn’t want leaves, you nitwit beast! Fàg!” It sounded like fog. In Gaelic, I was pretty sure it meant to leave.

I swallowed. He couldn’t know I spoke Gaelic, Scots, or otherwise. Fog. I glanced at the perpetual circling sprinklers and pulled on a stream of water. My ability to manipulate water paled in comparison to earth, but you don’t need a lot to make fog. Or was I making mist? *It doesn’t matter, Orange.*

I scattered the droplets in the air as fine as I could control, mostly around Cool’s head to make a pretty rainbow in the sunshine. That delayed him.

“*Na dean sin*!” he screamed.

There are no words for “no” in Gaelic, or “yes” for that matter. You have to add a negative to any verb you say. I’m pretty sure he said, “Don’t do that.” I let the mist dissipate.

“All of ye, with your hands, close up the doors.”

He whipped out his phone, and again the app signaled as he muttered in Gaelic.

How was I going to break his powers? I could not command them, but I could bargain. I didn’t know what would prove stronger: Cool’s compulsion or my bargain. We’d find out.

I sidled up to Maria and whispered, “I’ll make you a bargain. I will distract Cool while you run away, deal?”

“Éist!” Cool said, his upper lip contorted with fury. It sounded like weesht, a very old word for “shut up.”

I shut up for a tiny amount of time and whispered, “He hasn’t told you not to say deal. Please, please, please say deal.” And please let there be a radius to his power. And please let mine be more powerful than his.

Through cracked lips, Maria whispered, “Deal.”

The others followed suit, slowly. I welcomed the clang in my head signaling a bargain was in place.

I slammed the doors with magic and said, “What is next?”

He glanced up. “Just give me two seconds, ye beast. I just need a little more power.” I fought to keep the triumph off my face. I’d been *right.* Just like my fight with the pixie, he was getting power from the app, sucking it out of some poor gamer as we spoke. “How can I help you?”

He met my eyes, an ugly look contorting his too-perfect features. “Do ye have a phone?”

I dug my phone out of my pocket.

“Duel.”

Why did it sound like I was about to “Drink me,” or take the blue pill? Or was the way out the red pill? *Focus, Orange, you daft beast.*

I navigated to the app, and when a little popup asked me if I wanted to go visible, I hit “yes.”

The techs started walking toward the exit. I took a breath; my bargain had worked.

Immediately, a notification came up that I was in an armory, and I could arm myself with a variety of weapons. I picked a knife that looked a whole lot like Kristy’s grandda’s. We could add copyright infringement on top of every other crime.

Immediately, a map of the kingdom popped up on my screen with boring things like suburbs and apartments greyed out, but the star fort glowing as an anchor of Alexandria. The closest person to duel was a little avatar that said Cú Chulainn.

My head buzzed like it didn’t have enough blood. Cool was *Cú Chulainn*? I’d laughed when I’d seen his name in the list of vanquished foes on Harry’s list. I wasn’t laughing now.

This was not just any fairy standing across from me. This was arguably the greatest warrior in the history and/or mythology of Ireland. Though I thought he’d gotten his head cut off at one point. If he had, it was a temporary problem.

The blinking app asked me if I wanted to challenge him. I was about to fight the greatest warrior over an app that I didn’t know how to use in the real *Tír na nÓg*.

I was the top of the goblin heap, but still probably didn’t compare to any fairy, especially not a former god. I walked behind Cool so the other folks would not be in his line of sight and hit the glowing button: I challenge thee.

I *hated* that phrase.

Cool pivoted and grinned. He looked like a predator—an enormously gorgeous, Celtic predator.

I licked my lips and said, “I challenge thee.”

With a stab of his finger on his phone, he accepted my challenge. Out of the corner of my eye, I saw folks creep out toward the edge of the fort. Apparently, my bargains were stronger than a fairy’s hold, at least when he’d run out of instructions. Good to know.

“A *púca*, are ye?” His accent was getting stronger.

“Do I look like a horse?” The black, havoc-wreaking, ship-sinking shapeshifters were the stuff of nightmares. Sometimes my anthropology degree could be a hindrance more than a help.

“It’s the Irish for goblin, ye daft creature.”

The screen said, “Choose your weapon.” I picked the knife, the only weapon I had. My avatar was a stereotypical goblin with ears twice the size of mine on a wizened face with a huge bulbous nose, a hunchback, and huge feet, for some weird reason. I was sort of happy that wasn’t reality, though the ears were more or less right. The little figure wore a rough tunic and had zero defensive armor or shields.

His avatar had considerably more going for it. He was decked out like something out of pre-history. His “armor” was a thick linen tunic that went to his thighs stuck to his body with leather thongs. A deerskin covered his shoulders. It still looked more like a deer than leather. He covered all of that with a leather apron around his waist down to his knees. Leather straps wrapped around his legs tying on flat pieces of leather for shoes.

Not for the first time, the weight of millennia settled on me. This dude was alive when we went fighting in cloth armor. I shook it off. His weapon was not made of cloth. He held a white spear with a bunch of barbs at one end, and hazy memory surfaced of the spear that inflicted wounds no one would ever recover from.

He flexed his muscles on the screen and in front of me, and I rolled my eyes, though every part of me was shaking.

“Challenge accepted.”

Cool and his avatar posed, his phone held in his hand like a sword.

He earned the name Cú Chulainn, but it wasn’t his true name, his birth name. What was it? I wish I had a second to spare to get to a search engine. Unlike many of the creatures I had interacted with, forgotten with the weight of the years, humanity remembered this dude.

I compared my life and weapon stats to his in a little bar at the top of the screen and knew that this was going to be a really short fight. Well, the point was not to win, the point was to live long enough for the workers to get away. I just prayed that death in *Tír na nÓg* was not death in the real world.

They could hardly harvest power from unsuspecting humans if they killed them for reals. I hit strike and was informed promptly that I had lost a limb. I flexed my fingers, relieved beyond words that I had both hands in the real world. Though I was suddenly very, very tired.

He struck with a fancy move on screen, and I lost more life.

He preened at that and struck again, menacing me in the virtual and the real world. Instinctively, I reached for my magic to defend myself, and couldn’t find any power—not earth, not water, not emotion. I fought panic. *You knew this was going to do something to you. It’s not gone forever.* It couldn’t be gone forever.

I struck again in the app and managed to do a tiny bit of damage while losing more life. This battle had to last. I couldn’t see the techs anymore. *Please let them get in their very steel cars*. Did steel work like iron against the fae? Guns were more steel than iron, right? He didn’t like those. Please let them get away.

He struck again, stabbing the spear straight at me, and cut off my head. For one mad second, I groped for my neck and realized if I could still control my hand, I still had my head.

As my character lost her life, I was informed I would need to be revived by a life-restoring potion from the god of healing, *Dian Cecht* at Slainge’s Well. At the same moment, a familiar chime sounded in my head, signaling the end of a bargain. They were safe.

I fell to the ground, completely spent, pain pulsing along my body and somewhere deeper than that where I could usually reach my magic.

When Cool flung his hands in the air in victory and turned to be adored by his minions, he saw they were gone. He let out an inhuman roar and suddenly there was a real spear in his hand that matched the one in the game.

I wiped at my nose, surprised to see it was bleeding. “I don’t know how amazing a warrior you are if you have a spear that wounds people permanently.”

He swung it with startling precision a centimeter from my nose. I could not stop myself from jerking back. “Tell me your name.”

I struggled to my feet. “Your minions are gone, beyond your reach. What do you want with it all? You’re a warrior, not an art collector. What was this for?”

“You will see. Though you won’t.” He swung the spear, and I was out of reach in seconds. I blinked. I was out of magic and yet I’d just jumped back sixteen feet. I looked around, shocked. Dun stood at the top of the fort.

“You will not harm the princess!” Dun shouted. I winced. I *told* him not to call me that.

Cool stood arrested as Dun laboriously hauled me up to him with all the magic he possessed.

“The Princess of brownies? You’re not a brownie.”

I flopped over the top of the fort, and thankfully hit a slope of sod-covered dirt. “I’m the *Peallaidh,* the damn princess of goblins, brownies, and who knows what else.”

I grabbed Dun by the scruff of his neck and leaped. Human weak, my ankles busted like two balloons on impact, but I took off anyway. “Dun, there are car keys in the grass right by the fort’s entrance. I need them. They’re earth.”

In two seconds, Scharf’s fob came hurtling at my face. Human slow, I missed them. They hit my nose, and I would probably have another black eye for my trouble. I snatched them out of the grass and hit the fob like pressing a button twenty times would make me safer as we ran for the sedan. I scrambled for the passenger side door, the closest one, and got it open. I threw Dun in before diving after him.

“Thief! Thief. He has my home!” He continued with other protestations I could not listen to.

I got behind the wheel as Dun crouched on the floor of the passenger seat. I floored it as Cool came running down the lawn shouting something I couldn’t hear through the window over Dun’s wailing about his home.

I wrenched the wheel to get us onto the street and took off; it had been years since I’d driven, but I had the basics. “We’ll get your home back. I promise we’ll get your home back.”

I slammed on the brakes, trying to control the fishtail when I saw Scharf, her familiar dark suit covered in dirt, walking with none of her usual grace determinedly north. I shouted, “Bargain: you get in and I drive as fast as you do. Say deal.”

She blinked twice at me. I glanced at Dun. “Can you grab her?”

He shrugged, bounced out of the car, and shoved her into the backseat, managing to whack her forehead and her shins. “Sorry, she’s so tall!”

“Shut the door!”

He slammed it and I kept my bargain, putting the pedal to the floor as I passed a sign that said, “Drive like your children live here.”

# 18

I didn’t stop the car until we got to Scharf’s street. She stayed quiescent in the back while Dun crouched on the floor in front, squealing at every twitch of the car. When we pulled up to the free-standing white clapboard house on a street of red-brick row houses in Arlington, Virginia, I slammed on the brakes and heard a curse from the back seat.

I got out and scanned the yard, which looked scruffy in the slow dawn of spring. Scharf rented the main and upper floors. There was a mother-in-law apartment that a lawyer rented at the back of the building, but he worked ninety-hour weeks. I had no second to spare to see if he was there or care what he’d think if he did see me hauling a semi-conscious FBI agent out of her own backseat. Dun scrambled out after me. “This can be my new home?”

“No.” A chill wound its way up my spine, and I realized I felt some of what Scharf must have when I had invited myself to stay. “This is my home. I told you we will get your home back. You can stay here for a few days.”

His face turned a much lighter shade of brown and hero worship showed in his eyes. I gulped, thinking of the seriousness of guests. “This is, uh, emergency housing. This isn’t a guest thing.”

His head bobbed and the tuft of white on the top whipped back and forth. I pivoted and opened the back door. Scharf uncurled and her head flopped out, eyes closed. I poked her in the shoulder. I wanted to make a bargain, but my magic was still flatlined, donated to Cool and Tír na nÓg. “Out!”

She opened her eyes and focused on me. “Your name isn’t Astrid.”

“Is that where you are in the timeline? Speed it up.”

I pulled at her shoulder until I got one hand out and yanked.

“Where is Cool?”

“He had to go. Come on.”

She got herself out of the car, and I slammed the door.

“Invader!” a squeaky voice from behind us screamed.

“Grimm? Not now.” The garden gnome was less than a foot high and dressed in an old-fashioned police uniform, a gag gift to Scharf. No one involved knew he wasn’t plastic until I’d arrived.

He brandished his little police stick and poked Dun’s legs. I crouched to reach eye level with them, a novel experience.

“Dun, meet Grimm. He is a guest!” I said, giving the little gnome the stink eye. When the brownie whooped, I gave him the same glare. “An emergency housing informal guest.”

Scharf stood still as I delivered this lecture staring into the middle distance. I fought a shudder. Scharf was never still. She had a treadmill in her living room instead of a recliner and spent the rest of her life fidgeting or moving. When the two little creatures didn’t go for each other’s throats, I stood up and snapped my fingers in her face. “Wake up! Hup, two, three, march!”

She started for the front door at a stumble.

Grimm forgot the invader to stare after her. “She’s got a chunk missing.”

“What chunk?”

Gnomes were also considered beasts in the magical world like most goblins, me included, though they were far weaker. I forgot sometimes that they weren’t goblins, which meant he had some powers I didn’t know about. “What do you see?”

He looked up. “You failed to protect her.”

“No, I didn’t!” I remembered the veil that fell over her eyes when Cool said her name; the truth stung. “Okay, yeah, I did, but at least I got her out of there. What’s missing?”

He cocked his head. “Energy? But not that. You’re missing that. You should have taken me with you. You would not come back in pieces. She’s missing will? Forward. Ideas. I can’t say.”

“I get the picture. Do you, uh, know if she will get it back?”

He shrugged and made for the flower beds at a running trot before diving between two bare bushes. The earth parted for him like water. Scharf reached the porch and just stared at the door. I reached for it to unlock it with magic, but I didn’t even have the juice to do that.

It took me an embarrassing three seconds to realize I also held her keys. I got the door open and all of us inside.

An open arch to the right led to the living room which is where I slept on the couch opposite the TV and the treadmill. I shoved Scharf at the couch.

The room also held a coffee table and a rack of weights. And miraculously nothing else. Scharf’s slovenly wreckage of a living room was the first thing I attacked when she let me stay. The kitchen and bathroom had received similar transformations. The only place spared was her bedroom upstairs. Sometimes the mess meandered down the stairs, but I just shoved it back to the top with magic and tried to ignore it.

Dun squealed and laid himself on the treadmill like it was a treasured throne.

I squinted at her. “You’re not hearing voices, are you?”

“Yes.”

My limbs went cold.

“Yours.”

I took a breath. “Cool’s?”

She glanced around listlessly. “He’s not here.”

“Okay.” I clapped my hands. “How do you break a fairy’s hold?”

“Bread and salt,” Dun said from his perch on the treadmill. “Iron. Garlands, especially primroses. Running water. True naming them back. If you can challenge them. Maybe don’t do that to the one who already defeated you.”

I cocked my head at him. I knew about the iron from all the folklore I’d been reading for my degree, but the salt and the primroses were new. “How do you know all that?”

He rolled his eyes. “Who doesn’t? How do you walk and talk at the same time? How do you love and breathe? How do you keep the fae from you?”

“How old are you, Dun?”

He held up one finger. “This many.”

I shook my head and almost turned away. “That many what?”

“I forget.”

“But you know how to keep the fae from you.” Which meant he’d been alive in a time when everybody knew that. I stopped that train of thought in its tracks and examined Scharf.

Iron, bread, salt, running water. I didn’t have any primroses. And in March, I doubted even the gnome could conjure any. I glanced at the rack of kettlebells and grinned. I grabbed the two lightest and hauled them into the bathroom one door down from the living room and put them in the bathtub. With magic, I could have brought the whole rack. But human-frail, I could only handle these two. I turned on the shower for the running water and then crossed the hall to the kitchen.

I grabbed the saltshaker and then a loaf of sourdough I’d baked. I looked at it mournfully, not wanting to sacrifice it for a shower. I dug through Scharf’s stash for her multi-grain, eight-seed monstrosity she called bread and put a few slices on the lip of the bathtub, shaking salt liberally over it all.

I went back to the living room and grabbed her hand. “Up you go.”

She flopped back.

I thought for a second and screamed, “Lieutenant Scharf, up and at ‘em!”

Scharf tumbled to her feet and a swaying attention. I hooked her arm over my shoulders and dragged her toward the bathroom. “You can take a girl out of the army...”

“What girl?” Scharf asked.

“Never mind.”

When we got to the bathtub full of kettlebells and surrounded by bread and salt, I unwound her and said, “In you go.”

When she hesitated, I pulled her phone from the holder on her waist and pushed her down to sit on the bread. She tumbled awkwardly into the anemic stream of the shower, her head narrowly missing a kettlebell. She didn’t react to the sudden stream of water down her face. I wanted to leave her there but was terrified she was going to drown.

“Dun? Grimm? Somebody.”

In less than a minute, the two tiny creatures poked their heads around the door.

“Make sure she doesn’t drown.”

Grimm saluted smartly and took up a post on the closed toilet. Dun solemnly grabbed Scharf’s hand and patted it. I looked at my crew and clarified my instructions. “Shout if she stops breathing.”

I went to the kitchen and broke out the baking supplies. The anti-fairy measures weren’t immediately working, which meant I might have to bargain as I had with Maria in the park. That had definitely worked. Except currently, I had no magic left to bargain with.

Fortunately, I was half-human and had figured out a while ago how to bargain with my human half for magic. I had thought when I first made that bargain that it would mean unending access to magic whenever I wanted, but as with all bargains, it wasn’t that simple. If I did too much magic and not enough living, I found my power fading. Or when a malignant fairy stole it all with an app. I wasn’t thinking about the app yet, or what it meant that there were old gods leaching off most people with a smartphone in the city and beyond.

Feeling guilty about Scharf’s missing health bread, I started on a batch of my nemesis: muffins. I loathed the cupcakes made of bread, but this wasn’t about my baked goods opinion. The oven dinged, and I put the tray on the top shelf, wincing at the heat I could have avoided with magic.

I ducked across the hall to check in on Scharf, still sitting insensate in the tub. The gnome perched on the edge of the bathtub with Dun holding him around his middle, his finger beneath Scharf’s nose.

“Still breathing!” he said with a squeak, and Dun nodded, almost overturning both of them.

“Thanks!” I said and retreated.

I checked on the muffins and saw them puffing and glistening in the oven.

For another ten minutes, I alternated between patting Scharf and patting the oven. Finally, I pried a muffin out of the tin and dashed back to the bathroom to see Dun and Grimm tussling on the rug.

“Stop that. What are you doing?”

“Gimme, gimme!” Grimm said, and I tore them apart.

“It’s mine to do, mine to do!”

“You’re a guest!”

“Both of you stop,” I shouted. “Thank you for your noble service. Go away.”

I shoved them bodily out of the bathroom and turned to Scharf, feeling the tiniest stirring of my magic from the baking. “Scharf, bargain. I will give you a muffin if you snap out of it. Say deal.”

“What?”

When nothing happened, I got right up in her face and shouted. “Deal!”

“Deal?” The chime in my head was my favorite sound in the world. I threw the muffin at her. It plopped in the water beside the kettlebell.

At first, the vacant look didn’t change, but then she sputtered and coughed and tried to wipe the water from her face while turning off the shower while standing upright. She splashed back down, and I hit the handle to kill the shower.

When she was more or less upright and her soaking hair was out of her eyes, she looked at me with a frown. “You hate muffins.”

I sagged to the toilet in relief. “Thank god. Or whoever.”

She raised one arm to watch water sluice from the sleeve of her blazer. “Orange, what the hell?”

She tried to get out of the bathtub and stubbed her toe on a kettlebell, screeched, reached for the side of the tub, and came up with a sodden piece of bread. She dropped it. “What the hell?”

I leaped from the toilet to the sink as she wrestled with the beige shower curtain. (The entire house was beige. One thing I could not get her to change.) She won the battle, ripped it down, and staggered out of the tub into the towel rack. She grabbed for one and ran it over her hair, leaving dark, wet spikes in every direction. With her eyes darting everywhere, she looked like a dog that ran through a sprinkler. It was bizarre to watch someone come back online, piece by piece.

She got her balance back first and then her wariness. “Orange, what the hell?”

Words were clearly the last thing to come back. “What do you remember?”

She looked back at the bathtub, staggered for a moment, and then righted herself to pull out the heavier kettlebell with a couple of fingers. “These will rust!”

“Scharf put down the exercise equipment. What do you remember?”

“You’re baking muffins and you’re never serious. What the hell, Orange?”

She fished out the other kettlebell and then a soggy piece of bread. She just cocked her eyebrow at me. Apparently, the bread didn’t merit, “What the hell.”

“What do you last remember?” I insisted.

She blinked and her face lost its animation for a second, and my heart stopped for the same beat. “We were at the kid’s house. The thief. He had a shield in his shed. We were interrogating him. And then… And then… We must have come home where you have had the weirdest idea yet.” She held up a piece of bread in one hand and the kettlebell in the other.

She’d forgotten all of Alexandria. She didn’t know we found the treasure, and she didn’t know about Cool. To borrow a phrase: *What the hell?* “The kid isn’t the thief.”

“We didn’t need your bargains; we had solid evidence in the form of stolen goods on his property. *You* said he was involved.”

“Seriously, just drop the kettlebell.” When she didn’t move, I said, “I’ll get you another muffin.”

I went across to the kitchen and fished the hot tin out of the oven where I’d left it. The pumpkin seeds on top had gotten a little singed, but they didn’t smell too terrible. I put one on a plate, grabbed a water bottle out of the fridge, and put it on the little round white table and chair set wedged at the end of the counter. Scharf wouldn’t let me paint them either.

“Well?” Scharf asked from the door. She leaned against the jamb in a holey dark blue shirt that said FBI across the front in faded yellow letters and running shorts, her hair still in wet black spikes around her head. I grabbed her by the shoulders and propelled her into the chair by the muffin.

“Orange, what the hell?”

Cool’s name was on the tip of my tongue, but I bit it instead. Even if I told her, what could she do? He’d just say her name again, make her throw away her gun, and do his bidding. The best I could hope for was to leave her out of this, go back there, and hopefully steal everything back.

I scrubbed my hands over my face. I couldn’t tell her. She couldn’t help with any of that and would just be a very dangerous weapon in the hands of a deranged fairy. I leaned against the counter, watching her inhale the muffin and the water. With shaking hands, I fished another muffin out of the tin and a protein bar she liked out of the cupboard.

I whispered, “Bargain, you’ll forget the last half hour too, and I give you these.” I didn’t even know if this was going to work; I’d never tried a bargain like this before and certainly never manipulated Scharf like this.

I was mimicking Cool and hoping a goblin’s power and a fairy’s weren’t that different. I shoved down the guilt; this was to protect her, not hurt her like the poor little fairy I’d squashed. “Please.”

“What did you say?”

“I said I’ll make you a deal.”

“Okay.” The chime in my head hurt. Slowly, blinking back tears, I handed her the muffin and the bar. A blank look stole over her features as the second chime signaled a bargain fulfilled. I closed my eyes. *I’m sorry.*

She blinked and looked down at the food in her hands. “You baked muffins? You hate muffins.”

I summoned a smile. “But you don’t.”

# 19

*I can’t believe I just did that.*

I busted out of Scharf’s house fighting tears. I didn’t really understand how magical bargains were governed, but I was pretty sure putting the whammy on an ally to make them forget a giant chunk of their life and an important part of their job did not put me in the good guy column.

I wiped my eyes and ran for Scharf’s car. I couldn’t mess around with public transportation. I had to get to the art and somehow get it back without tipping off Cool. And hope enough of it came back that they wouldn’t go looking for the perpetrator because there was no way I could fight him again.

“I come!”

“I come too!”

I pivoted to see Dun and Grimm standing in lockstep on the front porch. They could not have been more different, one in his starched old-fashioned police uniform half the size of the other in a shapeless sack that looked a lot like one of Scharf’s pillowcases. “Did you… Never mind. You can’t come.”

“You’re going home!” Dun said. “I come.”

I took a deep breath. I didn’t want to risk him, but I also had no hold over him. I was trying like hell not to have a hold over him. “Okay fine. But not you,” I said, pointing to Grimm. “You have to stay and guard Scharf. She’s in danger.”

Grimm rocked on his heels. “Because you took another piece out of her.”

“Did not!” I winced. “Did I?”

He nodded once, looking solemn.

“It was to protect her!”

“Okay.”

At that moment, I hated his credulity and absolute faith in me. “So you’ll stay and protect her?”

He nodded and stepped back to take up his traditional post by the door. As soon as he stopped moving, he looked like a lawn ornament. I did not tell him that.

I looked at Dun, “Well, come on.”

I got in and turned on the car, my eyes on Scharf’s front door. Dun clambered into the passenger seat. I gingerly pulled into the tiny street and accelerated away. When she didn’t come running out of the house screaming about grand theft auto, I let out a breath.

“We take back my home,” Dun said, standing in the front seat and hanging onto the seatbelt like he was on the subway.

I didn’t want to lie to him, so I stayed silent.

“Except you can’t take back my home. You are already defeated,” Dun said morosely.

“I am going to take back the art which hopefully means nobody will care about your home. Well, except the National Parks system. Or maybe it’s the Virginia Parks system. Maybe it’s a private donor?” I could barely keep law enforcement in DC straight. I didn’t have a prayer of sorting out their various historical preservation efforts.

Dun smiled and slid to sit, his feet sticking straight out in front of him on the seat. “You take back my home.”

It took longer to get to the fort this time, or maybe it just felt that way. I had a new respect for Scharf as I tried to get in the right lane to exit the beltway so I didn’t miss Virginia altogether. I made fun of her driving, but DC drivers were terrifying. After getting cut out of the turning lane for a solid mile, I had to channel my inner Scharf to muscle my way into the lane twenty feet before the exit.

Dun whooped with excitement, but I just clutched the steering wheel and panted as I turned onto a smaller street. It was crazy how tiny neighborhoods nestled next to highways. I wound my way back to the fort and parked in the lot by the visitor’s center next to a random truck, trying to hide the sedan behind it.

I turned off the car and stopped.

“We take back my home?” Dun said, his hand on the door.

Finally, I nodded, grabbed the keys, and got out. I stood with my heart pounding and Dun at my heels.

“Can you magically see where people are?” I asked him as I tiptoed toward the visitor center, telling everyone not to notice me and knowing it wouldn’t matter if there was a fairy about.

“Can’t that?” Dun said, pointing to my butt.

“Can’t what?” I dug out my phone. “Oh, yeah!”

I pulled up Fae Kingdom and hesitated. The problem with this game was that everyone could see me too. I deleted the app and gladly told the phone to wipe its data and redownloaded it. This time, I created an avatar that was a green gnome named Blue. I did the quest that asked if I believed in fairies to get the slightest bit of power, just in case, and went visible on the map.

I zoomed in and scrolled this way and that, looking for players. There was a fairy and a wizard dueling on the other side of the park, but I could see no sign of Cú Chulainn. Either he had gone invisible, or he was gone.

“Come on.”

I darted toward the fort and flattened myself against the wall like I was some kind of army person. I peeked into the interior. Empty or filled with creatures that could make a goblin ignore them.

“Occam’s razor,” I muttered to myself.

Dun peered up at me. “Who’s Occam?”
