“No. The simplest explanation is the right one. Actually, there probably was a dude named Occam.”

“Is he in the fort?” Dun asked, mystified.

“I hope not.”

I stepped inside, hugging the wall. How was I going to get all that art out of here? Where was I going to put it? This was a terrible plan.

The doors to both armories were still blown open. I licked my lips and peered around the first door.

Empty.

“Crap!”

I stepped inside. There was nothing but smooth dirt in both armories. The only evidence of the fight I could see was a pile of leaves on the ground where I’d tossed them at Cool and a bit of grass that looked a little trampled.

I turned in a circle. Nothing and nobody was here.

I scuffed a toe in the infamous dirt, and something clinked against my shoe. I peered down at the metal money clip with an artsy Celtic knot on it. I stood up. Of course, he wouldn’t have taken all the iron stuff.

Which reminded me. I looped around outside the fort to collect Scharf’s gun and any other metal things Cool made the agents toss. I hauled them back to the car for lack of anywhere else to put them and popped the trunk.

As I dumped the weaponry, I gaped at what was already in the trunk. A rack of shotguns and an arsenal of bullets, a vest, and a tote full of things like handcuffs greeted me.

I shook my head in wonder and shut the trunk. “How can she drive like she does knowing what she has in the trunk?”

“Who?”

“Never mind.”

“Is the treasure in there?” he asked.

“It depends on what your definition of treasure is.”

“Is the treasure in there?” Dun asked, pointing to the phone in my hand.

I laughed. Then frowned. It was as good an explanation as any. I consulted the map, clicking on the icon for the star fort. Nothing. Faint lines for the fort were sketched in the parchment looking just like any private residence or business that wasn’t participating in the game. “Gone here too. Damn it.”

“It sucks doesn’t it?”

I spun around. Two teenage girls stood across the lawn under the painted arch that said “Fort Ward.”

They seemed to be polar opposites of each other. Light and dark skin, tall and short, one wearing bright colors and one wearing black. But they both clutched their phones in identical grips.

“You used to get the coolest things here, but they totally disappeared,” the short one said.

The other bobbed her head. “We were coming here to clean up, and, like, duel, but nothing.”

“Zip,” the other echoed.

My gaze bounced from one to the other. “I’m sorry, who are you?”

The short one winced. “Sorry.” She waved her phone. “We saw you in the game. I’m…” The other elbowed her.

“We’re not supposed to say our real names, only game names.”

“Oh, I’m… Red,” I said. “Or Blue, rather.”

They took that in stride.

“Well, in the game, I’m Binky. She’s Griselda.”

“Yes, that makes sense. Those are completely appropriate,” I said, looking between the two of them.

The taller girl shrugged. “Awesome, right? It means Grisly!”

“No, it doesn’t,” I said automatically. There was an Italian Renaissance story we read in college about Griselda, which was the only reason I knew. “I mean, I’m sure it totally does. I have to—“

“What does it mean?”

I closed my eyes, thinking back. “I believe it means Dark Battle.”

The girls looked at each other. “Even better!”

Binky bounced on her toes. “Pick one for me!”

“Pick one what?”

“Something like that!”

Griselda elbowed her. “I told you ‘Binky’ was stupid.”

“Binky is just lovely,” I said. “What used to be here?”

“Oh, you didn’t even see it?” Binky asked. “It was an armory, first for weapons—“

“But then it became a market for, like, everything—“

“In rando Virginia, so nobody knew it was here!”

Griselda showed me her phone and a familiar shield. “I picked this up.”

Binky pouted. “But then it just disappeared.”

“We came yesterday and today?” Griselda threw up her hands. “Nothing.”

“Okay, thanks.” I turned to open the passenger side for Dun, trying to make it look like I needed something.

“We can still make it by Sunday,” Binky said as the girls wandered away.

“How? There’s no way my mom’s going to haul me downtown twice. How many pixies do you think we can squish between now and then?”

I straightened up. “What happens Sunday?”

They turned back. “The Games!”

“What Games?”

They both grinned. “You haven’t got an invite?”

“I just started playing,” I said, feeling vaguely ashamed.

“Oh, well, you’ve got to be level three. They only want serious people there.”

“Where?”

“The Teil games?” Griselda said.

“That’s not how you pronounce it,” Binky said.

“When?” I asked.

Griselda poked Binky. “Maybe that’s why they’re pulling some of the cooler stuff out of random sites, so they all go there.”

“When?” I repeated. They were worse than the gnome.

“Hey, we can duel you!” Binky suggested. “I don’t know if you can make it to three by Sunday. But that will help.”

“What’s on Sunday?” I asked again without much hope of an answer.

“You’ll lose ‘cause we’re both older,” Binky said.

“In the game,” Griselda confirmed.

“In the game,” Binky echoed. “But you get experience.”

I took a deep breath. What new wrinkle had Cool come up with? How was I going to get the art back from some kind of tournament with dozens of players involved? How was I going to get to level three in three days? Without killing any more pixies and god knows what other creatures who were just living their lives in *Tír na nÓg* being offered up as sacrifices?

The two kids finally ground to a halt when I didn’t answer.

“You don’t have to duel,” Binky said uncertainly.

I looked between the two of them. “No, fine.” So long as I didn’t have to kill another pixie.

I maneuvered to the app and clicked on the blond fairy that was a grownup, beautiful, exaggerated version of Binky and clicked: Challenge.

I had a flash of trying to explain this to NTEPUEL. *And then I challenged a child named Binky to a duel in the name of finding an art thief who probably also resurrected the real fairy kingdom online and is feeding the art to it.*

I’d forgotten with this new avatar I didn’t even have a knife. I made a move and my avatar attacked. I took a little damage. I had very little life to lose.

Binky winced. “Sorry!”

I shook my head. “It’s fine.”

She attacked me, and a weird thing happened. My *magic* responded. I felt it leave my body, not really under my control, and my avatar’s life went *up.* My gnome leapt and the Binky fairy fell. I ended the duel.

I looked at her, waiting for her to explode but she looked fascinated. “How did you do that?”

“I… I don’t know.”

Griselda looked between the two of us. “What happened?”

“She got stronger. In a duel?” Binky turned in a circle. “Maybe there’s still some kind of amulet or something around here to pick up.”

She had her phone in front of her, spinning it around, looking at the brown parchment version of reality.

“That must be it!” I said, my voice sounding odd in my own ears. “I just don’t know! I’m so new at this!”

Experimentally, I pulled on the trees near the fort, just to see how much power I had. It was more than before. I didn’t know by how much. I didn’t just pull power from pixies; I could pull power from *people* using an *app.*

I rolled my eyes; of course, I could. Wasn’t this how this whole thing worked? Cool had done it to me.

A germ of an idea wormed its way into my mind.

Cool had clearly set this up as a power source for himself. He’d drained me efficiently enough. But apparently, it wasn’t so precisely tuned that I couldn’t take advantage of it. Maybe there *was* a way I could beat him? At his own game. I snickered.

Binky and Griselda were bickering over whether such a thing was possible or not. I did not intend to enlighten them.

“Can I set a quest for you?” I asked, trying to formulate one in my mind. If this was going to work, I couldn’t just bargain with everyone in sight. It would leave way too much for me to do. But if I set quests for people to do themselves and still got power out of them? Perfection.

“Um, it doesn’t really work like that,” Binky said apologetically. “You can suggest quests to the game, but we could totally do a quest together that’s already in there!”

“Yes,” I said carefully. I’d barely learned to do my version of quests where a human held both sides of a bargain. Proof again, if I needed it, that we weren’t exactly the heroes of most stories. “Quest: You all do a, um, quest in the game and move up in the game.” I waited and a double chime sounded weakly.

It wasn’t much, but it didn’t have to be. I did a quick power check, moving branches of a nearby tree against the wind with magic to get a gauge on how much I had.

I licked my lips, and we chose the quest to do a dance routine and post it on social media. I volunteered to be a videographer as the girls bounced around the fort, away from the guns, because “they weren’t about that message.”

I uploaded it and marveled at the ecosystem they had going in this game with tendrils all over the internet bringing in new users and feeding them power on both sides of the veil between the creatures of Tír na nÓg and all the hapless humans on the other side.

When the girls checked their stats, they both squealed, and the chime repeated. I licked my lips and *pushed* on the trees again. I squinted; it was the tiniest bit easier.

“Let’s do that one again!” Griselda said.

“I bet we get *so* many views!”

“Too bad this isn’t a depot.” Binky swung toward me. “You get more points depending on where you are. But here is nowhere special anymore.”

“Is too!” Dun said from inside the armory. I braced, but the girls didn’t react, so he was still invisible. The brownie continued, “Names, places, everything counts.”

I froze. Names have power. I turned to Binky. “Okay, I’ll give you a name. You’ll change it in the game, right? That’s the new quest, changing your name to a cooler name.”

Binky squealed. “Yes, yes, yes!”

This chime was noticeably stronger. I tried to remember some other Renaissance-era names. “Adelaide means noble and kind.”

She rolled her eyes. “You don’t want to be kind in this game! You want to be…”

“Dark Battle!” Griselda said in a dramatic voice. “Plus Adelaide sounds like the name of a cow.”

“Fair enough,” I said. “Um, Regina means Queen?”

“Regina. Like….” Binky glanced down and started squealing.

“You’re right. Um, Mathilde means battle. Or mighty battle. Something like that.”

“Oooh, Matilda, like the movie!”

“No! Not like the… Yes, just like the movie.”

She navigated around the app with her little thumbs flying and finally hit something. A chime sounded. This time I didn’t have to check, I felt the rush of power in my limbs and grinned. I could *redirect* the power they were collecting. Whatever he was doing on Sunday, I could hijack it for myself.

“Orange!”

“Crap.”

I pivoted to see Scharf standing in the parking lot by a large SUV with a Rideshare sticker on it. I winced.

“I thought you said your name was Red?”

“No! Blue.”

“Um, right. It was real nice to meet you!” I said and jogged toward Scharf. “You should probably get home!” I suggested, not wanting them to hear any of this.

When I got closer to Scharf, I waved and pasted a huge grin on my face. “Good to see you!”

“You stole my car!”

A middle eastern man dressed in a Star Trek shirt and jeans exited the driver’s side.

“Hi, Hassan.”

He waved with an awkward smile. Scharf had met him in Afghanistan on one of her deployments. He translated for her unit and came over here on a visa for allies during the conflict.

“Explain yourself,” Scharf said, planting her feet, her face white and her teeth clenched.

I looked back at the fort, weighing the likelihood of Cool returning. The art was gone; the place wasn’t even a check-in point in the game. I wasn’t going to tell Scharf about this place, but it had become the perfect misdirect. “We found the dirt. We, uh, got a call from Carlisle. He said this is probably the place.”

“And so you decided to drive my car down here and investigate. Alone. Without me?”

“You were looking very sick. Not well. I was worried.”

“So you stole my car.”

I had a serious urge to make another bargain for amnesia, but I realized I had set a dangerous precedent, and it was not a good idea to try to whammy away any mistakes.

“I thought we were doing better,” Scharf said. “I thought I could trust you. Did you take my gun?”

I gulped and pointed to the trunk.

“What the hell, Orange?” She went to the driver’s door and yanked. I dug in my pocket and clicked the fob to get it open.

She marched back to me, swiped her keys from my hand, and hit the trunk release. She grabbed her Glock and slotted it into the shoulder holster under her left arm. She seemed to relax a little once she was armed again.

I stood there avoiding eye contact with either Hassan or Scharf, shame warring with fury. I hadn’t stolen her car. Well, okay I borrowed her car, but I hadn’t stolen her gun.

Scharf froze and dug in her trunk again to come up with another FBI Glock. “Orange, whose gun is this?”

I shook my head. “Yours.”

“It is not. How did another agent’s gun get in my trunk, Orange?”

“I don’t know.”

“And if I pulled some fingerprints off this gun?”

I closed my eyes and wiped the gun clean with magic. Fingerprints were made of oil, and thus my earth powers could erase them. “I would still not know, Scharf.”

She put it down and stalked closer to me, bending down so we were at eye level. I watched Hassan abruptly turn his back and start across the parking lot. “What is going on? And I know it’s not about the dirt or a joyride or my weapon. What is going on?”

A vivid memory of Scharf’s zombie face overlaid itself with her present flushed, furious visage. “I can’t tell you.”

All the anger seemed to whoosh out of her on an exhale, leaving her slumped. “What the hell?”

“Please don’t ask me. I really can’t say. I just can’t. I found the fort. Don’t ask me how. The dirt is inside. The doors are open.”

“But not the art.”

I shook my head, backing up as I did.

“Are you in trouble?” Scharf asked, her hands stretched wide in some wordless entreaty for trust or truth or something I couldn’t read in her eyes.

“No.” *You are.* “I’m fine.”

“Orange.”

I stopped and tried to infuse confidence into my words. “I’m okay. Truly. I have to go.”

And then I heard a terrible sound from Hassan’s pocket.

I spun around. “You play Fae Kingdom?”

He grinned. “It’s perfect. I go all over the city in this thing.” He patted his SUV. “So I get all the quests. Well, my daughter is the true master, but she sucked me in!” He spoke with a faint accent. In Afghanistan, he was a scholar and teacher who spoke at least four languages that I knew about, including near-perfect English.

I reached for his arm and pulled back quickly. “Delete it.”

“What?”

“Delete Fae Kingdom. Both of you.”

“It’s harmless. It’s just quests and fairy tales. We turned off the dueling function for her.”

Scharf looked between us. “What about the game, Orange? Isn’t that the one the security guard played to let someone in?”

So she’d remembered that much of her past. “Yeah. Nothing’s in the game.”

“You told him to delete it. Why?”

I went to turn from them, but if Hassan was in the game, he was vulnerable.

“If anyone asks you for your whole name, don’t tell them. Say it’s John.”

He smiled. “John?”

“I don’t know anybody else from Afghanistan, okay? Pick another name. Any name.”

I looked at his very metal car. “And don’t get out of this thing.”

“And spin around and cross my eyes and pat my belly and rub my nose?”

“It’s ‘tap your head,’” I said. “Never mind!”

“What?”

“The saying goes: Rub your belly and tap your head. But you don’t have to do that.” I normally wouldn’t have dreamed of correcting someone, but Hassan collected idioms like Scharf collected kettlebells, a leftover from his days drowning in US army slang. “It’s just a saying.”

“Just lie about my name and stay inside my vehicle if any stranger gets in the car. When do I rub my nose?” he asked.

I frowned. “There’s the song ‘Head, shoulders, knees, and toes?’ I don’t know of any saying about rubbing your nose.”

“But somehow the fairies are dangerous?” he asked, fighting incredulity.

I turned back to him. “Hassan, *I* am a fairy tale.” He was the second human to know what I was after the first case Scharf and I worked together where we’d desperately needed his help. I stood there, hoping that he would connect the dots. And miraculously Scharf wouldn’t.

Hassan dug out his phone and hit speed dial. When a female voice answered, he spoke rapidly in a language I didn’t recognize. Even in another language, the woman sounded skeptical but eventually seemed to agree. As he was reaching to hang up, I could hear his daughter say, “But Mom!”

As he was speaking, Scharf approached me. I didn’t notice and jumped when I saw her next to me.

“Did you find the man Harry let in? Why would that make the game unplayable? What is going on here, Orange?”

“Please, don’t ask me. We’ve been through this.”

“What did you do?”

That cut.

“*I* didn’t do anything. *I* am just trying to *help*!”

“Orange!”

I shook my head and ran away from both of them, unable to answer more questions without lies. An orange line stretched away toward a bus. We were far from any metro line.

“Orange!”

“Leave me alone!”

My eyes teared up, and I angrily wiped away the moisture as I walked, telling myself I was protecting her. This was for the best; she could not get anywhere near Cool, but it didn’t feel like that. It felt horrifying. Like I had been the one to betray her.

I’d sort it all out when we were finished.

The fairies were using these quests to gain power. I could set quests of my own and intercept the power. Whatever they were planning to do on Sunday, I might just get enough power to stop them.

I paused. I wasn’t the only one who could create quests. I turned in a circle, scanning for bright colors.

“Where’s the nearest goblin?” I asked my magic, not at all sure if it would work. I opened my eyes to see a perfect circle of orange around my feet. I groaned and said, “Where is the next nearest goblin?”

I turned and found a tiny blue goblin right in front of me. “Ack! I, uh, didn’t see you there.”

He looked down and cocked his head. “You called me?”

“I did? I mean, I just did. But I didn’t just…”

“I’m Blue.”

I sighed. He’d heard my fake name and come running. “Right. Can you do quests?”

“You okay?”

“Why does everybody keep asking me that? Yes!”

“Yes, I do quests ‘cause I’m a goblin like you?”

“Yes, fine, great. I need you to come to the National Mall on Sunday and set a bunch of quests. And I need you to find a bunch more goblins to do the same.”

“Can’t we just use the magical electricity?”

*Magical electricity?* “The app? No! We can’t use the app at all.”

He shook his head and dug out a phone that looked as big as a tablet in his tiny hands and spun it around. I remembered our message board.

With gritted teeth and fury tensing all my muscles, I grabbed my own phone and navigated to the janky website. I couldn’t help comparing it to the fairies’ sophisticated game. I registered as *Peallaidh* and started a new topic. “This is the *Peallaidh*. I need you to come to the National Mall in Washington DC on Sunday, March 17th to set some quests for a whole bunch of people.” I stopped typing. Sunday was St. Patrick’s Day, the archenemy of any Celtic magical creature. “Oh, they’re *vindictive.*”

I shook my head and kept typing. “Meet me by the Washington Monument at first light. I’ve been helping all of you; now it’s your turn.” I hit send and looked at the supplicant. His ears were trembling, and he’d turned pale. Regret immediately followed and I added a comment, “If you’re free and you can and you’re healthy.” After I hit send, I added, “And you feel like it.”

I closed my phone before I could uninvite them. “What did you want?”

“Nothing!”

He rolly-pollied to get away from me and took off down the street. I turned in a circle. “Anybody else?”

A guy I was pretty sure was human-made eye contact, looked horrified that he had, and hurried away too. Near the street corner, two people played Fae Kingdom, dueling each other. I was beginning to recognize the distracted look of two people twitching near each other, staring at their phones. I resisted the urge to scream and started running.

# 20

The Mall was already alive at 6 AM when I arrived. I slept on the 79 bus which ran up and down 7^(th) Avenue. The subway shut down at midnight, which I always found to be the height of stupidity for the capital of the nation, but the buses never ended.

I’d made it to level three of *Fae Kingdom* by believing in a lot of fairies and catching a lot of loot up and down the bus route as I passed, and finally received an invitation to the Teilteann Games on the National Mall the next morning.

The Teilteann games were real. They were Iron Age Irish Olympics/government gatherings/celebrations. There were sports celebrations like the long jump, running, and boxing, but also singing, baking, and lawmaking. They had some modern iterations, but not nearly the resurrection of the Greek Olympics. So it was a brilliant idea. So many quests of every description to steal from the participants.

I got real sleep because I’d transformed my appearance into a homeless version of Jack. I’d made the mistake of not changing anything one night and being asked for drugs and more every thirty minutes. Going completely invisible had different disastrous consequences. A guy had sat down, noticed me, and thought his schizophrenia had returned.

I got a text and looked at my phone, afraid it was Scharf, but the reality was worse. Astrid texted, “You coming today?”

I went hot and then cold and then hot all over. I’d totally forgotten it was Sunday. How was it Sunday again? I took a deep breath and texted, “New plan, I got us into a festival on the National Mall. Meet at the back corner of the Lincoln Memorial at 8 AM.”

The response was, “Okay…”

I thought for a second and typed, “Wear green.”

She didn’t respond, but I didn’t have time to panic about that. I got on the forum and searched for Dun. He had to have his own account to have known where to find me the first time. “Dun, I need a favor. You’ll have your house back today if you can get the gnome and grab all the cakes in a bag.”

I was shaking, waiting for an answer. Would he be up or on the forum?

After ten minutes of waiting for an answer and orange lines spreading out in all directions telling me how to get home, I saw a reply that said, “Princess, we will not fail you.”

I took a huge breath. “Meet at 7:45 AM by the Lincoln Memorial with the baggies.”

After getting off the bus, I walked down the green lawn through the trees by the Metro Stop. People milled around the American History Museum that had started all this. As I walked further, I saw crowds of people in green, black and white, and plaid all gathering along the mall. Was this all for the app? That seemed crazy.

“What’s going on?” I finally asked a woman in a green sweatshirt with a clipboard that looked official.

She laughed. “It’s the parade. St. Patrick’s Day? If you head over to Constitution Avenue, you can watch it!”

I nodded and backed away, bemused. Would the fairies consider the parade the enemy, celebrating St. Patrick? Or would they try and coopt them? *Probably both.*

I turned right and headed toward the obelisk of white marble of the Washington Monument, the top still flashing with an airplane red light in the predawn. It took ten minutes to make it onto the platform at the base of the huge obelisk ringed in American Flags. I chose the Washington Monument because it was the tallest thing in DC. Literally. By law, you couldn’t build a taller building, so hopefully, all the goblins could find it.

Most of the parade participants and watchers were gathering closer to the Capitol and the museums at the other end of the mall; the platform was relatively deserted.

There were supposed to be a bunch of goblins meeting me. I fought disappointment in the pit of my stomach. I’d told them they were free to come if they wanted to. Was it too much to hope that some of them wanted to?

I circled the monument again, searching for short, sneaky figures. The dais was deserted except for a dude with a fancy-looking camera. I gave him a second look because he’d crouched down, but he was just trying to get dramatic pictures of the flags and the side of the monument.

I turned to the eastern horizon to watch the sky lighten behind the Capitol building. First light could mean anything right?

I fought a surge of anger that cranked every muscle in my back taut and knew it hid a far deeper hurt. Why did my own kind want nothing to do with me except to challenge me or take from me? How messed up was this? I checked the message board and saw a few hundred goblins had viewed the message, and one named Harold had replied with a gif of a hedgehog farting.

How long could my pride take standing here? I waited until the sun kissed the top of the dome of the Capitol, told myself I was fine on my own, and started toward the Lincoln Memorial where the Teilteann Games were supposed to kick off.

“Where are you going?”

I glanced up to see Mrs. Henderson in one of her olive-green jumpsuits that matched her skin leaning on one of the flags, arms crossed.

I looked around. Other goblins were playing on the flags, blowing them in different directions, hopefully invisible to the photographer.

“Waiting for you,” I said with a smile.

“Why?”

“Because a bunch of fairies wants to steal all the…” I didn’t actually know what they were stealing when they played. “All the life force from the city. And all the art. I want to steal it back.”

“How can we help?” she asked.

I cocked my head; Mrs. Henderson had never been my biggest fan.

At her words, the goblins stopped their games and rolled over. They were multi-colored people all shorter than I. They wore normal human clothes and ancient garb I remembered from a class on the Renaissance and everything in between. There were probably thirty gathered. The only thing in common was their big ears.

My relief was swamped with sudden nerves. It should have occurred to me that accepting a position as the Princess of Goblins was going to involve public speaking. It had not.

I swallowed. “Um. Hi. Thanks for coming.”

One tiny blue one in the front blew a raspberry.

“Fairies are menacing the city, and we need to stop them.”

“Why?” a maroon dude wearing jeans asked.

“Why? Because it’s bad. They shouldn’t be doing it.”

“So?”

I sighed. Why did I think I was going to make an inspiring speech, and the goblins were going to march to my aid out of the goodness of their hearts? I was talking about *goblins.*

“Bargain. You help me this morning, and I promise to assist you with one problem you have.”

A green one picked his nose, ate it, and said, “All of us?”

“All of you,” I said and held up a finger. “So long as the person on the other end retains full control of their faculties and consents to everything you may get into your heads.”

“What’s consent?” someone asked from the back.

“Never mind! I have veto power, got it?”

“Well, I have Cheeto power too!” A particularly virulent orange dude said from the front row, thumping his chest.

“No. Not snacks. I reserve the right to say no. Just like you could.”

A tiny blue girl in the front row said, “We could have said no?”

I crouched down. “Of course!”

She grinned and started walking away.

“Wait! But if you go, you don’t get my help!”

One dude scoffed. “Your vetoed help!”

I looked to Mrs. Henderson who raised an eyebrow.

I rolled my eyes and resisted the urge to growl. “It’s a good bargain. You set a bunch of quests for a bunch of unsuspecting humans and get a bunch of power. And I help you.” I trailed off. It was better than a good bargain. It was an excellent bargain. For them.

“Quests for humans?”

I nodded. “Whatever you want!”

“More power?”

I sighed. I definitely should have led with that.

“Yeah, so you’re going to set quests for a bunch of humans and get all the power from them instead of the fairies! Everybody knows how to set a quest?”

One of the littlest snickered. Mrs. Henderson cuffed him on the head. He snarled and stuck sharp, pointed teeth into her hand. She shook him off as if a fly had landed on her. He finally let go, but the momentum carried him into a different goblin who roared and bopped him on the head. I used magic to stop the dark blue missile the goblin had become and hung him in the air.

“Stop.”

Mrs. Henderson crossed her arms and grinned.

“We’ll assume you all know how to set a quest.” Again, I fought anger. Could none of them have shown me? Would that have been too much to ask?

One of the tiniest raised his hand.

“What?”

“What’s a quest?”

I scratched my head, feeling mollified that I wasn’t the only one who didn’t know.

“A quest is a bargain,” I said. “Only it’s one your victim, um, partner, um, human has with himself.” I glanced at Mrs. Henderson. “Right?”

She shrugged. “When they want something from you, and you don’t want anything from them.”

I crossed my arms. “Somehow our magic helps them? Out of the goodness of its heart?”

The entire crowd erupted in hysterical laughter at such an outlandish statement.

I felt a tug on the leg of my jeans and quickly grabbed at the loops of my belt. I’d gotten them from a thrift store when I didn’t have time to find my size, so I had to be careful about keeping them up. I glanced down at the tiniest creature here, a wizened, maroon girl with her purple hair in pigtails. I didn’t know if she was a baby or as old as the hills. She said, “Power?”

“Lots of power.” I’d forgotten that most goblins weren’t half-human like me and able to bargain with themselves for their own magic. I could access my magic whenever I wanted to. Aside from their glamour and their ability to run away, they were dead in the water without a bargain. If they could give an unsuspecting human a little help and actually have some power in return, that did sound like a good deal.

I stood up. “We need to set quests for anyone playing Fae Kingdom, so we get the boost instead of the fae.”

One green dude in the second row scratched his head.

“Do you know what that is, the game?” I asked.

A few shook their heads. A few held up phones and nodded.

“Um, maybe don’t play it yourselves? The fairies are sucking the life out of everyone who uses it.”

One older goblin with nearly white skin scratched the blue hair on his head. “That explains it.”

“What?”

“My doctor diagnosed me with Chronic Fatigue Syndrome.”

I looked him up and down wondering what his glamour looked like and how he summoned enough responsibility to go to a doctor. “Well, yeah, it probably isn’t that.”

“There are already quests on the app,” one piped up in a lilting accent. I squinted, making sure I was seeing a goblin and not a red fairy.

“Exactly, we’re going to override those quests and use them to power us. If you have the app, you can win that way, or you can just approach a human and set a quest. Tell them if they win their wish in Fae Kingdom, they’ll get…”

“What?” Mrs. Henderson asked.

“I don’t know.”

The dark blue menace gasped and toppled over backward. I grabbed him with magic before he could touch one of the others. I could hear murmurs from the back repeating, “She doesn’t know? She said she doesn’t know!”

“Look,” I said, trying to reign in my frustration. “My dad may have said he knows everything, but I guarantee you he was lying through his teeth.”

“We know,” one of them said from the back, a girl; I couldn’t see her.

“Oh.” I dropped my hands and lost focus on my magic which let the blue guy topple into his fellow again and start a fight. “Stop it! So you want me to just lie to you?”

Most nodded enthusiastically. Mrs. Henderson shook her head; I didn’t know if it was in disagreement or chagrin. Slowly the others changed their vote and shook their heads.

“No!” one screamed from the back.

“Okay then! I’m still learning about quests. You set a task for somebody that *they* want to accomplish. That way your magic helps them do it, and you get power in return.”

Why did people play games? To win them? Some. To enjoy them? Hopefully. I took a deep breath. “Tell them they’ll get closer to winning and more enjoyment; whatever you think they’re looking for. But if you think of something better, tell them that.”

The pink fluffball tugged on my jeans again. “We can do what we want?”

I crouched down, getting a better look into her eyes. I would have guessed on the old side and more powerful than her size would suggest given the deference she received. I may have been the *Peallaidh*, but if I was reading the room right, I was not in charge here. The goblins were following me because both of these women were here.

“Did my dad say that was impossible too?” I asked her. “That you could do what you want?”

“Lied?” she asked, blinking twice.

“Lied.”

“Tell them they’ll get more of what they’re looking for,” Mrs. Henderson said at last with a ghost of a smile on her face.

I cocked my head. “Tell them that.”

I scanned the bulbous eyes and floppy ears all around me, fighting teary pride and gratitude. “Find the people on their phones playing the game and tell them you have a quest for them—to play the game in exchange for more of what they want.”

It was vague; I had no idea if it would work, but I had to try something.

“And stay away from the damn fairies,” Mrs. Henderson added.

Alarm spiked my pulse. “My god, definitely stay away from the fairies! Don’t go near them! Try to stay out of their way. Come get me if they try to get near you.”

I realized that I was about to put a bunch of goblins in harm’s way. This little phone game was the furthest thing from harmless you could possibly imagine. My stomach twisted. It was one thing for me to jump into the fray. It was entirely another to throw others in front of me.

“You can go if you want. You don’t have to!” I said impulsively as debates broke out about what exactly humans wanted.

“And miss all the fun?” the blue menace said with a bounce. I redirected his bounce toward the grass where he landed and rolled.

“Head toward the Lincoln Memorial. Most people will be behind it!” I shouted as the group started to break up.

They left on a cackle.

The dark blue one and his neighbor, a scuffed lime green dude, finally got the room to go at each other. I thought about trying to break it up but did not have the time or magic to spare.

“Thank you for coming,” I told Mrs. Henderson as I followed her off the dais toward Lincoln, barely visible behind another memorial.

She blinked twice, an unusually goblin-like gesture for her who tried so hard to look human. “This is a powerful place.”

I was about to ask what she meant by that but remembered something Whatever had said on the battlefield: why he stayed there instead of coming to this place and why my father liked DC. “Lots of striving?”

“The fairies don’t get to have it.”

She was telling me that she wasn’t here for me. I told myself that didn’t matter and held up my hand for her to slap. “Damn right.”

She just looked at it. “No.”

I put my hand down. “Okay then.”

“They’re not just fairies, you know,” she said as we marched by the parade.

“I know.”

“They used to be gods.”

“I know.”

“How do you always get involved with forces so much more powerful than you are?”

“They’re not gods anymore. The Celts that worshipped them are long dead. Now their gods are myth.”

She didn’t say anything.

“Like the rest of us,” I added.

“What are they doing here?” she asked, at last looking nervous. Mrs. Henderson was never nervous. “Why aren’t they hanging out back in Ireland?”

“The Celts came from mainland Europe. Celt is a Greek word. It means barbarian. They invaded Ireland just like everybody else.”

“And now they got some bee in their bonnet and invaded here just like everybody else? For what? For power?”

I nodded. “For the art? For *Tír na nÓg*?”

“Not good enough. We all have a purpose, us who are myths. If we can find out what the old gods originally did for their people, we will know their motives. What did the old Celts want of their gods?”

“Seriously, they were like any other bronze-age clan in Europe. Slavers, human sacrifice, the whole nine yards. Although they did allow women to divorce, which is cool.”

“Women could ask for a divorce?”

“Yep, and the grounds could be impotence, obesity, homosexuality, or infidelity.”

“So they were more advanced than 1950s America. Didn’t they worship nature, too? How do violent gods fit into that?” Mrs. Henderson asked as we started down the long path beside the Reflecting Pool toward the Lincoln Memorial.

“Some say the whole druid mythology and natural religion thing people get so passionate about now was just Roman propaganda, and druids were just lawyers. But we’ll never know. They didn’t have writing, so all of this is speculation.”

Mrs. Henderson nodded, a gleam in her eye that I didn’t know whether to appreciate or fear. “So who were the gods to a bunch of old blond dudes who didn’t want to divorce their wives and avoided their lawyers.”

“Um.” I squinted my eyes, trying to parse that. “Not exactly? And the Celts didn’t have blond hair. They just bleached it. And they wore a lot of stripes, not the white robe thing.”

“Nothing I know is true.”

I laughed. “They want power. That was true then, and it’s true now. And we’re not going to let it happen.”

Mrs. Henderson sighed. “Okay.”

I looked down at her. “Okay, what?”

With an air of long sufferance, she put her hand up.

“All right!” I gave her a high five. “Let’s do this!”

“Never do that again.”

“Deal.” A chime sounded and I groaned. “Not deal, deal!”

She melted away with a grin as a bagpipe lit into a reel that echoed down the mall. A phalanx of police officers came into view on Constitution Avenue where I could see crowds in green start to gather. The parade had begun. It was heading toward the end of the mall and the Teillteann games.

# 21

Mrs. Henderson and I walked next to the parade down Constitution Avenue toward the Lincoln Memorial. Behind the police cars, a group of bagpipers in kilts split the air with reels, followed by a group of people dressed in what I thought of as English fox hunting attire, only green, leashed to the largest dogs I had ever seen. They were furry and looked a little bit like giant gray terriers whose heads reached at least the chest of one of the owners.

Behind them, kids in black sweatshirts with a white swirl of letters danced down the street in black tennis shoes behind a truck blasting more reels.

I stopped, bemused to see a group of Asian dancers in beautiful blue silk behind them doing a traditional dance waving scarves. Next, a group of Latin dancers in bright South American colors and huge hats did another traditional dance involving a lot of spinning.

“It’s the same parade.” Mrs. Henderson stood with arms crossed a few feet behind me.

“The same parade?”

Behind the last of the dancers was a flatbed truck with a dude running for office for one of the DC wards. He was tossing green beads to the crowd who barely reacted.

“DC only has one parade. Memorial Day, St. Patricks Day, Labor Day. The same ten groups show up with a few new ones specific to the occasion.”

“Is the crowd always this rowdy?” I asked, watching a kid with two shamrocks floating above her head sitting in the gutter waving the Irish flag like it was a funeral procession.

“Open containers are illegal on the National Mall.”

“A stone-cold sober Irish parade. Ouch.”

“It’s better; the fairies won’t get as much power out of these folks.”

She cackled and pointed to a kid with a phone and jogged off to intercept him. I looked around too, mindful that I was supposed to be tracking down *Fae Kingdom* players, but only saw parade watchers.

I started back down the avenue as a different group of Irish dancers passed. They danced on the back of a truck in full, elaborate dresses in all the colors imaginable with bobbing curly wigs. They looked scared as the truck kept moving and they attempted to dance.

I looked at my phone and winced. I also had to meet Dun and Astrid and sweet-talk my way into a booth to sell our mug cakes. And make it look like I’d planned that all along.

Beyond the Lincoln Memorial, I found a series of volleyball pits and baseball diamonds, and the new Teailteann Games. Or at least that’s what the crooked banner proclaimed near the sidewalk and a very small parking lot.

I walked under the fluttering fabric and shivered. I didn’t know if I had officially entered some kind of realm or if I was making things up.

I looked around for Cool but didn’t see him. There were already a few kids on their phones. One was dressed as Robin Hood, who’d been English if he existed at all, and I hoped the fairies wouldn’t take that the wrong way.

Many others were dressed in green modern attire. I pulled the app up just to see what they were seeing. A banner across my phone screen matched the one above my head, and then a faux parchment scroll unfurled that said that I qualified for the games and listed a series of quests and duels to undertake.

I slowed to a stop when I saw the nature of the contests in the app. There were typical athletic pursuits like jumping, running, sword fighting, and archery. Presumably, those would be virtual, or we’d have a SWAT team on our hands on top of everything else.

But there were also mourning, chanting, singing, dancing, music, and storytelling contests, as well as crafts like goldsmithing, jewelry making, weaving, cooking, and baking.

I gulped. I had to be sure not to enter that. I knew I put magic into my baking, the small magic of home, but I didn’t know what a fairy could do with that.

I stuffed my phone back in my pocket and moved toward the vendor section where two human-looking people with clipboards directed sellers to various picnic tables. I waved at one and said, “I’m here to set up a stall for, um mug cakes?”

“For what?”

“Baked goods. Only they’re not baked.”

She blinked twice at me.

“I have a stall.”

“Name?”

I balked for a second and said, “Magic Bakes.”

The girl scanned her clipboard. “When did you register? I’m sorry, I don’t see your name?”

I took a breath and put a finger on the back of the clipboard. I knew I could obfuscate any object I touched, but I had rarely tried such fiddly work. “Can you check again?”

“I see a Magic Whales?”

I grit my teeth. “Not exactly?”

“Oh, here’s a Magic Wakes!”

“Close enough,” I said flatly. Apparently changing my hair to blonde was easier than changing the letter B.

She made a checkmark next to one of the names. I couldn’t worry about the dude whose spot I was taking. He’d deal.

“Booth 17.”

She pointed to one of the rickety tables close to what looked to be a tiny stage. I texted Astrid about being at booth seventeen.

As soon as I hit send, someone said, “Yeah, I know it’s booth 17\. I just heard her.”

I glanced up in shock. “Hey!”

Astrid stood loaded down with supplies a few feet away.

I was about to hug her but stopped short, remembering how we parted. “Hey.”

She was dressed in the same black suit with the same gym bag as last time. Today, she wore a Kelly green t-shirt instead of red, and her hair was in a ponytail instead of loose.

“Where’s the food, Orange?” she asked with an edge to her voice.

“It’s coming.”

“Yoohoo!”

Even given what I was facing today, I felt completely relieved to hear Dun and see him and Grimm hauling a wagon full of baggies toward us, not from the street but from the river. I recognized the red wagon from a neighbor’s yard and decided I didn’t recognize it after all.

As they got closer, I blinked again. They were both soaked. “Did you cross the Potomac?” I asked before I remembered my audience.

Astrid pivoted to watch them, and I wondered what she saw: the brownie and the gnome. I knew the brownie could pass in public, but everybody who saw Grimm move usually screamed. I grabbed the wagon and tried to hide Grimm from her.

“I wish you would discuss with me before hiring employees. It completely redoes our calculations.” Her voice was so cold.

“I didn’t. I left these at home and my roommates brought them.”

“Oh.”

I stretched my neck and pointed to the line of tables. “I thought this would be the perfect place to sell.”

“And you didn’t think to warn me until this morning?”

“It’s been a hard week.”

“You always have hard weeks.”

I didn’t answer her.

“What is this?” Astrid asked as we passed the tables of arts and crafts.

I slowed, examining the wares carefully, suddenly suspicious, but I didn’t see the stolen art. “It’s a Celtic festival. For St. Patricks Day,” I said because it would piss off any listening fairies.

We made it to table seventeen and started setting up. I squeezed Dun’s shoulders as he took out my baggies.

Astrid got out the microwave and the sign. She thawed as she saw the increasing traffic through the tables and around the sports games. “This is so many people.”

Grimm valiantly took up a post in front of the table and fortunately looked plastic again. Astrid hadn’t reacted to him at all, and I wondered if he could also go invisible.

I decided to let him stand guard since I didn’t know if he could make it home without one of us. As the minutes stretched on, I saw more goblins weave through the crowds.

Then I saw an equally tiny man in a red vest in a bowler hat and froze. Stupidly, it took a second to remember where I’d seen him before. In a video of the heist. He and his henchmen had stolen all the art at Cool’s bequest.

“I have to go,” I said absently as I moved around the table.

“What?”

I stopped short. Not ice but fire in her voice now. “I have to.”

“Are you some undercover agent?”

I laughed. “No kind of agent at all!”

“So they just let you on to robbery scenes because you’re nice.”

I considered a number of stories, but my human half wasn’t so happy about lying to my friends. “It was the FBI. They let me on the crime scene. I consult with the FBI.”

Astrid clenched her jaw. “What the hell have we been doing then? What is this? Some kind of infiltrate the neighborhood sting?”

“God no! I am a folklore expert. I consult on folklore! I am in the neighborhood because I live here. And I help Juya because she helped me get on my feet. And I’m helping you because consulting pays crap.”

“You’re serious.”

“Did you hear about the Smithsonian theft? I was looking at what was stolen. Telling them about the value of it. From my anthropology major.”

“But you didn’t even graduate.”

I sighed. “My dad was really famous. It’s not just the degree. It’s what he taught me.” There, that wasn’t a lie? “But I’m serious about this, our business. How many, um, folklore crimes will there be in the world? I don’t want to do that for a living.”

Weeks of meetings in the Hoover Building and I fervently didn’t want to do that for a living. “I want to do *this* for a living. I mean, I might have to always do some of that for a living, but I’d much rather feed people. Can’t I do both?”

I was shocked to see tears forming at the edge of her eyes. “I didn’t want to think you were doing this out of pity or secretly recording me, or I don’t even know.”

“I just want to bake magic cakes with you and take this as far as you think we can. This is my real job. That’s just something I have to do.” I wouldn’t think about for how long.

“And you have to go *now*—in the biggest crowd we’ve ever seen.”

“Yeah, but I’ll be back.”

I was surprised to realize that I really meant it. Even as I grew increasingly entangled with the FBI and my father’s world, I really wanted to keep a part of me out of that. “No bargains, no contracts. Just me and you and Magic Bakes.”

I stuck out my hand and she shook it. I heard no chime. I was relieved. This was my human half making this bargain. No magic was required to make it stick. Or maybe we just trusted each other, as Whatever asserted bargains were for.

“This time next week, I’ll be here, or at the market, rather. This won’t be here. And at Juya’s. And we need more money, you’re right. We need opaque bags, for one! And they need to last longer. And…”

Astrid nodded and smiled. It was a small smile, but it looked genuine. “I’ve been waiting for you.”

“I’m in.” I winced as I felt a tug on my leg. “But I…”

“We’re at a Celtic festival and they stole Celtic artifacts.”

“It’s also a really good place to sell a whole bunch of baked goods.”

She nodded. “It’s not dangerous, is it?”

I blanched; I hadn’t even thought of that. *How could you not even have thought of that?!* “You don’t play Fae Kingdom, do you? It’s this app?”

“I have a policy never to download games on my phone. And I have a child’s lock on all social media.”

“Oh.” I gave her an awkward thumbs-up. “Sounds fun.”

“Graduating early and starting a business. Those are fun.”

I couldn’t help but smile at the steel in her voice. She was back.

“I’m just checking out a lead,” I lied. And my goblin half decided that mendacity was the better part of not needing valor.

I tried to follow the little man through the crowd and heard a goblin call out, “Mind the leprechauns.”

I froze. That’s who those men were? “Oh, those little bastards.”

Collecting their treasures that didn’t belong to them. I caught sight of one and almost pulled my earth magic to squish him when I recollected the main plan. They had never seen me. Only Cool had. The point was not to fight the fairies. The point was to steal all their power out from under their nose. And then maybe I could fight the fairies once they were all nice and weak. I didn’t want to *hurt* anybody, just… I sighed.

I walked up and down the rows of booths looking for somebody playing the game. To my chagrin at this festival for Fae Kingdom, I didn’t see anyone playing Fae Kingdom, only vendors setting up. I noticed a familiar figure near the stage and ducked. The psychic had come to the festival. I paused; did she have anything to do with this? No, it couldn’t be. I’d suspected her at first, but this was Cool’s show.

“I help!”

I looked down at Dun; the little Brownie tagged along beside me. I looked back at our table where Astrid seemed in her element handing out samples, and Grimm stood sentry. I bit my tongue before ordering Dun back to the booth. Astrid was right; I shouldn’t randomly hire employees.

“Can you make quests for people?”

He scoffed. “Of course. What’s a quest?”

I stopped. “What do you do for people?”

He shrugged. “Give them what they want so they give me what I want.”

“That’s a bargain,” I said automatically. Then I thought about it for a second. “Though I guess that’s also just life.” Didn’t everybody try to get what they want?

“I also tell them they can have what they want, even if I don’t want anything from them.” He scratched the top of his head. “They seem to need to hear that, even if they could have totally done everything on their own.”

I pointed at him. “That! That is a quest! We have to set a quest for everyone playing Fae Kingdom.”

His hand dropped. “Why?”

“So we can get the magic from the game instead of the fairies.”

“Why?”

“So they don’t get more powerful.”

“Why?”

I paused. Why was it so important that I stop Cool? Because I had to admit to myself, this had gone far beyond getting the art back. I had to get the art back, but I wanted him gone. “Because they kidnap children and manipulate people and they’re stealing people’s life force without their consent.”

He cocked his head. “What’s consent?”

I sighed. Was I going to have to put together a sex ed course for goblins? “It’s where you don’t do anything to someone else until you ask them and they agree to… having it done? Also doing it?” I was going to have to find someone *else* to run Health 101 for goblins.

He nodded sagely. “Another quest.”

“What? No! Consent is not a quest.”

“You give them permission to get what they can’t give themselves permission to get themselves.”

I stopped. “Hunh. Can you help?”

He shrugged. “Sure!”

“No more whys?” I asked.

He scampered off.

“Be careful!”

He disappeared into the crowd, and I walked back up along the line of booths, still keeping my head down, so I cringed when I heard, “Orange!”

I spun around, grasping at magic and expecting a fairy. I saw a skinny kid at one of the counters waving furiously. “Kristy!”

“Any leads?”

A sad smile lit my face. “We’re very close.”

“Oh, well, okay.”

“Thank you for all your help with the investigation.”

“What help? I wish I could do more!” She fiddled with her phone. I heard the familiar twill of notes but couldn’t bring myself to bargain with her. I wished she wouldn’t play at all but didn’t know how to stop her.

“I’m hoping I can win the metal works competition.”

I looked at the collection of wired knots and curlicues laying across her table. “I haven’t seen better.”

I turned to go, and she added, “Oh, I found what the symbol meant. The one you asked me about.”

She picked up the shield with the knot in the center and six blades around the edge. I shivered. “Who?”

“It’s Lugh’s shield. He’s one of the *Tuatha Dé Danann*.”

I stared at the symbol I’d seen in the steam at two crime scenes, desperately trying to remember what I knew about Lugh.

“That means Folk of the Goddess Danu,” she said. “Gods of ancient Ireland, though now mostly referred to as the fae folk.”

“Since Christian monks began to write down their stories,” I added quietly, sucking in a breath. “Now they’re just folklore.”

“He is the god of —”

“Arts and crafts,” I said with a goblin cackle that had certain heads in the crowd whipping my way.

“Well, and justice and sun and storms, though I don’t know how he can be both of those. And skills in general.” She held out the little wire shield. “You take it.”

“Oh no, I couldn’t.”

“Please, for all your help. It’s little enough.”

I picked it up gingerly. “Thank you.” I looked around at a festival to all sorts of skills. “He had a son, didn’t he?”

Kristy laughed this time. “They all have a billion relatives.”

I shook it off and smiled at her again, forcing my face to contort. “He had a son, a warrior. This whole thing is a Father’s Day gift!”

I tried to remember what Lugh was supposed to look like—fair where his son Cú Chulainn was dark, but equally Irish pale. A god. A former god, a forgotten god. Did that make him more or less dangerous?

This was a deadly game. And I needed to look the part. I ducked down behind the next table where someone had stacks of misshapen bread for sale. Of course, the Irish sports contest would include soda bread competitions. Of course, it would.

I let my glamour drop so my curly, red Celtic hair could be free in all its glory. For the very first time in my life, I pulled it away from my floppy ears with one of the twist ties by the soda bread table to seal their bags. I put a little bit of glamour back to smooth the ragged edges of my ears so they looked more artistic and fake than like the birth defect I’d always thought they were.

I turned my t-shirt into a cloak of dark green, figuring people would be more likely to accept a quest from some kind of forest nymph than a kid in orange leather. I rolled my neck, fighting to keep loose. I could maintain my usual look even in my sleep, but I’d never tried anything like this for long, and it took energy and focus. I hoped the tradeoff would be worth it.

The jaunty music and the party atmosphere faded from my awareness. I flagged down my first quester.

# 22

I approached the first kid I saw with his eyes on his phone. He had a rash of acne and greasy hair, though in the game he played a huge ogre.

“A quest for ye.” I tried for my father’s Scottish brogue but ended up sounding like the actor from Star Trek aping a Scotsman. I dropped it. “Defeat your foe and raise higher in the, uh, competition.”

He blinked at me. “I’m just buying bread. I skipped breakfast.”

I nodded solemnly. “Buy the bread and be powered for your next duel.”

“I don’t duel. My mom won’t let me. I just do the crafting quests.”

“Work with me, kid. Do you accept my quest? Your next move will bring you glory of some kind to be determined.”

He cocked his head. “Okay.”

A two-toned chime sounded in my head; the quest had begun.

He hit a button, and the girl at the soda bread stand handed over a slice teeming with butter and honey. Apparently, they could get money from the app. I fought a shudder; the last thing we needed was fairy gold laundering on top of every other crime.

As he bit into the bread with a groan, I fought professional grumpiness. It looked far better than a microwaved mug cake. If I’d actually baked something real, I could have totally won.

Once he was licking the honey from his hands, I felt a rush of power. A teeny, tiny, minuscule rush of power. On the one hand, I was happy Cool didn’t get it, but I was going to have to get better at quests ASAP. Bargains were far simpler.

I spun and found another guy in jeans and a green t-shirt a little older than the first, so hopefully, he was allowed to duel by his mother. “A quest for ye, laddie.”

The light that lit his eyes was promising. “Cool! What?”

Cool. I looked around quickly, and when I didn’t see the vengeful fairy, I sighed. He had the *worst* nickname.

I spun back. “Complete this duel within, uh, the next five minutes, and receive a bonus.”

“What bonus?”

“Uh, my thanks.” One chime signaled a bargain in my head. Damn it.

When his phone chimed, and he pumped a fist in the air, I said, “Thanks.”

The chime sounded, and I turned away.

His arm snaked around my middle, and his lips landed close to my ear before I could move. “Where are you going, elf?”

I shoved him off with more magic than I intended out of sheer shock. It sent him flying four feet away sprawled on his back. “I’m not an elf. I’m a goblin.”

I walked away, wiping at my cheek, cursing myself for letting my guard down and forgetting that human didn’t mean harmless.

I approached a teenage girl next who wore a forest green, medieval dress at least four centuries later than *Fae Kingdom*. She clicked on her phone faster than I’d ever seen anyone type. “Quest: you do all the quests you want in the game and will not be harassed the rest of the day.”

She cocked an eyebrow “You can guarantee that? Because I’ve already been felt up by a leprechaun.”

I stood to my full, unimpressive height and said, “Yeah, I can. Do you accept?”

“Sure.”

A single chime sounded, which meant I’d made another bargain. I couldn’t really regret that.

“Do we shake hands?” she asked.

“No need.” I grinned and tripped away.

“You’re really bad at this.”

I stopped short, almost falling over to avoid bumping Mrs. Henderson.

“I didn’t even know a quest was a thing until last week!”

She guffawed; it wasn’t even a cackle, just gut-deep amusement. “Goblins have been sitting at crossroads giving quests to humans since the beginning of time.”

I squinted at her. “And by the beginning of time, do you mean…”

“And our princess can’t even figure out what one human wants without a fairy app to help her.” She gripped her sides as she laughed, her face lightening to a pastel olive.

“Yeah, I know,” I said, “It’s hilarious. I’m incompetent. Can we delay this conversation until there’s not a fairy sucking half the population dry, and the art is back in the museum?”

“They don’t have to like the quest or want to do it.” Her face returned to its dark olive green.

“What?” I asked, genuinely startled.

She shrugged. “A bargain is for when *you* want something.”

“No, a bargain is to prove whether someone is trustworthy.”

Mrs. Henderson snickered again. “You’ve been talking to that old goblin at the memorial. The old ones get batty.”

I opened my mouth to defend Whatever, but I did not want to get derailed. “So why do we get power from their quest?”

“A quest is for when *they* want something. It’s only right they pay for it.” She flung a hand at the milling crowds buying bread and mug cakes and other trinkets.

I translated that statement: A quest is for when somebody needed to trust themself if we were sticking with the naive version of the universe. “Oh, only right, right.”

Mrs. Henderson grinned and nodded so hard her ears flapped. “They’ll tell you what they want; you set the task for them to get it, whether they like that or not. It’s more power when they don’t, actually.”

“Gross. I’m still not clear how our magic helps with their quest. We don’t stand there and get it for them. I don’t feel magic going out to them.”

Her eyes fluttered to the horizon where the sun rose halfway up the sky. I wasn’t sure how long this thing lasted. “Our magic smooths the world out for them to get it for themselves. There’s a great deal more that goes into the success of an endeavor than a human wanting it or even working for it. We hold the chaos at bay. It’s only right we get a little something for that!”

I was still not convinced we should get something out of somebody else’s effort. Not for the first time, I remembered we weren’t exactly the heroes of most stories.

Mrs. Henderson tapped her chin. “How do I put this? We make better luck for them. The stars align. The dice land on snake eyes a little more often.”

“I think snake eyes is actually a bad hand. Or roll.” But I thought I understood. We changed the odds a little with magic. “We make snake eyes less possible. Metaphorically speaking.”

She cackled. “Goblins are a metaphor speaking.”

I smiled, wishing Scharf was here. We’d had an ongoing debate about fiction, folklore, and just what all of these fairy tale creatures really were, including me. I’d have to add metaphor to the list.

Mrs. Henderson abandoned me to go after a dude on his phone.

I approached a grown man who looked a little sheepish amidst the largely teenage crowd, though he was far from the oldest tapping on his phone. “A quest for ye, sir.”

He glanced up, his face going pale. “Oh, like, not in the phone?”

He looked pretty perturbed by that possibility.

An impish impulse overtook me. Not impish—goblinish. “Speak to five people in real life today and you’ll, um, improve your quests.” The two-toned chime sounded, and I fought the urge to pump a fist.

He gulped and his hand started to shake.

“Only if you want to!” I added quickly.

His Adam’s apple bobbed spastically as he nodded. “It’s why I came. I thought with the app, I could…” He looked around, almost jumping out of the way of a running group of kids. “They’re all so young though.”

I pointed to a woman at the far edge of the crowd dressed in a hippie skirt and a bright yellow top about his age. “New quest. Talk to her.” A stronger chime sounded.

He shook his head, then nodded, then shook his head. I gestured and put magic behind it to push him in her direction.

He walked closer and closer, then veered away just as another goblin backed away from another quester straight into his path. The little creature tripped him and sent him sprawling at her feet. As soon as he opened his mouth, the two-toned chime repeated itself in reverse, and I felt a rush of considerably more power.

The goblin picked himself up, looking confused. I realized we didn’t exactly keep the chaos at bay, just found the most beneficial bit to throw at a problem. A part of me was still freaked out that I had very little control over the magic I unleashed, but a part of me watched the man offer a shaking hand to the woman who was smiling like her whole day was made and couldn’t feel sorry.

I moseyed toward the stage and the crowds there watching a heartfelt singer belt out what sounded like Mozart’s *Requiem*. I grinned; if there were fairies listening, they probably wouldn’t like that.

I sidled up to an older woman with a fringe of curly gray hair and a younger woman who looked like a smoother, taller version of her. The older woman was whispering, “The original games were a mourning feast. The whole day would be filled with laments.”

I took a closer look at the singer on the stage. Would they appreciate the lament, or would they hate a requiem to a Christian god? I couldn’t guess.

I focused on the pair. “A quest for the both of you, er, ye.”

The old woman grinned, and the girl rolled her eyes. I had something totally different planned when I turned to the granddaughter and said, “Sing.”

She coughed and shook her head.

“You’re so good,” the old woman said patting her again.

“Sing,” I repeated, “And you’ll not hesitate to speak.” *Where is this coming from?*

I glanced at the granny who had tears sparkling in her eyes and was squeezing her grandaughter’s hands. *How do I know she’s her grandmother?*

As the requiem wrapped to scattered applause, the announcer climbed back onto the stage, and the girl said, “Okay, fine!”

She slouched toward the stage and whispered to the announcer who turned to the mic and said, “We have a last-minute entry. Keira O’Halloran.”

The kid went to the mic and sang, “La, la, la.”

She clearly meant to walk away, but the audience clapped. She had a light but powerful voice that immediately commanded attention. She stepped back to the mic and started singing “Danny Boy” in the middle of an Irish fairy festival. It would go either spectacularly well or terribly.

“I taught her that song,” the old woman said, tears streaming down her face. “I never in my life thought to hear her sing it to anyone but me.”

It soon became clear the girl was not butchering it; she was killing it in a high soprano. Tears pricked my eyes. The woman grasped my arm. “I never thought to meet an old one again.”

“I’m really not that old…”

“Do you have a quest for me?” There was deep hope in her eyes I couldn’t look at for more than a second. What had I started here?

I opened my mouth and again, totally unbidden, said, “Keep asking, and these last years will be rich.”

“Keep asking what?” she said with a light of devotion I could barely stand.

I also really didn’t like that I knew the answer. I pointed to her granddaughter. “Her. Keep asking her.”

She kissed my hand, and I heard the double chime. I backed away before I could do more and hid behind the riser trying to catch my breath. How did I know what to say? Where was I getting what they needed to hear? I clutched at the neck of my t-shirt. It wasn’t really a cloak, but the fabric felt like it was choking.

“You’re a hobgoblin,” a voice said from under the bleachers behind me.

I turned to see the pink ball of fluff I’d thought so old and powerful. She was dangling from one of the support struts. “What?”

“Why you can tell what they need. You can’t use their emotion without knowing what it is. Tis nothing to fear. Tis only magic.”

I wooshed out a breath. “Only magic, hunh?”

She grinned, flipped, and scampered off.

I shook my head. I was still taking from them. I glanced at the grandma, hugging her granddaughter as she hopped off the stage, glowing. She looked completely different, her posture changed, and her movements fluid. I could suddenly see her ten years in the future; she would be stunning. Was I just making that up or did I see it? That was a good deed at least, right?

“Orange!”

I gulped and spun and saw Hassan with his daughter on his shoulders.

“Oh no!” I jogged over. “I mean, hi! What are you doing here? I mean, how are you doing?”

“I know we do not let her play the game, but I thought a festival with real people would be safe, fun?”

“Hi, Orange,” Amina said, waving wildly as her black hair danced in two pig tails on either side of her head. Father and daughter were wearing jeans and neon green shirts.

“But if you are here…” Hassan said uncertainly.

“No, it’s fine. I had the same thought,” I said, trying to force normalcy into my voice.

“Everyone is Irish on St. Patrick’s Day! That is the saying, no?”

“Yeah.” I looked around, getting an inkling of why they started the festival on this day. In America, everyone is Irish on St. Patrick’s Day. That kind of statement matters in the world of magic. Could they draw even more from the people who used to worship them? It made a sickening kind of sense.

“Don’t play the game,” I said as he let Amina off his shoulders, and she scampered toward a table brimming with traditional wooden toys. “And don’t talk to the leprechauns.”

He laughed heartily, and I forced my face into a smile.

“And if you hear a tussle, run.”

The mirth fell off his face. “Orange, what is happening?”

“Nothing. Just the usual. The world is dangerous, right? Especially my world.” I looked around at the various cosplayers in Renaissance garb and wondered how many were human. “And this is my world. For all the little I can help.” And how much I could hurt. I didn’t say that part.

He gazed at the back of the Lincoln Memorial. From our view, we could just see columns on a huge concrete dais. Lincoln was hidden inside. “He said the work is what counts.”

I frowned. “Who said what?”

He pointed to the memorial. “I’m studying for the citizenship test. Lincoln features quite a lot.”

“That’s cool! Congratulations.”

He shoved his hands in the pockets of his jeans. “I have not passed it.”

I scoffed. “You totally will.”

“‘It is for us, the living, rather to be dedicated here to the unfinished work which they who fought here have thus far so nobly advanced.’”

“What does that mean?” I asked. He laughed loud and long, and I turtled into my leather jacket. “What?”

“It is true what they say, a citizen would not pass your own test.”

I rocked back on my heels. “There’s a big difference between a perfect union and a *more* perfect union, okay?”

“It is from the address at Gettysburg. It is written on that memorial. Mr. Lincoln was consecrating the graves of the battle.”

“He was?” I bit my lip, ashamed of my ignorance. “If you wanted to know the words to every Top-40 song released in the last decade, I could totally help.”

“He was saying that their work—the dead’s work—is not finished. That we still have that work to do. And it will not be finished in our time either. It is strange to live in a place with people who have forgotten that.”

“Or any folklore in about fifteen college classes. I’m your goblin.”

This time his laughter was so soft I could barely hear him.

“And I know patriotic stuff!” I insisted. “The National Anthem. Won’t make you listen to me sing. Um, the Pledge of Allegiance. Uh,” I searched my brain for how that started. We’d recited it daily through elementary school. “Oh yeah! I pledge allegiance! Duh.”

He put a hand on my shoulder, suddenly fierce, ignoring my chatter. “You are fighting still, are you not?”

I sobered and said, “Yes, I am still fighting.”

I may not have been able to quote Lincoln, but I was still fighting.

# 23

“What are ye doing?” a voice asked.

I glanced down to see a little man in a vest with a top hat, his face red with rage. He spoke with an Irish accent and looked really familiar. It took me a second to recognize the dude who stole the table on the tapes right in front of a camera. An actual leprechaun was tugging on my jeans. I looked at the sky. “I was joking about the leprechauns!”

Looking closely, I could see his clothes were a deep burgundy. I didn’t know why he was dressed in red, not green.

“What are ye doing?” he repeated.

My heart picked up. They obeyed Cool. Had they noticed the rest of the goblins? Did they understand what we were doing? Stealing the power?

“Thief,” he said with a hiss.

Yep, they did. “Am not! You are!”

“How are you stealing the magic?” he asked, tugging on my jeans until I grabbed for my waistband. “Even from the powerless!”

“They’re not powerless.” I laughed. “Everyone is Irish on St. Patrick’s Day.”

“That is a made-up thing.”

“We’re all made-up things,” I echoed, thinking of Mrs. Henderson’s metaphors.

“Give it back.” He yanked particularly hard.

“No, you give it back,” I retorted. “All of the treasure you stole. Where is it?”

He tried to push me over, and I stopped him with human strength. He toppled instead.

As he rolled to his feet, he said, “We will stop you.”

“I’d like to see you try.”

He bounded away, and a chime sounded.

I glared up at the sky for lack of anyone else to blame. “That was not a quest for him! I didn’t mean it.”

The leprechaun was heading for the river where I saw a couple of his compatriots dragging something toward the bank. They stumbled across a bike path and down a set of wide concrete steps set randomly in the riverbed behind the memorial.

The squeaking thing between them was the little blue goblin that had caused such havoc this morning. Without ceremony, they pitched him into the river.

I squeaked too and yanked on the little guy with magic. Only I hadn’t counted on the quests. I was quietly accumulating power just from the few I’d issued. When I yanked, I had planned just to get him back to dry land. Instead, he went sailing over all our heads like a squealing, blue meteor and landed somewhere out of sight on the other side of the Lincoln Memorial. Hopefully not on top of a bus full of tourists.

I looked around anxiously, ready to explain the really cool toy I’d brought when I noticed most of the crowd hadn’t looked up. Only select faces turned to the sky to watch his flight. The goblins and the leprechauns. There were more than I’d anticipated; two miniature armies wandering amongst the humans. If they hadn’t known before, the cat was out of the bag. Or was it the goblin out of the river?

As I watched, thinking we’d had a close escape, a green goblin bopped a leprechaun on the head. He retaliated by leaping for him. His friends joined in the fight and suddenly all the goblins were converging.

“I was kidding about the leprechauns!” I shouted as I ran for the two miniature warring armies. Goblins fought leprechauns in whirls of magic, dirt, water, and air. Nobody had lit anything on fire yet, but it was only a matter of time.

I ran toward one of the fights, trying to push away the crushing guilt that I’d brought them all here. Gently, with as little magic as possible, I separated the combatants. A little red goblin I didn’t recognize and a leprechaun whose coat matched his opponent’s skin kept trying to get at each other.

“Get out of here!” I shouted at the goblin. I didn’t even know his name. “Abandon ship!”

I ran for the next one even as I saw another goblin go into the river. I fished him out less dramatically and pried a leprechaun off Mrs. Henderson’s back even as she snarled that she was fine. It was a surprisingly even fight, but even with all our quests, I could tell the goblins were not winning. Worse, we didn’t have enough quests done.

“Stop! Let them go!”

Everyone ignored me. What else was new?

I was about to wade into another fracas when I heard, “Orange!”

I pivoted, all the air in my body leaving me in a whoosh. Scharf strode across the lawn, her eggplant sedan parked just outside the banner on the grass. She was dressed in jeans and a sweatshirt that said Quantico in stamped letters, so I wasn’t sure if she was on duty or not. But something had sent her straight to the last possible place she should be.

“How did you find me?” I winced. “Hassan called, didn’t he?” I said, answering my own question.

She scanned the area quickly. “Bishara is here?”

“Nope. Haven’t seen him.”

I bopped a leprechaun on the head and sent another goblin flying. Mrs. Henderson picked herself up off the ground by my feet. I hadn’t seen what put her there.

“Mrs. Henderson?” Scharf asked.

“I like bagpipes,” Mrs. Henderson said mechanically, pointing to the dude taking the stage. A moan wheezed out of the instrument, sounding like a dying animal.

I pivoted to Mrs. Henderson. “Get everybody out. Unless they can fight leprechauns, tell everybody to run.”

As I whispered that, I saw an orange line leading straight to Scharf’s sedan via Scharf’s pocket. It would be the fastest way out for me. I told it no thank you, and it disappeared in a huff. I didn’t think my magic had actual emotions or thoughts, but sometimes I wondered.

I turned, searching for more leprechauns. As I did, Scharf yanked on the shoulder of my jacket, so I had to reel and face her. “What the hell is going on? The truth. Now.”

I squirmed and pushed and finally loosed her hold. “No.”

I ran for another little goblin I saw in the clutches of a leprechaun who was using his bowler hat to pummel the creature. I freed him and told him to run.

“Who was that?” Scharf was right on my heels.

“Nobody.” I dodged around a dueling pair of humans, noses glued to their screens as the bagpipe finished the god-awful racket that was him tuning the thing and started into a quick reel that blasted out across the National Mall. I pivoted in a circle, not seeing any more skirmishes.

Even as I was glad they’d heeded my advice, I was worried that we hadn’t done enough quests to defeat the game itself. I hadn’t seen Cool yet, but he had to be coming. And Scharf could not be here when he got here.

I rounded on her. “Please go home.”

“The art is here. I know it is. And so do you. What about our bargain? What happened to aiding the FBI?”

“I am helping you!” I said and startled a dude dressed like Legolas approaching me.

“Cool ears!” he said, and I growled in frustration. He did a quick 180.

“Why aren’t you hiding your ears?” Scharf demanded.

I checked one more time and didn’t see any goblins getting their butts kicked. I would have to worry about the quests and the art later. Scharf could not be here.

I yanked her away by the sleeve of her sweatshirt toward the Lincoln Memorial. There was a tourist taking artful pictures of the ceiling of the monument with a huge camera, but other than that, we were alone away from the fair.

“How did you find me?” I asked, trying to figure out how much she knew.

Scharf crossed her arms. “We processed the crime scene at the fort.” She leaned against the side of the monument. “Imagine my surprise when I saw my own boot print inside an artillery storage where I have never been. And when I saw your own shoe print.”

“You know my shoe print?” I looked down at the beat-up athletic sneakers I hadn’t burned or drowned or ripped to pieces yet.

“When your roommate can go invisible, you learn to check the garden.”

“Right.”

“And the dirt at the fort matched the crime scene at the museum.”

“Of course.”

“And then there’s this.” She held up an evidence bag with a spoon in it, an ornate, clearly silver spoon with scrollwork all up the side. “Also in an artillery bunker.”

I crossed my arms, hunching into myself, still scanning the field for leprechauns. And Cool. “Am I under arrest?”

“Not yet.”

“Not yet?”

“I can’t explain any of that, Orange.”

I threw my hands in the air. “Well, aren’t you on a task force for that? To explain the unexplained?”

“I can’t remember a day of my life!” she shouted. She never shouted. My jaw dropped. Even faced with a flaming djinni or a crazy dragon, she had not lost her self-possession. I took a step back, gathering magic, even as my heart cracked. Was she okay or was she still suffering the effects of magical amnesia? I’d never forgive myself if this was permanent.

She took a deep breath and said in a strained voice, “But you are not surprised by this little story. Why not?” She was not under control; she was still pale as a ghost with her jaw clenched and every muscle in her body tense. I had never seen her so furious.

I licked my lips. “I didn’t steal the treasure.”

“You didn’t steal the treasure from the museum, or you didn’t steal it from the fort?” She put air quotes around treasure.

“Neither,” I said, meeting her gaze evenly.

“Who did?”

I winced.

“Orange now is not the time for your little games. Tell me what happened.”
